# Structural shifts

2079 claims from 220 sources. Each line links to the claim inside its source note.


## [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets]]
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C02-2|MT001-C02-2]] — Demographics is the clearest long trend: China's population is certain to shrink sharply over a century and India has overtaken it as most populous, though the investment implication is separate from the trend itself.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C02-3|MT001-C02-3]] — US long-term rates fell from the early 1980s to about 1% in COVID, a long tailwind for both bonds and stocks that he believes has ended, so the macro regime is now different.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C03-2|MT001-C03-2]] — Country-level deregulation and rule of law signal rising corporate profits and therefore good stock returns ahead (China, then India).
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C03-3|MT001-C03-3]] — India currently has a green light from deregulation, digitization and cell-phone access, with the highest GDP growth in the world this year, so he would take the opportunity.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C03-7|MT001-C03-7]] — Because lending has moved from banks to non-bank lenders, monetary data series are not comparable with those of 40 years ago, so old relationships need care.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C04-3|MT001-C04-3]] — Technology explains much of market movement but is the hardest pattern to turn into a signal.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C04-4|MT001-C04-4]] — Railroad stocks peaking before the transcontinental line was finished does not make it a simple bubble, because a full network (market structure) was what created value; judge tech cycles by structure, not price run-up.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C04-5|MT001-C04-5]] — Disruptive technologies differ: electricity utilities and refineries saw capacity match demand with stable buildouts, so not every rally in a new technology is a bubble.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C04-6|MT001-C04-6]] — Nvidia's ecosystem could give it an economic moat and survivor status in 10 years, though AI will still have booms and busts.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C05-3|MT001-C05-3]] — Linked or pegged currencies can break suddenly (early 1990s European currencies), and he missed it.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C06-9|MT001-C06-9]] — Bitcoin is fundamentally like gold and the other precious metals (platinum, palladium, silver) but at an earlier maturity stage with limited institutional adoption; they do not move in lockstep.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C07-3|MT001-C07-3]] — Bitcoin will survive while most other crypto assets will not.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C07-4|MT001-C07-4]] — Emerging markets are too different politically and economically to treat as one bloc, and are better viewed as part of international equity exposure.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C07-5|MT001-C07-5]] — The US, Europe, China and India are four big consumer markets, so firms can scale within each and growth will be pulled to them.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C07-6|MT001-C07-6]] — Countries differ in policy, demographics and technology exposure, so international diversification is attractive now and may not cost return, with possibly higher returns outside the US.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C07-7|MT001-C07-7]] — India offers diversification because the rupee is not dollar-linked and its economic cycle differs from the US.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C08-2|MT001-C08-2]] — For second-order beneficiaries of themes like AI, ask whether the company has a moat that keeps profits flowing three years into the trend.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C08-4|MT001-C08-4]] — Copper and other metals demand with supply constraints (mines cannot start quickly) should give visible profitability several years out.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C10-2|MT001-C10-2]] — India is underappreciated and underweight in international equity indices.
- [[MT001 - 133 - Jan van Eck- Macro Forces Shape Markets#^MT001-C10-3|MT001-C10-3]] — The expected spin-out of Jio from Reliance would clarify India's tech pillars and give a clearer market structure.

## [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327]]
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C02-3|MT002-C02-3]] — The CTA correlation (co-movement) style factor was negative in 2024, meaning trends in less correlated markets did better.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C04-4b|MT002-C04-4b]] — Structured-product issuance is a secular trend, so index vol supply and low correlation should persist into 2025, while the liquidity driver is less reliable.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C04-5|MT002-C04-5]] — Whether Trump's China stance turns less hawkish (and US-Russia stance softens) is the key geopolitical question, because US-China alignment would change global conditions.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C06-5b|MT002-C06-5b]] — Active ETF volumes, including managed-futures ETFs, have grown by orders of magnitude.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C07-4|MT002-C07-4]] — Once trend beta is available cheaply via replication or ETFs, investors can spend fee budget on managers trading esoteric, less liquid markets for alpha.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C08-6|MT002-C08-6]] — Dominant markets are more correlated with each other than alternative assets, so alternative markets bring diversification during a financial decoupling.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C09-1|MT002-C09-1]] — Young markets trend more, and as they mature with more participants they become noisier and more mean-reverting, an argument attributed to Perry Kaufman.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C09-4|MT002-C09-4]] — Cliff Asness argues equity markets have become less efficient because high-valued stocks trade at a premium over cheap stocks for longer than historically expected, as in 2000 and again recently.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C10-1|MT002-C10-1]] — Less efficient markets mean longer drawdowns for quant value strategies but eventually larger mispricing opportunities (Asness's drivers: indexing, low rates, social media).
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C10-3|MT002-C10-3]] — Long-term studies of value versus growth miss structural change: today's leading firms allocate capital better than stock pickers, so old valuation relationships may not hold.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C11-1|MT002-C11-1]] — Stock selection has become more efficient because easy targets (badly run companies, long-term bad and cheap) are rarer; activist targets are now good companies.
- [[MT002 - 2024 Trend Following Recap- Key Insights from Top Traders - Systematic Investor 327#^MT002-C14-2|MT002-C14-2]] — Trend-following managers' 2024 returns ranged from about +70% to -5% and SG Trend Index is dominated by the largest managers and not representative of the broad community.

## [[MT003 - 40 Years of Trading Lessons with Patrick Welton - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.153]]
- [[MT003 - 40 Years of Trading Lessons with Patrick Welton - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.153#^MT003-C08-2|MT003-C08-2]] — A reduced set of markets can replicate the trend following industry's returns, and popular trend ETFs would not have changed much if they had kept their market sets.
- [[MT003 - 40 Years of Trading Lessons with Patrick Welton - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.153#^MT003-C11-5|MT003-C11-5]] — Of roughly $100 trillion of allocatable capital, investors are under-allocated to alternatives rather than over, which is bullish for multistrategy and managed futures demand.
- [[MT003 - 40 Years of Trading Lessons with Patrick Welton - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.153#^MT003-C14-5|MT003-C14-5]] — Regimes are best viewed as macro biases, rates rising or falling and inflation up or down; they help but also mislead early quants.
- [[MT003 - 40 Years of Trading Lessons with Patrick Welton - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.153#^MT003-C15-2|MT003-C15-2]] — Trading approach should differ between high-inflation, rising-rate, supply-constrained regimes and demand-constrained, low-rate, low-inflation regimes; regime shifting is an imperfect setup, not a precise prediction.

## [[MT004 - 5% Rates, Bessent's Failed Gamble & What Comes Next w- Krishna Guha - The Real Eisman Play]]
- [[MT004 - 5% Rates, Bessent's Failed Gamble & What Comes Next w- Krishna Guha - The Real Eisman Play#^MT004-C02-1|MT004-C02-1]] — A Fed chair who frames the economy as driven by supply (productivity, labor incentives) rather than demand will rely on different models and may run policy differently from the new-Keynesian consensus of the past 20 years.
- [[MT004 - 5% Rates, Bessent's Failed Gamble & What Comes Next w- Krishna Guha - The Real Eisman Play#^MT004-C04-2|MT004-C04-2]] — A multi-year AI investment boom raises the neutral rate (r-star) because investment demand now exceeds savings, so the normal Fed funds rate over the next several years should be higher.
- [[MT004 - 5% Rates, Bessent's Failed Gamble & What Comes Next w- Krishna Guha - The Real Eisman Play#^MT004-C08-4|MT004-C08-4]] — AI investment and wealth effects make the US economy in aggregate less sensitive to higher rates than before the AI boom, which makes it hard to pinpoint the yield level where growth slows.
- [[MT004 - 5% Rates, Bessent's Failed Gamble & What Comes Next w- Krishna Guha - The Real Eisman Play#^MT004-C09-4|MT004-C09-4]] — AI capex leaks abroad through imported chips while consumption funded by stock wealth is spent at home, so a stock sell-off hits domestic demand more than AI investment suggests.
- [[MT004 - 5% Rates, Bessent's Failed Gamble & What Comes Next w- Krishna Guha - The Real Eisman Play#^MT004-C10-1|MT004-C10-1]] — Greenspan's belief that markets are self-equilibrating helped produce weak financial stability regulation that was paid for in the financial crisis, even though his macro management was rated very good.
- [[MT004 - 5% Rates, Bessent's Failed Gamble & What Comes Next w- Krishna Guha - The Real Eisman Play#^MT004-C11-4|MT004-C11-4]] — A tariff trade war produces an initial wave of inflation through higher import prices.

## [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money]]
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C01-2|MT005-C01-2]] — Government spending on infrastructure, reshoring and green energy raises metal demand while supply is constrained, creating a supply-demand imbalance that supports metal prices.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C01-3|MT005-C01-3]] — Owning exploration-stage miners through an activist, drill-focused approach can outperform the underlying metal price in a metals bull market because discoveries add leveraged value.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C01-6|MT005-C01-6]] — The firm's portfolio companies expect about 114 drills turning over the next 12 months, more greenfield drilling than the large gold majors combined, which is the basis for expecting discoveries.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C01-7|MT005-C01-7]] — Majors have underinvested in exploration and left it to juniors, so they must buy juniors to grow production, which should drive a mining M&A cycle that benefits junior shareholders.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C02-4|MT005-C02-4]] — Imperial Rome lasted five centuries on gold and silver coinage used for trade and army pay, cited as evidence that hard-money systems support durable empires.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C02-5|MT005-C02-5]] — Song Dynasty paper money worked because notes could be exchanged for metal coins, so metal backing is what made the paper currency credible.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C03-1|MT005-C03-1]] — Spanish American mines produced over 150,000 tons of silver in the 16th to 18th centuries, over 80% of world supply, and the single Potosi ore body gave 37% of world silver from 1545 to 1810, so a monetary-metal supply windfall made Spain the richest power.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C04-1|MT005-C04-1]] — William Boyce Thompson turned Newmont from a trading company into the largest gold miner by building a real operating business staffed with the best geologists, engineers and advisers, so team quality is a driver of mining success.
- [[MT005 - A Brief World Economic History For Gold, Silver, and Fiat Money#^MT005-C05-4|MT005-C05-4]] — After Bre-X, Canada's NI 43-101 imposed qualified-person sign-off, standardized resource and reserve reporting, independent technical reports and fuller disclosure, which is why Canadian exchanges host most of the world's mining companies and why disclosure risk in Canadian-listed miners is lower.

## [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317]]
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C07-2|MT006-C07-2]] — Trend following earns its profit from rare, massive outlier moves in the tails, while noise dominates most of the return distribution.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C08-1|MT006-C08-1]] — All liquid markets show fat-tailed (leptokurtic) daily returns, with mean-reverting noise in the bulk and trending behaviour in the tails.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C09-2|MT006-C09-2]] — During outlier moves correlations between markets break down, so uncorrelated systems riding the outlier improve results.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C11-1|MT006-C11-1]] — Markets behave like complex adaptive systems with fractal, scale-invariant structure that grow from small seeds into large trends, so no part is independent of the others.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C12-3|MT006-C12-3]] — Boundaries make the price path constrained and path-dependent rather than random.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C13-1|MT006-C13-1]] — Stochastic (ergodic) models of markets lack path dependence, whereas real markets show path dependence.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C14-2|MT006-C14-2]] — Outliers are natural and occur more often than normal-distribution models imply.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C15-1|MT006-C15-1]] — Nonlinear deterministic dynamics limit prediction, which supports reacting rather than predicting.
- [[MT006 - A Challenging October for Trend Following - Systematic Investor 317#^MT006-C15-2|MT006-C15-2]] — Complex systems are computationally irreducible, hence inherently unpredictable for any embedded observer.

## [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332]]
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C04-2|MT007-C04-2]] — The stock-bond correlation turns positive when inflation is high, so inflation regime determines whether bonds hedge equities.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C04-3|MT007-C04-3]] — Tariffs and changing trade flows (less US trade, more intra-Asia) should break down cross-country correlations, ending the globalisation-driven connectedness.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C08-1|MT007-C08-1]] — Cutting immigration shrinks labour supply, lowering payroll growth and potential growth while pushing up wages, which is stagflationary.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C08-2|MT007-C08-2]] — Long-term growth equals population growth plus productivity growth, so a declining working-age population needs productivity gains.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C08-3|MT007-C08-3]] — New institutional economics: growth depends on institutions, including labour force composition, not just worker counts.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C10-3|MT007-C10-3]] — Regime breaks (end of Bretton Woods 1971, rate spikes in 1980, 1992 EMS failure, euro) were not anticipated, so be aware anything is possible.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C11-1|MT007-C11-1]] — After each regime break markets and the world survived.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C12-2|MT007-C12-2]] — Convergent strategies assume a known equilibrium; in a regime change the equilibrium (e.g. term premium, inflation target) is uncertain, so mean reversion fails.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C15-1|MT007-C15-1]] — Complex systems have nonlinear shock responses and contagion, so ask whether models handle nonlinearity and what happens if correlations go to one.
- [[MT007 - A Financial Regime Shift Is Coming - Systematic Investor 332#^MT007-C16-3|MT007-C16-3]] — Less regulation (fewer Federal Register pages) helps small caps because compliance has a higher fixed cost for small firms (Laffer and Canto).

## [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388]]
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C02-5|MT008-C02-5]] — Commodity bubbles differ from equity bubbles because mining supply is constrained, so demand shocks translate into price spikes.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C05-1|MT008-C05-1]] — EM inflation is below US core inflation (about 3%), making EM more attractive and driving rotation toward EM, along with higher EM real yields.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C05-2|MT008-C05-2]] — A K-shaped US economy, where real economy and asset economy diverge, shapes asset flows worldwide.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C06-1|MT008-C06-1]] — Safety is relative: treasuries remain safe but less so, so investors diversify more of their flows.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C06-2|MT008-C06-2]] — Bonds are a less reliable but not a failed equity diversifier; in crises with aggressive Fed cuts they still diversify.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C06-3|MT008-C06-3]] — Awareness of regimes, in which relationships change, gives an edge in portfolio formation.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C07-1|MT008-C07-1]] — Asset price relationships are conditional on environment, e.g. stock beta is not a single number.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C07-2|MT008-C07-2]] — A stock's beta differs across crisis/non-crisis and recession/non-recession regimes.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C07-3|MT008-C07-3]] — The dollar-rate differential relationship weakened after Liberation Day (a credibility discount), and gold has risen independent of real yields and the dollar.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C08-1|MT008-C08-1]] — Strategy behaviour differs by volatility regime, which can be identified with hidden Markov models.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C08-4|MT008-C08-4]] — Stock-bond correlation is inflation-dependent, so third factors indicate the regime.
- [[MT008 - AI Bubble Peak- - Systematic Investor - Ep.388#^MT008-C11-X1|MT008-C11-X1]] — If the downward payroll revisions reflect lower labour supply (people leaving the US), weak job creation need not raise unemployment, so the weakness matters less for the economy.

## [[MT009 - AI Can Build Your Portfolio—But Can You Trust It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.419]]
- [[MT009 - AI Can Build Your Portfolio—But Can You Trust It- - Systematic Investor - Ep.419#^MT009-C16-1|MT009-C16-1]] — The binding constraint in asset management is human decision bandwidth, which agentic AI relaxes.

## [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399]]
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C01-1|MT011-C01-1]] — AI-related capex is distorting US growth data so aggregate GDP overstates breadth of the economy, complicating policy reads.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C01-2|MT011-C01-2]] — Index earnings growth is heavily concentrated in the Magnificent 7, so headline earnings overstate broad profit growth.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C01-3|MT011-C01-3]] — Labor share of output fell to a record low 54%, consistent with AI/capital-intensive growth shifting income from wages to capital.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C01-4|MT011-C01-4]] — Data-center construction is geographically concentrated, so an AI capex unwind would hit construction employment less broadly than headline spending implies.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C01-X1|MT011-C01-X1]] — Much AI capex leaks abroad as imports (e.g. chips from Korea), so its net contribution to US GDP growth is smaller than the gross spending figures imply.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C02-2|MT011-C02-2]] — Geopolitical conflict exposes AI infrastructure concentration in unstable regions, adding a risk channel for AI-linked assets.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C03-2|MT011-C03-2]] — Restricting immigration cuts labor-force growth and creates labor shortages that push up inflation, so social and economic policy goals conflict.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C05-7|MT011-C05-7]] — An oil shock has asymmetric geographic effects; the US (net energy exporter) is more insulated than Europe or Asia, so shocks drive FX and relative equity moves.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C05-8|MT011-C05-8]] — Energy price spikes hit lower-income households hardest because fuel is a larger budget share, weighing on consumption and building pressure for subsidies.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C06-1|MT011-C06-1]] — AI is a positive supply shock, raising output at lower cost, so in theory disinflationary and a case for rate cuts.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C06-4|MT011-C06-4]] — Hyperscaler AI capex is approaching $1 trillion a year and rising, a very real demand impulse.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C07-2|MT011-C07-2]] — China becoming the world's production hub in the 2000s was a bigger disinflationary force than AI probably is.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C07-3|MT011-C07-3]] — AI is energy-intensive, so during an energy supply shock its disinflationary efficiency gains may be offset by higher energy demand.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C07-4|MT011-C07-4]] — A downturn from higher energy prices could accelerate AI adoption and job losses as firms cut costs.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C15-2|MT011-C15-2]] — Vanilla multi-asset funds are long-only, short inflation risk, and lack tactical strategies, so cannot respond to correlation or structural shifts.
- [[MT011 - AI, Inflation, and the Portfolio That Refuses to Sit Still - Systematic Investor - Ep.399#^MT011-C18-2|MT011-C18-2]] — In a flow-driven, deglobalising world non-predictive trend strategies are well suited, though returns may not be massive.

## [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge]]
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C02-2|MT012-C02-2]] — The UAE-US 5 GW AI campus equals about 2.5 million GPUs of compute, a measure of AI capex scale.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C02-3|MT012-C02-3]] — Restricting US AI exports pushes allies toward Chinese full-stack technology, reducing long-run US tech dominance.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C03-1|MT012-C03-1]] — Cheap energy (solar, nuclear, gas) gives Gulf states a cost advantage in producing AI tokens, since AI is effectively converting electrons into tokens.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C03-2|MT012-C03-2]] — Power and cooling are only about 15-20% of COGS, so a 2x power advantage is worth only about five points of margin, a smaller edge than often assumed.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C03-3|MT012-C03-3]] — Latency and proximity create demand for local data centers in Europe, the Middle East and India beyond power cost.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C04-1|MT012-C04-1]] — Export restrictions would repeat the internet pattern in reverse, with US AI walled off and Chinese AI serving the rest of the world.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C04-2|MT012-C04-2]] — A WSJ article shows Europe lags in business culture and tech because complex regulation stifles innovation on labor and technology; US policies under consideration mirrored these.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C04-X1|MT012-C04-X1]] — Because the US captured most of the internet boom's value by letting its firms sell globally, an open AI export policy should let it capture an even larger AI boom.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C05-1|MT012-C05-1]] — US benefits economically and strategically when the world's AI runs on American technology; thus diffusion favours Nvidia, AMD and OpenAI.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C08-3|MT012-C08-3]] — China already has frontier AI, so restrictions cannot keep it from China.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C08-4|MT012-C08-4]] — Selling chips to China keeps Chinese firms in the CUDA ecosystem and US profit/tax base rather than the Huawei ecosystem.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C08-5|MT012-C08-5]] — Technology restrictions accelerate Chinese indigenous development, as seen in autos and robotics.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C12-3|MT012-C12-3]] — Invest America seeds each newborn with $1,000 in an S&P 500 account that compounds to about $50,000 by 18 and $150,000 by 30 with contributions.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C13-1|MT012-C13-1]] — After the Tesla pay ruling companies considered leaving Delaware; claim of mass reincorporation.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C14-1|MT012-C14-1]] — Grundfest research (2009-2024) shows Delaware fee multiples of seven times or higher are 23 times and ten times or higher 57 times more frequent than in federal court, indicating governance risk.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C14-2|MT012-C14-2]] — 55% of the highest-multiplier awards come from two judges and the chancellor assigns cases, creating concentrated unpredictable outcomes.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C15-1|MT012-C15-1]] — In the Tesla case a plaintiff with nine shares generated a roughly $345-380M lawyer fee, showing misaligned litigation incentives.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C16-2|MT012-C16-2]] — SEC commissioner Hester Peirce argues blockchains could better track securities and regulatory capture produces market fragility; she proposes a sandbox.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C16-3|MT012-C16-3]] — The number of US public companies is far below its peak, linked to regulation and IPO inefficiency.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C17-3|MT012-C17-3]] — Stablecoin payment rails cut transaction friction, raising growth and reducing the tax burden on the economy.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C17-X1|MT012-C17-X1]] — With stablecoin regulation in place, tokenization will extend from IPOs to listed stocks and possibly real assets.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C18-1|MT012-C18-1]] — Baidu joins DeepSeek, Qwen and another in Chinese open-source models, creating competitive dynamic and rapid innovation.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C18-2|MT012-C18-2]] — Open models can be used to improve other models, making open-source competition hard for closed labs to match.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C18-X1|MT012-C18-X1]] — Crypto's 2019-2020 promises stalled because of hostile, capricious US regulation; a clear framework removes that brake, and internet money transfer has barely changed in 25-30 years.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C19-1|MT012-C19-1]] — Proprietary labs will train on available open models, so bans on DeepSeek are whack-a-mole.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C19-2|MT012-C19-2]] — About half of US AI researchers are of Chinese background, so anti-China rhetoric risks hurting talent supply.
- [[MT012 - AI, Middle East, China, Tariffs, Recon Bill, Invest America - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT012-C20-1|MT012-C20-1]] — Skilled immigration from China, such as an AI visa, would be the best US competitive move.

## [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387]]
- [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387#^MT013-C02-1|MT013-C02-1]] — AI-driven economic disruption should be positive for trend following because trend strategies need real change in the economy to profit.
- [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387#^MT013-C02-3|MT013-C02-3]] — AI asset-allocation analyses recommend managed futures as a core element, suggesting spreading knowledge will increase allocations to trend.
- [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387#^MT013-C04-2|MT013-C04-2]] — Hedge funds improved managers, structures and processes under competition and mark-to-market discipline while private equity merely grew bigger.
- [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387#^MT013-C05-X1|MT013-C05-X1]] — Allocators are heavily exposed through private equity to software companies that AI could disrupt, adding to the case against long-term leveraged long equity exposure.
- [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387#^MT013-C14-3|MT013-C14-3]] — Hedge fund industry splits into scalable strategies forming part of tactical asset allocation (like CTAs, with falling costs) and pure alpha that goes in and out of vogue.
- [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387#^MT013-C15-1|MT013-C15-1]] — Multi-manager mutual fund and ETF products failed as a business model.
- [[MT013 - AI, Risk, and the Future of Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.387#^MT013-C18-2|MT013-C18-2]] — Low-cost ETF model portfolios create a market for beta-like alternative indices at low fees.

## [[MT014 - Adapt or Perish- Why Systematic Investors Must Evolve in 2024 - Systematic Investor 325]]
- [[MT014 - Adapt or Perish- Why Systematic Investors Must Evolve in 2024 - Systematic Investor 325#^MT014-C01-2|MT014-C01-2]] — US assets (dollar, equities, bonds) drove most of trend returns in late 2024.
- [[MT014 - Adapt or Perish- Why Systematic Investors Must Evolve in 2024 - Systematic Investor 325#^MT014-C02-2|MT014-C02-2]] — US equities strongly outperform other markets (even Europe, France, Korea), so diversification outside the US did not pay in 2024 and unpredictability requires adaptive strategies.
- [[MT014 - Adapt or Perish- Why Systematic Investors Must Evolve in 2024 - Systematic Investor 325#^MT014-C03-1|MT014-C03-1]] — The MSCI World index is now over half US equities, whereas a classically diversified equity portfolio would be about a third, so index investors are concentrated.
- [[MT014 - Adapt or Perish- Why Systematic Investors Must Evolve in 2024 - Systematic Investor 325#^MT014-C13-4|MT014-C13-4]] — CTA industry assets have been flat at about $350-400 billion for years despite education efforts.

## [[MT015 - Ajay Banga - President of World Bank - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment]]
- [[MT015 - Ajay Banga - President of World Bank - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT015-C01-2|MT015-C01-2]] — The World Bank has broadened its mandate from poverty to a 'livable planet' covering climate, pandemics, fragility and food security, steering development lending toward these themes.
- [[MT015 - Ajay Banga - President of World Bank - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT015-C02-3|MT015-C02-3]] — BRICS-type institutions are complements rather than rivals because development needs are too large; he wants more cooperation (e.g. with IDB and AfDB) but not fragmentation, since scale matters.
- [[MT015 - Ajay Banga - President of World Bank - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT015-C04-1|MT015-C04-1]] — AI democratises knowledge as Google did, but poorer countries need infrastructure to convert it into tangible benefits, and guide rails are needed so technology unchecked does not cause harm.
- [[MT015 - Ajay Banga - President of World Bank - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT015-C04-2|MT015-C04-2]] — Less cash and digital ID bring the poor into the formal economy, giving access to credit, insurance and services, which supports financial inclusion and growth.
- [[MT015 - Ajay Banga - President of World Bank - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT015-C08-3|MT015-C08-3]] — Global practices must be tailored to local country conditions; one-size-fits-all fails but replicable, scalable models adapted locally give speed.

## [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407]]
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C02-7|MT016-C02-7]] — The economy is K-shaped: AI infrastructure capex drives growth but is not labor-heavy, so jobs numbers soften while the capex-linked market stays strong.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C02-8|MT016-C02-8]] — Index earnings growth comes entirely from the AI capex build, a reflexive loop where the market itself, not the broad economy, drives outcomes.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C04-3|MT016-C04-3]] — The US is moving from neutral referee of global capitalism to a more interventionist state using markets, the dollar, technology and policy as instruments of power (a single regime shift).
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C04-4|MT016-C04-4]] — The US is adopting state-directed capitalism (government stakes, picking winners), analogous to China's capitalism with Chinese characteristics, because great-power competition forces it.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C05-1|MT016-C05-1]] — Populism is accelerating as baby boomers die (examples: billionaire tax, proposals for 50% state ownership of AI firms) and becomes accepted on both sides.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C05-2|MT016-C05-2]] — Structural inflation persists and accelerates due to global conflict, protectionism, fiscal spending and populism.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C05-4|MT016-C05-4]] — The US-China fight over who holds the primary reserve currency and sets the rules is the fifth pressure pushing for state action.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C05-6|MT016-C05-6]] — Precedent: Japan's central bank owns 8-9% of its equity market and Norway runs a ~$3T sovereign wealth fund, so state equity ownership via money creation has been done.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C06-X2|MT016-C06-X2]] — Precedent: China has for a decade printed money and channelled it as direct state investment into strategic companies, the model the US is now adapting through a market-based fund.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C07-2|MT016-C07-2]] — The plan is not fair globally and the main risk is China (and the rest of the world) pushing back.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C07-X1|MT016-C07-X1]] — Disagreement: the US lacks Norway's social-welfare mindset, and state stake purchases hand cash to selling billionaires, so the plan may not reduce inequality.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C07-X2|MT016-C07-X2]] — Government equity stakes come with growing state say over company decisions (e.g. hiring American), so stake-held firms will partly serve national goals, not only shareholders.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C07-X3|MT016-C07-X3]] — The state-ownership and monetisation plan is a 10-20 year programme that only incrementally eases the pressures, not a one-off fix.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C08-3|MT016-C08-3]] — US actions in Iran, Venezuela, Panama and the Philippines aim to build a moat around dollar-denominated trade (commodities priced in dollars) against Chinese yuan trade.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C08-5|MT016-C08-5]] — The US will not accept Iranian or Chinese control or taxation of Hormuz traffic in other currencies, so conflict continues beyond the nuclear justification.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C08-X1|MT016-C08-X1]] — As part of the trade bargain the US is likely to let China buy more passive stakes in US businesses, with the line drawn at any influence over them.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C10-1|MT016-C10-1]] — The AI capex cycle differs from the dot-com bubble because the US government implicitly guarantees it to compete with China.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C10-3|MT016-C10-3]] — The US waited 10-20 years to act, so the plan has less control and more risk, with China likely to poke at it (gray swans).
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C11-X1|MT016-C11-X1]] — Challenge to the China-model analogy: China's state-directed system produced a property bust with big investor losses, and the US cannot control the narrative as China does, so the model may not work.
- [[MT016 - America's 250-Year Reset feat. Cem Karsan - Systematic Investor - Ep. 407#^MT016-C11-X2|MT016-C11-X2]] — The US approach, though barely started, is already working by forcing dollar strength and more control, leaning on incumbent advantages and a more open, more popular system, so success is more likely than not.

## [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402]]
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C01-1|MT017-C01-1]] — Passive vehicles hold about 60% of US equity fund assets, and price-insensitive ownership including benchmark-hugging institutions is meaningfully higher.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C02-1|MT017-C02-1]] — The rise of passive via ETFs extends to fixed income, commodities, currency and multi-asset products, so the structural argument applies across asset classes.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C02-3|MT017-C02-3]] — The mix of participants (the agent ecology: value, passive, momentum, vol-targeting, dealers) determines how a market behaves, so who is at the table matters.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C03-1|MT017-C03-1]] — The key question about a market is which agents populate it and how they interact, not fair value, macro backdrop or central bank action; fundamentals are loosely coupled autonomous systems.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C03-3|MT017-C03-3]] — Passive allocates by index rule regardless of price, so it does not push back on stretched valuations or step in when valuations crash.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C04-1|MT017-C04-1]] — Index funds are rational for individuals but the aggregate effect of price-insensitive capital changes market structure; the argument is structural, not about whether to own index funds.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C04-2|MT017-C04-2]] — The share of price-sensitive agents has shrunk because passive displaced them, even though active capital remains large in absolute terms.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C04-4|MT017-C04-4]] — Passive reduces the share of capital doing balancing work while forced index-inclusion and rebalancing flows have grown.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C05-1|MT017-C05-1]] — Divergent traders (trend, momentum) and passive long-only are on the reinforcing side; convergent traders (value, mean reversion, counter-trend) provide balancing, and that group has been diluted.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C05-4|MT017-C05-4]] — The key structural change of two decades is the shift from price-sensitive to price-insensitive ownership, more than HFT, algos or mega-cap dominance, which are downstream effects.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C07-1|MT017-C07-1]] — Demography (retirees drawing down passive holdings) is the force that may eventually reverse passive growth, probably affecting the next generation.
- [[MT017 - Are Markets Becoming Living Systems- - Systematic Investor - Ep. 402#^MT017-C18-2|MT017-C18-2]] — Because passive dilutes balancing agents while leaving reinforcing ones intact, the flow is thinner and the multiplier higher, so the same fingerprint appears with more violence per unit of flow.

## [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame]]
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C01-1|MT018-C01-1]] — Huawei's Mate 70 reportedly runs a non-Android OS on a chip made at SMIC, not TSMC, suggesting China has achieved high-end chip manufacturing despite export controls.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C01-3|MT018-C01-3]] — Citing Elon Musk, China's economy may become two to three times the size of the US, and since war's foundation is economic, the US is like a long-time biggest kid facing a much bigger one.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C02-1|MT018-C02-1]] — China produces a much larger share of STEM graduates (35% vs below 10% in the US), and with work ethic gives it the pieces to run fast.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C02-2|MT018-C02-2]] — Tariffs and technology blocks (ASML, AI) protect US industry only briefly and remove the competition US firms need.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C02-3|MT018-C02-3]] — The US should focus on running its own race as fast as possible, without naivety about China's intentions, but full decoupling from the second largest economy is unwise.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C05-2|MT018-C05-2]] — Evidence for price-driven behavior: SUV sales collapsed when gasoline spiked in 2009, which is why Europe has small cars, so taxing fuel moves consumers to electric.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C05-3|MT018-C05-3]] — Brazil's government-run instant payment system Pix reached about 43% of all payments within a few years, mirroring UK Faster Payments, India UPI and others, while the US lags (ACH takes days).
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C05-4|MT018-C05-4]] — Visa and MasterCard's operating margins above 60% show an oligopoly the US fails to challenge, and instant public rails threaten those margins.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C06-1|MT018-C06-1]] — A government-run instant payment rail enables fintech and crypto innovation on top, and single-provider infrastructure is natural, whereas private provision leads to excessive margins.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C07-3|MT018-C07-3]] — Anthropic open-sourced MCP, a data connection protocol, as a strategic competitive tool (like Meta's Open Compute Project and Google's Kubernetes) to avoid proprietary connectors from rivals.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C07-4|MT018-C07-4]] — Open source has become a strategic weapon for large technology firms that removes IP control from competitors.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C08-1|MT018-C08-1]] — Open-sourcing a standard is a savvy competitive tactic against a rival that might create proprietary connectors, and should boost AI innovation and competition.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C08-4|MT018-C08-4]] — The US may be the first nation where most top 1-2% wealth is given away rather than kept in dynasties, which flows back into consumer surplus.
- [[MT018 - BG2 Thanksgiving Roundtable- DOGE, EV Tax Credit, Tariffs, Conspiracy Theories, Invest Ame#^MT018-C10-2|MT018-C10-2]] — Research with the Milken Institute finds funded accounts raise educational attainment, home ownership, entrepreneurship, and financial literacy and reduce incarceration, a structural rebalancing from state to individual.

## [[MT019 - Backtesting Can't Predict The Future - Systematic Investor 310]]
- [[MT019 - Backtesting Can't Predict The Future - Systematic Investor 310#^MT019-C10-3|MT019-C10-3]] — Trends exist in any complex adaptive system, so human behavioural bias (prospect theory) is a symptom, not the root cause.
- [[MT019 - Backtesting Can't Predict The Future - Systematic Investor 310#^MT019-C11-3|MT019-C11-3]] — Markets are nonstationary: the mix of participant groups changes as traders enter and leave, so the balance of divergent and convergent pressure shifts.
- [[MT019 - Backtesting Can't Predict The Future - Systematic Investor 310#^MT019-C14-1|MT019-C14-1]] — AI agents also exert impact, so trends and mean reversion will persist; AI will not remove them.
- [[MT019 - Backtesting Can't Predict The Future - Systematic Investor 310#^MT019-C14-5|MT019-C14-5]] — In nonlinear outlier territory, small differences in price paths lead to significantly dispersed trade results.
- [[MT019 - Backtesting Can't Predict The Future - Systematic Investor 310#^MT019-C15-2|MT019-C15-2]] — Butterfly effect: tiny initial differences create dispersed outcomes in tail events, so correlated markets can still diversify.

## [[MT020 - Bill's Turtle Trading Journey, Modern Systematic Trading, and more! - Open Interest 10]]
- [[MT020 - Bill's Turtle Trading Journey, Modern Systematic Trading, and more! - Open Interest 10#^MT020-C11-2|MT020-C11-2]] — Single stocks have a weaker trend component than futures (splits, dividends, long-dominated), so ETC specialises in futures where it has relative advantage.
- [[MT020 - Bill's Turtle Trading Journey, Modern Systematic Trading, and more! - Open Interest 10#^MT020-C11-3|MT020-C11-3]] — Futures are balanced (one long per short) while stock markets are dominated by longs, so they are different processes.

## [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309]]
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C02-1|MT021-C02-1]] — When central banks stop moving in sync, multiple macro narratives create tactical and dynamic opportunities, which favours active management after 2020.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C02-2|MT021-C02-2]] — Typical endowment and pension portfolios are 85-90% convergent strategies that are implicitly short volatility, with very few divergent strategies.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C04-3|MT021-C04-3]] — A deconstruction of the SG Trend Index since 2000 shows CTAs used faster models earlier; in a less converging, more divergent world shorter signals may work better again.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C06-1|MT021-C06-1]] — When inflation is higher the stock-bond correlation turns positive, so bonds stop acting as the safety trade.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C06-2|MT021-C06-2]] — Average bond volatility is about 4% but rises to roughly 6% in high-inflation regimes, estimated over decades, which makes bonds a less stable base for 60/40.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C08-1|MT021-C08-1]] — In 2022 bonds failed as a hedge and fell with equities, so mark-to-market valuation of bonds still matters even if the yield is locked in.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C08-2|MT021-C08-2]] — When equities fall, bond returns are good when inflation is low (right-shifted, thin left tail) but negative on average, wider and fat-tailed to the left when inflation is high.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C12-2|MT021-C12-2]] — Many trend managers now run 70-80% trend plus 20-30% non-trend diversifiers, one of which is carry.
- [[MT021 - Bonds Behaving Badly - Systematic Investor 309#^MT021-C12-3|MT021-C12-3]] — A study of CTA evolution found carry and other non-trend factors increasingly present in CTA returns, because managers added convergent strategies to smooth returns after trend struggled.

## [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320]]
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C04-2|MT022-C04-2]] — From the Hedge Nordic roundtable: large CTAs hold a privileged position through long-standing relationships with sophisticated investors, which shapes how the industry gets assets.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C05-1|MT022-C05-1]] — A senior Man Group contact said much of their business is solutions-based, reflecting that allocators want a manager plus research support, not just a product.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C05-3|MT022-C05-3]] — A loyal core of institutional allocators of roughly 300 billion in hedge fund assets has decade-long relationships, which is a stable but not growing asset base.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C06-2|MT022-C06-2]] — Managed futures has not grown in a decade, creating zero-sum competition for a finite pool of assets.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C08-2|MT022-C08-2]] — Consultants avoid hedge fund ETFs because their reporting buckets require a hedge fund category, a structural constraint on adoption, not performance.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C09-1|MT022-C09-1]] — Capital-guaranteed CTA notes (zero-coupon bond plus option on a CTA program, as at Man AHL) only work when interest rates are high enough to leave money for the option, so they disappeared in the low-rate decade.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C09-2|MT022-C09-2]] — Principal-protected notes lost their main buyer, insurance companies, because regulators/accounting treated them as bond plus hedge fund, not as a bond.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C09-X3|MT022-C09-X3]] — Capital-guaranteed CTA notes may also have faded because retail regulation still classes them as complex products, since the underlying strategy is seen as complex despite the guarantee.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C10-3|MT022-C10-3]] — Return stacking and capital guarantee are both ways to combine instruments (bond plus option, or bond plus futures) to deliver exposure.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C16-1|MT022-C16-1]] — In five years replication will be a tool delivered through QIS swap products, ETFs and UCITS, showing financial engineering layered over the underlying strategy.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C17-3|MT022-C17-3]] — For 20 years the Bloomberg Aggregate bond index had Sharpe 0.9, negative equity correlation, 350 bp over cash and a max drawdown of 3.8% at 3-4% vol, which made bonds the superb diversifier.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C17-4|MT022-C17-4]] — Bonds are the Voldemort of asset allocation: a cornerstone of nearly every portfolio but with performance this decade that would get an active manager fired.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C18-2|MT022-C18-2]] — US corporate bond volatility may have risen from about 4 to 8-10, possibly above the SG CTA Index, so models for the next 10 years cannot assume 4% bond vol.
- [[MT022 - Bonds- The Voldemort of Asset Allocation - Systematic Investor 320#^MT022-C18-4|MT022-C18-4]] — Allocators are shifting from 60/40 to roughly 50/30/20 with the 20% in assets uncorrelated to stocks and bonds (infrastructure, private credit, private equity, managed futures).

## [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305]]
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C01-2|MT023-C01-2]] — A Goldman Sachs piece with skeptics (MIT's Acemoglu, Jim Covello) argues AI has few tasks that can be automated and is very costly.
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C02-1|MT023-C02-1]] — Unlike the internet, AI needs about a trillion dollars of capex on data centres and chips and cannot yet point to a complex task it will solve, so the disruption case is weaker.
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C02-2|MT023-C02-2]] — Estimates of AI productivity range from 0.5% in total over a decade (MIT academic) to at least 0.5% a year, a huge range of expectations.
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C06-1|MT023-C06-1]] — Immigration drove the US growth surprise, so reversing it reduces labor supply, raises wages and is stagflationary (Posen).
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C09-1|MT023-C09-1]] — A new trend fund must differ from the many 60-100 market trend managers in strategy (speed, sectors, concentration) or cost to attract allocators.
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C10-1|MT023-C10-1]] — Some new trend managers differentiate by going back to classic, more volatile trend following and trading markets large funds cannot.
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C10-3|MT023-C10-3]] — CFM raised its performance fee from 20% to 30%, going against the race to zero fees set by replicators and ETFs.
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C14-3|MT023-C14-3]] — GIC and JPMorgan paper: regime shift (geopolitics, fragmentation, higher inflation, rates and vol) makes long-only allocation harder, so investors seek alpha over beta and hedge funds.
- [[MT023 - Building A Hedge Fund Allocation - Systematic Investor 305#^MT023-C16-6|MT023-C16-6]] — Emerging managers can be added for capacity and because they are more niche, smaller and nimble.

## [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384]]
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C02-3|MT024-C02-3]] — A breakdown in world order (a fourth turning) could create huge moves in financial markets, as already seen in commodities, which is an opportunity for trend following.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C03-3|MT024-C03-3]] — Gold's rise reflects loss of trust in fiat currencies (Dalio says to hold gold), while other commodities move for different reasons.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C04-3|MT024-C04-3]] — A breakdown of trust makes countries prioritise secure supply chains and more spending (military), sacrificing efficiency, which is inflationary and part of the secular move away from globalisation (Bill White).
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C04-4|MT024-C04-4]] — The transition from a carbon to a metal economy and the 20-year lag to build a mine mean price must adjust, producing trends in commodities.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C04-5|MT024-C04-5]] — Current inflation is largely supply-driven (gas from Russia, compromised supply chains) rather than demand-driven.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C05-2|MT024-C05-2]] — Equities return about CPI+0 in high inflation (real return zero), about CPI+10-15 in low inflation, and a little in between.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C06-1|MT024-C06-1]] — In rising inflation bonds return CPI minus, and at about 4.5% inflation they are strongly negative and more correlated with equities, so they fail as the building block.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C06-2|MT024-C06-2]] — Commodities mirror bonds: they do very well in inflation and very poorly in low inflation.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C06-6|MT024-C06-6]] — Over 70 years, rising inflation periods have lasted as long as falling ones, though investors remember mostly falling or low inflation.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C07-4|MT024-C07-4]] — Trends come from supply constraints and shocks, as when low storage and a cold spell cause big natural gas trends.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C09-1|MT024-C09-1]] — Commodity-only trend is valued by allocators as a separate real-asset and inflation risk factor, so separating it from financial trend gives them choice; standalone mixing may not be more diversified.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C09-4|MT024-C09-4]] — Commodities are non-fungible across time, geography and grade (61% vs 65% iron ore), giving true diversification.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C10-X3|MT024-C10-X3]] — Pension portfolios tilted to the green transition did well while green was popular but have underperformed traditional portfolios over the last two or three years.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C14-5|MT024-C14-5]] — Autocorrelation differences arise from market structure (equity rebalancing, value traders, day traders, zero-day options) that changes over time, so trend should be adapted to structure.
- [[MT024 - Building an Inflation-Proof Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.384#^MT024-C16-1|MT024-C16-1]] — Japan's changes (rates, JGBs, carry) are more important for global markets than people realise.

## [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C01-1|MT025-C01-1]] — Cyber crime costs about 4-5 trillion dollars a year (3-4% of global GDP), an area where even the US and China have overlapping interest in rules.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C01-2|MT025-C01-2]] — The post-1989 win-win order has been replaced by a competitive, more mercantilist one (soft power to hard power), and transitions between world orders are unruly.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C02-1|MT025-C02-1]] — Leaders want growth, and shoring that excludes the cheapest suppliers has an economic cost, so value chains will be secured but not fully decoupled.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C02-3|MT025-C02-3]] — New technologies could raise productivity by about 10% over the decade and offset slower trade growth, so the 1970s stagnation will not repeat.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C02-4|MT025-C02-4]] — For decades trade grew two to three times faster than trend GDP and was the engine of growth, which decoupling removes.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C02-5|MT025-C02-5]] — Global trend growth has fallen from about 4% to about 3%, not enough to meet development goals unless technology lifts it again.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C03-3|MT025-C03-3]] — Red tape, weak follow-up of the Draghi report and internal political impulses hold Europe back, though sentiment has improved since Davos.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C03-5|MT025-C03-5]] — Chinese exports to the US fell from 20% of total in 2013 to about 11-12%, so China is more diversified; the US and China together are almost half of world GDP.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C03-X1|MT025-C03-X1]] — The US economy will stay vibrant despite policy noise because of its structural weight: about 27% of world GDP with 5% of world population, almost half of global military power, and most of the big innovative companies.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C04-1|MT025-C04-1]] — China faces structural challenges: peak demand for manufactured goods, property prices and a shrinking workforce, as global demand shifts to services and digital trade.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C07-1|MT025-C07-1]] — Corporate purchasing commitments (Apple 25% green aluminium) create demand for green materials irrespective of legislation.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C07-X4|MT025-C07-X4]] — Corruption costs trillions of dollars a year that would otherwise be invested productively; a WEF coalition of about 100 companies goes beyond national law on anti-corruption and transparency.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C08-1|MT025-C08-1]] — Energy security requires diversification: Europe's dependence on Russia for almost half its natural gas was costly, and the energy transition must not compromise security.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C08-2|MT025-C08-2]] — Gen AI could bring commercial fusion forward from three to four decades to maybe one decade.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C08-3|MT025-C08-3]] — China bases more energy on electricity from renewables, balanced with coal, LNG and nuclear.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C08-4|MT025-C08-4]] — DeepSeek's model built for about a quarter of the cost suggests AI is closer to the software and applications phase than a hardware phase needing trillions in investment.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C08-5|MT025-C08-5]] — Gen AI will replace back-office jobs but, as agriculture fell from 95% to 2% of Swiss jobs, workers move up the value chain, raising productivity and prosperity.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C08-X5|MT025-C08-X5]] — Climate policy must be balanced with energy access and security (a triangle): about 700 million people still lack electricity, so energy supply must grow while growth is decoupled from CO2 emissions.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C08-X6|MT025-C08-X6]] — Brende expects the largest AI-driven changes in medicine and synthetic biology, with many breakthroughs ahead.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C09-1|MT025-C09-1]] — Technology competition between China and the US will matter more than military competition, and others can enter.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C09-2|MT025-C09-2]] — Many of the 10 biggest companies did not exist two or three decades ago, so the next giants may not have started yet.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C09-3|MT025-C09-3]] — Comparative advantage in AI comes from a large home market with data, capital, universities, R&D and engineers.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C09-4|MT025-C09-4]] — AI should reduce inequality in theory, but it needs education, energy and capital, and the platform economy shows winner-takes-all dynamics, though emerging economies with young populations get opportunities.
- [[MT025 - Børge Brende - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT025-C09-X7|MT025-C09-X7]] — China has about 130 unicorns with roughly 10 added each quarter, and its growth now comes mainly from services and digital trade, showing new-technology opportunities beyond the US.

## [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319]]
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C05-5|MT026-C05-5]] — Ageing populations shift savings into fixed income, increasing bond demand and keeping yields lower for longer.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C15-1|MT026-C15-1]] — Yavin's argument: adding more assets (even equal weighted) removes idiosyncratic risk and increases exposure to the common factor (e.g. S&P 500), so diversification benefits diminish.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C15-3|MT026-C15-3]] — CTA performance is driven by a handful of latent macro factors (e.g. commodity move, 2008 risk-off with stocks down and bonds up) that change over time.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C16-1|MT026-C16-1]] — With 300 markets a trend portfolio really holds a few independent macro bets, so idiosyncratic markets like cocoa contribute little and macro trends drive performance.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C17-2|MT026-C17-2]] — Positive skew in a large trend portfolio comes from exposure to underlying macro factors, since idiosyncratic left and right tails wash out, so purer factor exposure gives better skew.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C17-3|MT026-C17-3]] — Allocators could hold a vanilla low-cost CTA as core beta and add a lower-correlation CTA for alpha, as in equities.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C17-4|MT026-C17-4]] — Because returns are non-linear, correlation understates diversification in trend following: benefits continue beyond where correlation analysis says they level off.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C18-1|MT026-C18-1]] — Three replication methods exist: position-level, bottom-up simple model, and return-based; position-level replication is fine and Rob uses it, trading about 20 instruments that replicate his 300-instrument portfolio.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C18-2|MT026-C18-2]] — Bottom-up replication builds a simple trend model on about 30 liquid futures: cheap and good for ETFs to reach investors who cannot meet minimums.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C19-3|MT026-C19-3]] — Low CTA dispersion years indicate one or two dominant factors; high dispersion years indicate varied market events where different weights and trades lead to different results.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C19-4|MT026-C19-4]] — In the simulated universe about 30 factors explain everything, so about 30 futures suffice if they are spread across asset classes and liquid; 30 US bond futures would not work.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C22-1|MT026-C22-1]] — A simple momentum model on 30 futures automatically captures sign changes and is a more sensible cheap trend product than regressing on a benchmark.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C23-1|MT026-C23-1]] — If investors believe a few risk factors drive CTAs and diversification across managers is limited, a cheap bottom-up ETF with about 30 markets at about 50bp is reasonable; return-based replication of an index of many managers is a bad move.
- [[MT026 - CTA Performance- Less is More- - Systematic Investor 319#^MT026-C23-X2|MT026-C23-X2]] — Replication ETFs opened trend following to smaller investors, and CTA managers now offer versions of their own strategies as ETFs, an alternative to replication.

## [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339]]
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C02-1|MT027-C02-1]] — The new US administration's willingness to stress systems widens the range of macro and market outcomes, so fat tails and higher volatility should be expected.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C02-X1|MT027-C02-X1]] — In March 2025 Tom's main macro risks were US stagflation, tariffs, a possible Ukraine ceasefire and a shift in the US role in the world forcing other economies to change how they engage with the US.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C03-3|MT027-C03-3]] — The SG Trend Index and the broader SG CTA Index are 98% correlated, with trend roughly 1.2-1.3 times the broad index, so the roughly 2% annual 5-year gap is mostly leverage and replicating either is equally feasible.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C04-1|MT027-C04-1]] — If non-trend components added meaningful value the broad index would show a clearly higher Sharpe than the trend index, which Andrew does not see; Sharpe in the space has stayed flat or declined over 20 years.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C04-3|MT027-C04-3]] — Trend followers show very high correlations because trend is the dominant factor, yet performance dispersion arises from differences around the periphery (universe, sizing, speed), as in equities.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C05-3|MT027-C05-3]] — A replication portfolio built on US-centric instruments missed diversification in March 2025 (e.g. euro vs dollar, Bunds, JGBs) and underperformed the broad index, ending closer to the trend index; diversification helped actual CTAs.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C06-2|MT027-C06-2]] — Allocators rebalance annually with long-term assumptions, so flexible macro managers and CTAs have an edge in reacting to big information changes.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C08-1|MT027-C08-1]] — Mutual fund AUM in managed futures has been flat, but stacked products (equities plus managed futures, e.g. Catalyst about $8bn at 200bp) are growing.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C09-2|MT027-C09-2]] — Wrapping an ETF equity position in a mutual fund destroys its tax efficiency (ETFs avoid realized gains) and adds fees.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C09-3|MT027-C09-3]] — Getting equity exposure via futures in the US triggers annual mark-to-market taxation (around 30% on some), further eroding tax efficiency of stacked products.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C12-1|MT027-C12-1]] — Rather than simply lengthening horizons, CTAs now run many more models and allocate risk across them; the SG Trend indicator keeps a medium-term model for consistency.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C12-X1|MT027-C12-X1]] — Tom doubts that capacity (AUM) explains the industry's slower trend horizons, because no CTA has grown very large since Winton (above $30bn then, now below $10bn); multi-model risk allocation is the more likely cause.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C15-1|MT027-C15-1]] — Bottom-up replication indices work at first then drift as the industry evolves, whereas Andrew's top-down replication is adaptive because it targets current industry positioning.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C15-2|MT027-C15-2]] — Replication is a short-horizon bet on weekly industry positioning; it has stably high correlation over long periods and is rebalanced weekly using weighted data (3 current and 17 stale points).
