- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C15-X1|MT027-C15-X1]] — Even if erratic, short-cycle policy news favours short-term models, CTAs will at most shorten their models at the margin, not move wholesale to short horizons.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C16-2|MT027-C16-2]] — Index-based replicators lose correlation to the benchmark more in stress periods than mechanical bottom-up replicators.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C16-3|MT027-C16-3]] — Single-manager trend selections vary more over time than the replicator versus index, so replication is a structural-alpha tradeoff against noise.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C17-1|MT027-C17-1]] — Managed futures ETFs hold about $3bn of the roughly $330-340bn in the space and have grown 10x in 5-6 years, so they are not a big threat except BlackRock's entry.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C18-3|MT027-C18-3]] — PIMCO's program was designed to be more sensitive to equity sell-offs, explaining its underperformance in 2022.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C19-5|MT027-C19-5]] — BlackRock's launch may legitimise managed futures in wealth management, though platforms may then only use BlackRock.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C20-1|MT027-C20-1]] — Barclay managed futures AUM data include Bridgewater's two products, which fell from about $145bn (Feb 2021) to about $97bn, so stagnant industry AUM may reflect one firm's decline.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C20-2|MT027-C20-2]] — The competitive threat to hedge funds a decade ago was banks offering cheap QIS products, whose performance and fees are not visible, making comparison unfair.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C21-1|MT027-C21-1]] — QIS products are bought by smaller to large groups for tactical trades (e.g. a curve steepener with continual delta hedging) rather than the hedge fund team, so money isn't coming from traditional hedge fund allocations.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C21-2|MT027-C21-2]] — After 2022 more investors adopt trend following because bonds stopped diversifying equities, undermining the stock-bond relationships on which asset allocation models rely.
- [[MT027 - CTAs vs ETFs- The Showdown That Could Change Everything - Systematic Investor 339#^MT027-C22-1|MT027-C22-1]] — A star ETF labelled CTA holds essentially only commodities and some rates, showing the CTA label is loosely used and its recent returns reflect a commodity run.

## [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329]]
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C01-1|MT028-C01-1]] — Andrew expects the new US administration, staffed by self-made billionaires focused on where America can go over the next 20 years, to revive animal spirits in the US.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C03-2|MT028-C03-2]] — In the first five years of the 2020s bonds produced a zero Sharpe ratio, a key trigger for growing managed futures since the old argument was just to hold bonds.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C03-3|MT028-C03-3]] — Rather than moving 60/40 to 50/30/20, allocators may move to 60/20/20 by taking the diversifier money from bonds while keeping equities.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C03-X1|MT028-C03-X1]] — US allocators building 'robust' portfolios increasingly use uncorrelated alternative strategies to fill the diversification gap left by bonds, driven by worry that equity returns will slow.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C04-3|MT028-C04-3]] — The SG CTA Index holds the 20 largest open CTAs, about half non-trend (quant macro, short-term under 10 days hold, commodity-only, technical), while SG Trend and short-term indices are subsets, and CTA is a regulatory term not a synonym for trend following.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C06-1|MT028-C06-1]] — Hedge fund indices are only usable by institutions that can invest in hedge funds, so wealth managers need an investable, mechanical approximation, which motivated replication.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C07-3|MT028-C07-3]] — A five-manager flat-fee index (Bridge Alternatives) tracks the 20-manager SG CTA Index very closely, so diversification arrives quickly with 2-4 managers and lifts index correlation to the 80s percent.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C08-3|MT028-C08-3]] — A bottom-up trend tracker may match long-term return and correlation but its drawdowns and risk differ from real CTAs, because it omits individual markets and risk management.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C09-1|MT028-C09-1]] — Replication with a full factor set correlates around 0.9 to the index and about 0.8 with a limited set, with similar equity beta and crisis alpha.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C10-1|MT028-C10-1]] — Cocoa's roughly $5-6bn profit over 2-3 months is small relative to $300bn of CTA assets, so missing cocoa matters little for a replicator.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C10-2|MT028-C10-2]] — Replication underperforms when many markets it does not hold (Mexican peso, Canadian rates, softs, commodities) trend at once, but idiosyncratic hits and misses mostly cancel across managers.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C10-4|MT028-C10-4]] — Because large commodity moves pull other markets, a replicator holding oil and gold still captures some exposure to markets it does not trade (planets and moons).
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C11-1|MT028-C11-1]] — Replication is an incremental tool and allocators combine it with managers like Man AHL, who are better at short-term inflections.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C12-1|MT028-C12-1]] — An index should be predictable and unchanged over time; replication's index-like ethos suits wealth managers who want a consistent product.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C12-2|MT028-C12-2]] — ETF model-portfolio managers care about an accessible, predictable diversifier rather than which commodity is ripping.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C15-1|MT028-C15-1]] — Managed futures AUM grew to about $350bn in 2009-10 and has stayed between $300bn and $400bn since, with the main increase after strong 2022 performance.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C15-2|MT028-C15-2]] — The size cutoff for the SG CTA Index rose from $800m (2010) to $1bn (2016), $1.5bn (2019) and $1.82bn now, with growth in the mid-sized group, while the SG Trend (top 10) minimum is $4.8bn.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C15-3|MT028-C15-3]] — Trend following is about 80% of the CTA space and commoditized with few entrants; nearly 90% of new quant start-ups are not trend and many are becoming multi-asset quant groups targeting high Sharpe.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C16-1|MT028-C16-1]] — The CTA industry has settled into a cheaper, more commoditized core trend with more focus on risk controls and other differentiators, instead of the change Andrew expected in 2015.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C16-2|MT028-C16-2]] — A one-week replication bet on industry positioning is preferred to locking into a fixed trend design for 10 years, because the industry will evolve.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C16-3|MT028-C16-3]] — Managed futures sits in the hedge fund bucket (a pension has about 8% in hedge funds, of which little in managed futures), so a diversifier with strong statistics gets only about a 50bp allocation, limiting growth.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C20-2|MT028-C20-2]] — BlackRock's Aladdin and model portfolios could set managed futures as a 2-3% allocation across wealth management, driving institutional adoption.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C20-3|MT028-C20-3]] — Replication barely depends on which instruments are used since it is a short-term correlation bet; the UCITS-friendly version excluding commodities has a nine-year track record.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C21-1|MT028-C21-1]] — A non-commodity replication index uses AUD and CAD as proxies for commodities; it should outperform the hedge fund index by about 250-300bp versus 350bp for the standard model, and correlation falls from 0.9 to 0.8.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C21-2|MT028-C21-2]] — About a quarter of European investors want ESG-sensitive products and many cannot hold commodities, creating demand for non-commodity CTA exposure.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C21-3|MT028-C21-3]] — Excluding asset classes from a traditional trend model reduces diversification and returns, which is why replication is attractive for commodity-restricted investors.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C22-1|MT028-C22-1]] — Futures are zero-sum so ESG labelling of derivatives is unclear; there is no CTA with an SFDR Article 8/9 stamp.
- [[MT028 - CTAs- Innovation Meets Legacy & the BlackRock Effect - Systematic Investor 329#^MT028-C22-3|MT028-C22-3]] — Investor constraints differ by country (German investors avoid agricultural commodities but accept gold and oil), so a single compliant product is hard.

## [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148]]
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C04-1|MT029-C04-1]] — Alternative and OTC markets less affected by the Fed put (the Evolution program) kept earning during the CTA winter, so researching new markets with different price drivers added genuine value.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C05-1|MT029-C05-1]] — Because the utility of a CTA is crisis alpha and convexity relative to what the allocator holds, asset-class-specific trend products (e.g. fixed income only) are better building blocks than one pooled program.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C05-2|MT029-C05-2]] — Crypto trends well because it has no valuation anchor and is in permanent imbalance, so big trends form.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C06-3|MT029-C06-3]] — In Rob's data equity trend does less well than trend in VIX or fixed income, which may relate to carry being naturally higher in fixed income.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C06-5|MT029-C06-5]] — Commodity markets diversify naturally (equal weight works), but fixed income has high loading on the Fed as a common factor, so portfolio construction there must be cleverer.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C07-3|MT029-C07-3]] — About 70-75% of AHL's P&L over the last 25 years came from fixed income, so implementation specific to that sector matters.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C07-4|MT029-C07-4]] — Rob argues fixed income dominates CTA P&L because it is the largest and most liquid sector and enjoyed a secular decline in rates for 30-40 years that ended around 2010-2012, and recently made little.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C08-1|MT029-C08-1]] — Trends arise from sustained imbalances, such as an interest-rate gradient between central banks, which creates sustained carry flows and positive autocorrelation; EURUSD without such gradient shows little trend while EM currencies with high carry trend strongly.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C08-2|MT029-C08-2]] — Emerging market currencies with strong carry show strong trend, whereas developed-market FX like EURUSD does not trend well.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C09-3|MT029-C09-3]] — In commodities, supply takes time to meet demand shocks (e.g. AI power demand), creating sustained price imbalance and trend; liquidity should be judged by physical versus financial market size.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C11-1|MT029-C11-1]] — After the GFC transaction reporting and central clearing of swaps improved OTC market liquidity visibility, though broker relationships remain valuable.
- [[MT029 - CTAs- The Good, The Bad, & The Misunderstood - Top Traders Unplugged 148#^MT029-C13-3|MT029-C13-3]] — CTAs are not designed to profit from equity crises; good crisis returns result from the strategy and diversification, so selling them as crisis alpha is a mistake and uncorrelated returns are the real value.

## [[MT030 - Can Trend Following Be Saved- - Systematic Investor 348]]
- [[MT030 - Can Trend Following Be Saved- - Systematic Investor 348#^MT030-C03-1|MT030-C03-1]] — Apple built its supply chain in China (about $275bn pledged 2016-2021, engineers trained locally), so tariff-driven reshoring of production is unrealistic over the near term, which makes trade-war policy hard to trade on fundamentals.
- [[MT030 - Can Trend Following Be Saved- - Systematic Investor 348#^MT030-C04-X1|MT030-C04-X1]] — Institutions bring trend following in-house (own teams or QIS swaps) not only for cost but because staff gain status by moving from allocating to external managers to running money themselves.
- [[MT030 - Can Trend Following Be Saved- - Systematic Investor 348#^MT030-C13-1|MT030-C13-1]] — The core trend beta was discovered decades ago; when banks offered QIS products and GSA a 50bp product in the early 2010s it was commoditised, pushing managers to differentiate through added features.
- [[MT030 - Can Trend Following Be Saved- - Systematic Investor 348#^MT030-C18-2|MT030-C18-2]] — Replication works because it captures managed futures trading against slow investors using a few instruments such as the 10-year Treasury; niche markets like palm oil add little for that edge.
- [[MT030 - Can Trend Following Be Saved- - Systematic Investor 348#^MT030-C19-3|MT030-C19-3]] — Bonds have failed since 2020 (inflation not transitory, high stock-bond correlation), which will drive trend into wealth-management portfolios; allocators avoid admitting the mistake of holding 40% in bonds.
- [[MT030 - Can Trend Following Be Saved- - Systematic Investor 348#^MT030-C20-1|MT030-C20-1]] — Recent winners are not highly correlated to the CTA index (not 90%) and differ materially, while others will seek cheaper beta-like exposure that suits replication.

## [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379]]
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C02-5|MT031-C02-5]] — The VIX rises mechanically as the market falls because at-the-money vol slides up the skew, so its rise alone does not show fear.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C04-4|MT031-C04-4]] — For most companies margins fall as rates rise because rates track inflation and inflation raises costs.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C04-5|MT031-C04-5]] — About 25% of companies, notably negative-working-capital businesses such as insurers and many financials, see margins improve as rates and inflation rise because they get cash upfront, pay later and face fewer competitors.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C05-1|MT031-C05-1]] — Transactional or distributor businesses (card networks, banks, Coca-Cola) benefit in inflation because nominal volumes rise without proportional cost increases.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C05-2|MT031-C05-2]] — Buffett's top holdings (banks, insurers, energy, distributors) are positioned for rising long rates and a steeper yield curve, which is a risk-management play.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C05-3|MT031-C05-3]] — Chevron is the major most likely to benefit from Venezuela developments because it has existing Venezuelan operations.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C08-5|MT031-C08-5]] — Karsan's structural-inflation and populism thesis led to calls on gold and gold vol (gold at 2500, buy 3000 calls) years ago.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C09-1|MT031-C09-1]] — Gold lagged other commodities despite being the best asset for structural inflation, so the gap was a relative-value opportunity in precious metals, not a broad commodities call.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C09-4|MT031-C09-4]] — In the 1960s-70s, precious metals were both the best-performing and most volatile asset.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C09-6|MT031-C09-6]] — Equity index went nowhere nominally from 1968-82, so equity implied vol compressed in that regime.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C09-7|MT031-C09-7]] — FX vol (and precious metals, which trade like FX) should be long in the structural inflation regime.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C09-X1|MT031-C09-X1]] — One secular driver (the 40-year fall in interest rates) drove nearly all asset returns, so getting the single regime driver right matters more than short-term direction calls.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C10-1|MT031-C10-1]] — Regime rule: precious metal, FX and bond vol rise on all horizons, while long-dated equity vol falls (short-term only in line).
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C10-4|MT031-C10-4]] — Populism is the outcome of an inequality cycle, so this is not a sample of one; it provides a structural basis for the 1960s-70s analogy.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C10-5|MT031-C10-5]] — Inequality expansion is felt unequally across generations; young labor is disenfranchised while the old own capital, which drives political change.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C11-1|MT031-C11-1]] — The inequality/populism cycle is useful for decade-scale outcomes but not for 1-5 year forecasts.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C11-4|MT031-C11-4]] — From 1968-82 the equity market fell about 55-60% in real terms yet was flat nominally.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C12-2|MT031-C12-2]] — 60/40 delivered zero real return over 1900-1920, 1929-1949 and 1963-1982 (60 of 82 years), so it only worked after rates peaked in 1982.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C12-3|MT031-C12-3]] — 60/40 success since 1982 is an artifact of falling rates that created 400%+ equity multiple expansion.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C12-4|MT031-C12-4]] — Profit margins are highly correlated with rates and moved from record lows in 1982 to record highs, adding to returns.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C12-5|MT031-C12-5]] — Over 125 years there was zero diversification benefit between stocks and bonds, and stock-bond correlation is mostly positive.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C12-6|MT031-C12-6]] — Sharpe is ~0.35 for stocks vs 0.37 for 60/40 over 125 years, and 0.27 vs 0.26 over 1900-82, so 60/40 adds nothing.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C12-7|MT031-C12-7]] — The 60/40 diversification benefit of the last 40 years (Sharpe 0.62 vs 0.52) comes only from falling rates.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C15-3|MT031-C15-3]] — In populist periods presidential turnover is faster (5 presidents in 1962-82, about 4 years each vs 7.1 years average in the last 40), reflecting political upheaval.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C16-X2|MT031-C16-X2]] — The populist political pressure on markets is unavoidable unless the democratic system is circumvented by authoritarianism, which is the stated exception to the thesis.
- [[MT031 - Cem Karsan on the Market’s Quiet Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.379#^MT031-C17-2|MT031-C17-2]] — Political pressure from populism is the ultimate problem for equities, and for bonds too.

## [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra]]
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C02-2|MT032-C02-2]] — China leads global open-source AI models (six to seven high-quality providers; Qwen over 400M downloads) while Llama 4 is sputtering.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C02-3|MT032-C02-3]] — Open weights let Chinese labs build on, distill and remix each other's work, speeding progress without separate giant training clusters.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C02-4|MT032-C02-4]] — Leading models are distilled to small, cheap models fast: a 30B-parameter Qwen matched GPT-4o performance.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C02-5|MT032-C02-5]] — China's weaker IP and copyright stance lets its model makers train on copyrighted data freely, an advantage over US labs.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C03-1|MT032-C03-1]] — Models that use tools (web search) need not compress the whole internet, so reasoning models shift the open vs closed balance and cut the size and cost needed.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C03-3|MT032-C03-3]] — China's long embrace of open source (Linux) and weaker IP protection made open-weights AI a natural strategy.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C04-1|MT032-C04-1]] — Seven or eight well-funded Chinese open-model labs co-evolve fast through distillation, producing quick progress unlike software competition.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C04-2|MT032-C04-2]] — Shared best practices (open source) give a community higher output and less chance of a breakout monopolist than closed competition.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C05-1|MT032-C05-1]] — Leading Chinese open models offer about 90% of frontier intelligence at about a 90% lower price, driving demand from developers and enterprises.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C05-2|MT032-C05-2]] — Enterprises want an accountable vendor for open-source software (Red Hat for Linux), so a US-domiciled open model company could win.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C05-4|MT032-C05-4]] — Large tech firms that are not winning on offense fund open source to commoditize a rival (Facebook Open Compute; Alibaba funding Zhipu).
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C06-1|MT032-C06-1]] — Laggard big tech (Microsoft, Amazon, Apple) should fund open-source competitors instead of backing a proprietary lab.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C06-2|MT032-C06-2]] — New US entrants can start from Chinese open models and reach near-frontier quality without frontier-lab spending, and regulatory capture fights may follow.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C07-3|MT032-C07-3]] — Chinese open-source release cadence and quality now exceeds the US open-source ecosystem.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C08-2|MT032-C08-2]] — Inference-time reasoning and agent-to-agent interaction raise compute demand, refuting the earlier overbuild (Cisco 2000) concern.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C08-3|MT032-C08-3]] — Google's monthly tokens grew from 5T to 480T and then above 1 quadrillion, about 200x in a little over a year.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C08-4|MT032-C08-4]] — Groq inference capacity is fully consumed within hours of deployment, so demand outstrips supply.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C08-X1|MT032-C08-X1]] — Search queries have turned into AI inference transactions, so ordinary search volume now feeds directly into compute demand.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C09-1|MT032-C09-1]] — Private labs can lose money (OpenAI about $7B a year) that public companies like Google would not allow, a new competitive advantage.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C09-3|MT032-C09-3]] — Nvidia, Dell and possibly SK Hynix are the winners of AI capex as suppliers.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C09-4|MT032-C09-4]] — Reasoning models consume 10-100x more tokens per query, a driver of token growth.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C10-3|MT032-C10-3]] — Prices fall very steeply for older model generations, so users can switch to older or cheaper models to cut losses.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C10-5|MT032-C10-5]] — Coding-agent companies can distill open models under Apache licenses to replace expensive proprietary APIs for most use cases.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C10-X1|MT032-C10-X1]] — Jevons paradox: as AI capacity grows and intelligence gets cheaper, total token consumption rises rather than falls, so efficiency gains do not reduce compute demand.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C11-1|MT032-C11-1]] — OpenAI can use its high-margin consumer business to subsidize a cheap open model for price-sensitive coding workloads, as Amazon did with AWS.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C11-3|MT032-C11-3]] — Google's TPU advantage depends on third-party use, as most TPU volume is internal.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C11-X1|MT032-C11-X1]] — AI pricing will rationalize from flat subscriptions toward consumption-based pricing with automatic routing to cheaper models, which improves lab economics (already happening in enterprise).
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C12-1|MT032-C12-1]] — User memory and history create switching costs and pricing power for ChatGPT even if models commoditize.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C12-2|MT032-C12-2]] — Value accrues to whoever owns the consumer (as Google search did for 20 years), and ChatGPT is the first real threat.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C12-3|MT032-C12-3]] — Open-source distillation drives intelligence up and cost down, so model value shifts to applications.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C12-4|MT032-C12-4]] — ChatGPT may exceed one billion weekly users in 2025.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C13-1|MT032-C13-1]] — AI will be a heterogeneous market with many competitors and lower margins, yet a rising tide lets many do well; OpenAI plus Anthropic are worth over $500B.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C15-5|MT032-C15-5]] — Tariffs make US onshoring (rare earth magnets, chips, data centers, energy) investable.
- [[MT032 - China Open-Source, Compute Arms Race, Reordering Global Trade - BG2 w- Bill Gurley and Bra#^MT032-C16-2|MT032-C16-2]] — The EU and China each represent a roughly $300B US goods deficit, so they were the key targets.

## [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G]]
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C02-1|MT033-C02-1]] — Companies are walling off data from AI (Reddit suing Anthropic, Anthropic cutting Windsurf off, Salesforce changing Slack/CRM terms to bar training), a strategic shift as AI gains value.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C02-2|MT033-C02-2]] — Enterprise applications will split into open-data and closed-data vendors, and competitors will use open data to take customers.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C03-1|MT033-C03-1]] — Platform dependency risk: TripAdvisor fell from $20B to $1B once Google took over recommendations, so AI app companies reliant on a model provider face the same risk.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C03-2|MT033-C03-2]] — OpenAI hit 400B annual searches 8 years faster than Google, so platform cycles in AI are much faster than in the internet era.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C03-3|MT033-C03-3]] — Because AI moves fast, model and platform firms vertically integrate and close data access to monetize, forcing app companies (Windsurf, Cursor) to build their own models.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C03-X1|MT033-C03-X1]] — As AI assistants need access to personal data, firms that own the underlying data systems (Google: phone OS, Gmail, office suite) gain a competitive edge if they choose to wall off access.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C04-1|MT033-C04-1]] — China's industrial policy seeds hundreds of competitors in chosen industries and lets Darwinian competition choose winners, unlike Soviet-style single state champions.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C04-2|MT033-C04-2]] — Many competitors try different approaches (Chinese solid-state lidar about $130 vs Waymo about $5,000), giving more optionality.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C05-1|MT033-C05-1]] — Each supplier faces 50-100 competitors so China's supply chain is more robust, explaining EV success (500 US EV startups, a handful of survivors).
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C06-X1|MT033-C06-X1]] — After Darwinian competition identifies winners, the Chinese state subsidizes them (e.g., about $2B to BYD), a second stage of the industrial policy.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C07-3|MT033-C07-3]] — China's four deep-pocketed open-source AI players fit its pattern of many competitors and affordability goals, like the EV and solar systems.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C08-1|MT033-C08-1]] — About half of AI researchers are of Chinese origin and China's AI patent count exceeds the US, so restricting Chinese students threatens US AI leadership.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C08-2|MT033-C08-2]] — Graduates who cannot stay in the US found competitors abroad, so keeping them (green card with diploma) would add US companies.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C08-X1|MT033-C08-X1]] — Because China caps the equity upside of its entrepreneurs, a welcoming US could attract Chinese founders to build their companies in the US.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C09-1|MT033-C09-1]] — US openness to talent drove productivity and national security leadership; capricious policy damages 'brand America' for future talent.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C09-2|MT033-C09-2]] — Roughly 40-50% of top US AI researchers are Chinese, so broad restrictions on Chinese students would be self-defeating.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C10-2|MT033-C10-2]] — Skilled immigration has been capped at about 200-250k a year for 20 years; the US should increase it to cherry-pick top talent.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C10-3|MT033-C10-3]] — China's rare earth magnet monopoly is a kill shot risk for US autos and defense, causing factory shutdown risk.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C11-1|MT033-C11-1]] — Selling a downgraded chip keeps Chinese developers in the CUDA ecosystem and lets Nvidia compete, slowing Huawei's global spread.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C11-4|MT033-C11-4]] — Without rare earth access the US faces about six quarters of disruption (growth slowdown, auto and military part shortages) while supply is rebuilt.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C11-5|MT033-C11-5]] — Denying China AI chips while calling AI existential raises the odds China moves on Taiwan.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C11-X1|MT033-C11-X1]] — Both the US (rare earths) and China (Nvidia chips) need years to remove their dependencies; the US needs 3-4 more years to shift supply chains, making conflict in that window especially costly.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C12-3|MT033-C12-3]] — Leading-edge chip fabrication is nearly all in Taiwan today; US share could reach 15-20% by 2030 and 40-50% with a UAE fab, but only without a Taiwan war.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C15-2|MT033-C15-2]] — ISS and Glass Lewis have 97% share of proxy advice and may have misaligned agendas (Senator Hagerty).
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C16-1|MT033-C16-1]] — Super-voting shares are used to escape proxy advisors who may not serve shareholder interests, and index funds should not just follow them.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C16-2|MT033-C16-2]] — An AI-based governance advisor could replace ISS/Glass Lewis; the hosts invite founders to build one.
- [[MT033 - China, AI Immigration, Rare Earths & Chips, Tariffs, Markets - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT033-C16-X1|MT033-C16-X1]] — Thin index-fund margins push passive managers to outsource votes to proxy advisors (both over 80% foreign-owned per the senator), concentrating governance power that may not track shareholder interest.

## [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company]]
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C01-2|MT034-C01-2]] — Europe is the most open advanced economy and lacks fossil energy, so trade and energy shocks hit it harder than others.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C02-2|MT034-C02-2]] — Energy price rises feed through to the broader economy within months.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C05-1|MT034-C05-1]] — European productivity diverged from the US since the internet revolution and never caught up.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C05-2|MT034-C05-2]] — US leads in AI pioneering (chips, data, energy price) but Europe is not behind in AI diffusion at SMEs.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C05-3|MT034-C05-3]] — AI diffused in about 3 years versus 30 for electricity, so productivity impact may arrive faster than expected.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C05-4|MT034-C05-4]] — AI is deflationary only if it raises productivity enough, offset by energy use and the roughly $1B cost of frontier training.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C05-5|MT034-C05-5]] — Geopolitical fragmentation may limit the global scale needed to amortize expensive LLMs.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C05-X1|MT034-C05-X1]] — Early signs that AI diffusion is starting to lift European productivity numbers, though it is too soon to confirm.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C06-2|MT034-C06-2]] — Each of the 27 EU states adds its own rules (gold plating), slowing reforms like capital markets union and producing 27 regimes.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C06-3|MT034-C06-3]] — European workforce decline will force debates over pensions, retirement age and immigration; AI will not substitute everything.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C06-X1|MT034-C06-X1]] — A culture of vested interests and accrued rights in Europe delays labour-market and productivity reforms, requiring cultural change as the workforce shrinks.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C07-3|MT034-C07-3]] — The dollar still holds about 60% of reserves and 40% of trade invoicing and remains the crisis destination.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C07-4|MT034-C07-4]] — Confidence in the dollar is slightly eroded but changes slowly; the ECB widened euro repo lines to boost euro availability.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C08-2|MT034-C08-2]] — Stablecoins mainly serve crypto-to-fiat transitions and cross-border payments; their growth has been flat since October 2025 and Lagarde doubts the business case.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C08-X1|MT034-C08-X1]] — Because payments are going digital and central bank money anchors the financial system, central bank money must exist in digital form, and the rails for digital assets should be a public good.
- [[MT034 - Christine Lagarde - President of the European Central Bank - Podcast - In Good Company#^MT034-C08-X2|MT034-C08-X2]] — Stablecoins exploit a real market failure in cross-border payments; if banks and the digital euro fix cross-border payments, that part of the stablecoin business case disappears.

## [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B]]
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C01-X1|MT035-C01-X1]] — Coatue frames AI as a sector that could eventually reach about 75% of total US market cap, by analogy with earlier dominant sectors (industrials, transport), against the view that AI is already too big to grow.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C02-1|MT035-C02-1]] — Tech waves (mainframe, PC, internet, SaaS, AI) stack on one another, so each is larger than the last.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C02-2|MT035-C02-2]] — Market leadership has rotated over centuries (finance and real estate, manufacturing, energy, now about 50% tech), and AI power and semicap suppliers should be counted in the tech trend.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C02-3|MT035-C02-3]] — Technology rose from 5% to 15% of global GDP over Coatue's life and will exceed 15% in 10 years.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C02-5|MT035-C02-5]] — CoreWeave offers pure-play AI exposure, which is rare among public companies with legacy businesses.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C02-X1|MT035-C02-X1]] — In 2025 much of the AI value creation accrued to private labs (OpenAI, Anthropic) while the public Mag 7 were flat, so public investors captured little of it directly.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C03-1|MT035-C03-1]] — Bitcoin (about $2T) is about 0.4-0.5% of world net worth of $450-500T (equities 120T, real estate 100-150T, gold 15-20T), so a rise to $4-6T is plausible and institutions cannot ignore it.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C03-4|MT035-C03-4]] — Falling Bitcoin volatility may move it into an institutional asset class.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C05-2|MT035-C05-2]] — Interest-paying stablecoins could let the Treasury sell bonds directly to consumers worldwide, bypassing dealers.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C05-3|MT035-C05-3]] — Google may lose search share to ChatGPT but has offsetting assets (YouTube, Waymo, Android, Gmail).
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C06-2|MT035-C06-2]] — Users who subscribe to ChatGPT cut Google page views by about 8-11% (peak to trough, over two years), versus 4% annual growth without it.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C06-4|MT035-C06-4]] — ChatGPT's adoption beat Twitter, Instagram, Facebook and TikTok and held up despite new entrants (Gemini, Grok, Meta), with no virality.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C06-5|MT035-C06-5]] — Network effects and switching costs from permanent memory begin to protect ChatGPT.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C07-1|MT035-C07-1]] — Share of Nvidia GPU allocation differs from cloud revenue share: Amazon 44% vs 20%, Oracle 5% vs 19%, CoreWeave 11%, Microsoft and Google matched at 30/20.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C07-2|MT035-C07-2]] — Amazon's low GPU share means AWS is behind in AI, is using its own chips, or Nvidia avoids a dominant customer.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C08-1|MT035-C08-1]] — With Stargate, Anthropic, sovereigns and telecoms, there could be a dozen hyperscalers instead of two or three.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C08-3|MT035-C08-3]] — Expensive reasoning models may run on Nvidia while cheap local models run on custom chips.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C09-2|MT035-C09-2]] — AI productivity matters more than tariffs for the equity outlook, though tariffs may not have passed through inflation yet.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C10-1|MT035-C10-1]] — A productivity boom could produce nominal GDP growth of 5-6%, as in the 1990s.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C10-2|MT035-C10-2]] — Public flight path: tariffs manageable, multiples full as in the 1990s, 10-year in the fours, plus an AI super cycle.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C11-2|MT035-C11-2]] — Meta's structure to buy 49% of Scale AI at a $30B valuation (about $15B) avoids regulatory scrutiny, showing urgency and the size of the AI prize, at roughly 1% of Meta's market cap.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C12-1|MT035-C12-1]] — Anthropic revenue accelerated: about 12 months for the first $1B, 3 months for the next, 2 months for the next, showing AI revenue growth speed.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C12-2|MT035-C12-2]] — Large venture-backed firms should list for financing, discipline, brand and retail participation (democratization).
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C12-3|MT035-C12-3]] — A quasi-public tier of companies above $5-10B stay private because private markets, private equity continuation funds, secondaries and private credit now have the depth to fund them.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C13-3|MT035-C13-3]] — The Mag 7 grew over 20% compounded for 3-4 years while opex grew about 2%, a historically unseen margin expansion driven by AI efficiency.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C13-4|MT035-C13-4]] — Microsoft headcount shows zero-rate hiring, then a 'get fit' stabilization, and now an AI era where it may have hit peak headcount.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C14-1|MT035-C14-1]] — AppLovin doubled revenue per employee in four years, revenue doubled while headcount fell 35% as it right-sized and used AI.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C14-2|MT035-C14-2]] — Google has 187,000 employees against OpenAI's 2,700, showing small lean AI-native firms can take share.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C14-3|MT035-C14-3]] — Jensen Huang plans to triple Nvidia with flat headcount because agents will report to him.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C14-5|MT035-C14-5]] — Firm-level AI efficiency aggregates into economy-wide productivity.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C14-X1|MT035-C14-X1]] — Headcount cuts at AI-first firms such as AppLovin are driven by competitive design (fewer layers, faster decisions) rather than cost-cutting for its own sake, which is why they coincide with faster growth.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C15-1|MT035-C15-1]] — AI may cut labor per company but increase firm formation (Jevons-type effect), so unemployment may fall rather than rise.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C15-2|MT035-C15-2]] — Coatue matrix: companies growing over 25% and profitable should prepare for an IPO.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C16-1|MT035-C16-1]] — Profitable low-growth companies should use AI-driven architecture shift to invest and play offense, even accepting losses.
- [[MT035 - Coatue’s Laffont Brothers. AI, Public & VC Mkts, Macro, US Debt, Crypto, IPO's, & more - B#^MT035-C16-3|MT035-C16-3]] — Companies growing under 25% and burning cash should reinvent: go all in on a small fast-growing product or open source an asset.

## [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294]]
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C03-6|MT036-C03-6]] — Cocoa had long untrended periods and is not very liquid, so some trend followers may not have included it in their basket and missed the later trend.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C04-5|MT036-C04-5]] — VIX and natural gas/electricity are analogous because neither can be easily stored or replicated, giving futures term structures and option dynamics that look alike.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C05-6|MT036-C05-6]] — Deglobalization and food and energy security concerns require more domestic infrastructure and add frictions to commodity trade, which could push prices higher.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C05-7|MT036-C05-7]] — The green transition requires large amounts of industrial metals such as copper (e.g. rebuilding the grid), supporting copper demand.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C05-8|MT036-C05-8]] — Greater extremes in temperature and precipitation (e.g. Brazil deforestation) destabilise grain and other commodity prices and should raise price volatility.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C05-9|MT036-C05-9]] — Remilitarization raises demand for metals and energy.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C06-1|MT036-C06-1]] — In both world wars wheat prices rose three to four times in sustained moves, an analogy for a geopolitically driven commodity rally.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C06-2|MT036-C06-2]] — Better technology and supply chains limit how far grains can rally compared with the wars, putting downward pressure on grain prices at the top.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C08-X1|MT036-C08-X1]] — As of May 2024 Henry Hub natural gas was cheap; a trend follower would be short, but a long-term bull should warehouse it via the futures-plus-options structure rather than pay roll costs or be short.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C11-1|MT036-C11-1]] — The edge relies on liquid options and storable underlyings, so it does not extend to weather derivatives.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C11-2|MT036-C11-2]] — Physically backed ETFs (gold) give real storage but blended futures ETFs lose it through roll costs, leaving a gap the strategy fills.
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C13-1|MT036-C13-1]] — Commodity equities are noisy proxies for commodity prices (hedging, diversification, passive flows, equity beta).
- [[MT036 - Commodity Super Cycle Inbound! - Systematic Investor 294#^MT036-C13-2|MT036-C13-2]] — Applied to copper, silver, gold, oil, some gas and grains; gas is limited because of poor storability (seven or eight markets running).

## [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331]]
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C01-1|MT037-C01-1]] — The Bundesbank forecast only 0.1% German growth for 2025 before tariffs, and said Trump tariffs could cut a further 0.6%, making a third consecutive contraction year possible.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C02-1|MT037-C02-1]] — Germany's model relied on exporting capital goods to China and cheap Russian gas; both sides turned against it, causing the slump.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C02-2|MT037-C02-2]] — High gas prices made energy-intensive sectors such as chemicals uneconomical in Germany.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C02-3|MT037-C02-3]] — China moved from importing German capital goods to competing in mid-tech and higher-value goods (solar panels, EVs), hurting German exports.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C02-4|MT037-C02-4]] — Germany's rising dependency ratio (fewer workers per dependant) is now biting, with anti-immigration politics limiting the fix.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C02-6|MT037-C02-6]] — Energy prices alone cannot explain the German slump because oil is no higher than before the Ukraine invasion (though gas may differ).
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C03-1|MT037-C03-1]] — Policymakers in the West assumed China's WTO entry would drive political liberalisation and open markets; instead China became a strong producer closed to foreigners, a forecasting error with trade consequences for Germany.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C03-2|MT037-C03-2]] — German policy missteps (nuclear closure, fiscal conservatism limiting public investment, weak digitalisation) added to external shocks.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C05-1|MT037-C05-1]] — Trend following has near-zero equity beta over the long term, so statistically its return is alpha, although commoditisation weakens that view.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C10-2|MT037-C10-2]] — Gold and the dollar rising together is an unusual relationship breakdown.
- [[MT037 - Crafting the Perfect Investment Portfolio in 2025 - Systematic Investor 331#^MT037-C11-X1|MT037-C11-X1]] — Marks's counterargument to the bubble case: the Magnificent 7 have established moats and generate huge cash flows, so their high P/Es may be justified.

## [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve]]
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C02-X1|MT039-C02-X1]] — In early 2025 (by mid-February, when markets priced five US rate cuts) the long-standing US-over-Europe equity pattern reversed, with European equities significantly outperforming, linked to defensives and geopolitics.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C03-4|MT039-C03-4]] — In winter 2024-25 a colder-than-expected season and shallow inventories disrupted natural gas curve trades, prompting discussion of forming spreads more dynamically.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C10-2|MT039-C10-2]] — Alliances and tensions between countries can revert, as the 1807 British bombardment of Copenhagen shows, so current geopolitical ruptures may normalise.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C13-5|MT039-C13-5]] — Credit QIS has been mostly index-based (CDX), which reduces breadth versus equities.
- [[MT039 - Crowded Trades, Fluctuating Markets- Navigating the New Era of Investing - Systematic Inve#^MT039-C14-1|MT039-C14-1]] — Growth of credit QIS faces constraints in liquidity, data validity, history and costs, although more assets keep being indexified.

## [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4]]
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C02-3|MT040-C02-3]] — There are industrial-policy motives: Japan has become the main place the US can get military equipment built in time, so yen policy is about more than currency levels.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C05-3|MT040-C05-3]] — A CFM-associated paper found a degradation of short-term trend performance in some contracts, which has been known for a while.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C09-2|MT040-C09-2]] — Yields near 5% are a normalization to the 1990s-2000s; the 2010s was the anomaly.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C10-1|MT040-C10-1]] — First fracture of the regime-adaptive paper: higher, stickier inflation makes stocks and bonds more correlated (contrasting with the disinflationary Great Moderation era).
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C10-5|MT040-C10-5]] — Third fracture: erosion of institutional norms (pressure on the Fed, active tinkering in bond issuance, fading fiscal conservatism).
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C11-1|MT040-C11-1]] — Both sticky inflation and debt concerns make bonds less reliable diversifiers for equities, with significant asset-allocation implications.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C12-1|MT040-C12-1]] — Stock-bond correlation moved from -0.5 last decade to +0.3 this decade.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C13-3|MT040-C13-3]] — The sticky-inflation regime was interspersed with disinflationary episodes (immaculate disinflation, AI hopes), so it is not constant.
- [[MT040 - Debt, Inflation & Fed Credibility- The Three Market Fractures - Systematic Investor - Ep.4#^MT040-C14-2|MT040-C14-2]] — Within-sector correlations seem lower, partly from deglobalisation and US policy, which is good for trend (anecdotal).

## [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner]]
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C02-1|MT041-C02-1]] — DeepSeek's R1 was a real algorithmic innovation (more experts working in parallel on smaller parameter counts), so the cost gap is not just copying.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C02-2|MT041-C02-2]] — Open weights under an MIT license let anyone fork and run the model in any direction, which spreads the model faster than a restricted license.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C02-3|MT041-C02-3]] — DeepSeek optimised below Nvidia's CUDA layer to the bare metal, like cloud providers stripping software layers when SaaS replaced on-prem, so compute efficiency can come from software rather than more chips.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C02-4|MT041-C02-4]] — DeepSeek app usage is global (share of daily users relative to ChatGPT: ~15% India, ~10% China, ~8% Indonesia), so the rest of the world is up for grabs between US and Chinese models.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C03-1|MT041-C03-1]] — DeepSeek's founder built wealth in quant hedge funds before AI, an analogy to Renaissance applying early machine learning to trading.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C03-2|MT041-C03-2]] — Counting power the total compute cost is near $1B like rival models, but DeepSeek's $6M final training run was 30-50% cheaper than OpenAI o1's $10-15M, so the efficiency gain is real but modest.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C03-3|MT041-C03-3]] — DeepSeek's API price is about one twelfth of OpenAI's, a much bigger gap than the training-cost gap, which may reflect OpenAI margins or DeepSeek subsidy.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C03-X1|MT041-C03-X1]] — Part of DeepSeek's cost advantage is simply being 6-8 months later on a fast cost-decline curve, and its algorithmic gains will be copied, so the efficiency edge is not a durable moat.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C04-2|MT041-C04-2]] — Model quality scales log-linearly with compute, so DeepSeek needs about 10x more compute for the next step, and its gap to OpenAI in compute was at its smallest at R1.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C05-2|MT041-C05-2]] — Cheaper AI inference raises total demand for compute (Jevons paradox), so efficiency gains need not cut chip demand.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C05-3|MT041-C05-3]] — A foreign open-source model beats one proprietary model from a single company on safety, security and free speech because more eyes and less single-party control reduce risk.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C05-4|MT041-C05-4]] — Open weights spawn rapid forks (500 Hugging Face variants in 48 hours, 1300 a week later), so R&D is done by the whole world, not one firm.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C05-5|MT041-C05-5]] — Enterprises prefer commodity open models to proprietary licensed inputs, which is why they and the clouds deployed R1 quickly.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C05-X1|MT041-C05-X1]] — Because DeepSeek published its methods, any breakthrough flows back to US labs (borrowing is bidirectional), so open publication erodes any one lab's lead.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C06-1|MT041-C06-1]] — Open models help inference-optimisation firms (e.g. Fireworks) and non-GPU chip challengers, so everyone except the big proprietary labs benefits.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C06-3|MT041-C06-3]] — China backs open source (Linux donors, RISC-V) because a country with fast, cheap engineers gains from a world with weak IP protection.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C07-2|MT041-C07-2]] — If the US walls off and China leads open source, the rest of the world may standardise on Chinese models.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C07-3|MT041-C07-3]] — The claimed two-year US lead in AI looks wrong after R1, so policy should assume the gap is months.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C07-6|MT041-C07-6]] — The frontier model stays closed (only agents are released) while models one or two generations back get open-sourced.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C08-1|MT041-C08-1]] — Open source approximates pure competition, commoditising the product and cutting prices, which spurs innovation and Jevons-style demand.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C09-2|MT041-C09-2]] — Protection weakens domestic firms (Detroit), and decoupling may leave the US shut out while the world buys cheap Chinese cars and models.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C09-3|MT041-C09-3]] — China held 33% of world GDP when open in 1820 and fell to 5% by the end of Mao's closed period, an example that closing the economy costs share.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C10-1|MT041-C10-1]] — An open model has no nationality; a fork of R1 could win, so the US cannot rely on keeping the winner American.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C12-1|MT041-C12-1]] — High-tariff successes (even McKinley's, about four years) were short-lived, and no economic argument supports walls over the long run.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C12-2|MT041-C12-2]] — Lower-wage workers will outcompete US workers, so US manufacturing succeeded post-war only when Europe and Japan were destroyed.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C12-3|MT041-C12-3]] — Reshoring cheap goods raises consumer prices (as in Europe), so tariffs on low-end goods fail.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C13-1|MT041-C13-1]] — The McKinley tariffs caused strife but plausibly helped industrialise the US and build wartime strength, a counter-argument to free trade.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C13-2|MT041-C13-2]] — A Fed study of 2018 washing-machine tariffs found higher prices (including for dryers sold with them), domestic producers raising prices under the tariff umbrella and few jobs created within two years.
- [[MT041 - DeepSeek, Open Source, Tariffs, DOGE, Market Impact - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Gerstner#^MT041-C19-3|MT041-C19-3]] — Cheaper models and reasoning agents raise inference demand, so compute demand is larger than before despite DeepSeek.

## [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385]]
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C02-2|MT042-C02-2]] — Central banks have been net gold buyers every year since 2011 after being net sellers in 2000-2009, a structural change in gold demand.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C02-5|MT042-C02-5]] — Commodity super cycles start with a change in the monetary system; earlier false starts failed because no such change occurred, but this time may differ.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C02-6|MT042-C02-6]] — Popular CTA replicators hold fewer markets and little commodity exposure, so in a commodity super cycle they could miss the diversification a full CTA offers.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C06-2|MT042-C06-2]] — Last year's regime shifts created fundamental changes that are now maturing into strong trends in 2026.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C11-1|MT042-C11-1]] — CTA style analysis shows the industry became slower and more diversified (carry, other risk premia), so the index return history is not what an allocator gets today.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C13-X1|MT042-C13-X1]] — As deglobalization makes markets behave more independently, the choice of volatility-estimation speed matters more for trend returns.
- [[MT042 - Diversification is more important than ever! - Systematic Investor - Ep.385#^MT042-C14-5|MT042-C14-5]] — After Liberation Day, deglobalization, policy divergence and geopolitical conflict create macro change and trends that favour trend following.

## [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20]]
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C02-2|MT043-C02-2]] — A merchant's fundamental view gave an edge in 2004 when Asia was booming and few vehicles offered commodity exposure.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C04-2|MT043-C04-2]] — Oil broke $50 in 2005 and is near $55 in late 2025 because shale added about 9 million barrels a day in 15 years.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C04-3|MT043-C04-3]] — GMO seeds make grain output resilient to weather, so food supply grows beyond population trend without a land grab, capping grain price spikes.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C04-4|MT043-C04-4]] — Fund is pivoting to electrification metals (copper, aluminium, lithium) because supply and demand are harder to adjust, while other commodities look well supplied.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C04-X1|MT043-C04-X1]] — Regional gas prices can decouple: US Henry Hub stayed quiet while LNG prices rose because the Ukraine war made Europe compete with China for cargoes.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C05-2|MT043-C05-2]] — Trading houses use physical information to trade leveraged derivatives, which produced large profits in 2021-22 distortions.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C09-1|MT043-C09-1]] — The 2014 bearish oil view combined shale supply growth taking OPEC share with a global GDP slowdown (1-2% not 3-4%), expecting inverse to contango.
- [[MT043 - Doug King on Commodities, Risk Control & the Illusion of Trends - Open Interest - Ep.20#^MT043-C10-1|MT043-C10-1]] — A physical trading arm fed information into the hedge fund (like Cargill/Black River, Trafigura/Galena) but proved hard to run and was disbanded.

## [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor]]
- [[MT044 - Drawdowns, Diversification, and the Hidden Risks in Today’s Markets - Systematic Investor#^MT044-C18-2|MT044-C18-2]] — Bond carry and secular decline in rates, plus rising equity valuations, give a tailwind to momentum that is stronger in bonds.

## [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356]]
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C07-4|MT045-C07-4]] — Trump 1.0 tweets did not create much trend, but Trump 2.0 coincides with larger global change in geopolitics and supply chains.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C08-3|MT045-C08-3]] — Trend recoveries often follow a real narrative change in a broad range of markets, such as rates rising after the post-GFC period.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C11-4|MT045-C11-4]] — Endogenous shocks (policy, inflation) tend to help trend because they are fundamental valuation shifts.
- [[MT045 - Drawdowns, Recovery, and Replication - Systematic Investor - Ep.356#^MT045-C16-1|MT045-C16-1]] — Replicators miss markets outside their set (cocoa), so differences reflect market regimes not luck.

## [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio]]
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C01-X1|MT046-C01-X1]] — Demographic shifts (a 1950s baby boom followed by sub-replacement birth rates) are public and can be mapped decades ahead, so their fiscal impact in the 2020s was forecastable from the 1990s.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C03-5|MT046-C03-5]] — Commercial real estate, especially office, is a problem because remote-work lifestyle change coincides with higher financing rates.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C04-X2|MT046-C04-X2]] — Even if AI is a real secular theme, many AI-linked companies will fail regardless of the economic cycle because winners and losers have not yet been sorted, so stock selection within the theme matters.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C05-2|MT046-C05-2]] — AI is a real secular theme comparable to the Internet, unlike crypto, so AI leaders belong in the long book.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C05-7|MT046-C05-7]] — AI is making top coders 7-8x more productive, which supports the thesis that AI demand is real and durable.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C06-1|MT046-C06-1]] — A stagnating Chinese economy under Xi raises the risk of military action, as leaders divert attention from domestic problems.
- [[MT046 - Druckenmiller on How AI is Dominating His Long Portfolio#^MT046-C06-3|MT046-C06-3]] — Japan had been a value trap for decades but in 2023 market breadth was strong because deflation seemed to be ending (nominal growth), a shareholder-value push, and a central bank behind the curve on inflation.

## [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340]]
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C04-2|MT047-C04-2]] — ETF wrappers allow almost any strategy to be packaged, including trend following, raising concern about financial engineering.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C06-2|MT047-C06-2]] — The Bretton Woods design makes the dollar the core reserve asset, so the US must supply dollars through current account deficits, leaving foreigners with large claims (a Triffin-type flaw).
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C06-4|MT047-C06-4]] — Per Adam Rozencwajg, commodity super cycles often coincide with a change in the monetary system, so a shift in the dollar regime would support commodities.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C09-1|MT047-C09-1]] — Index composition changes dramatically over decades (railways about 60% in 1900, energy about 30% in the 1970s, financials 25-30% in 2007), so today's dominance of technology is unlikely to persist for 20 years.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C09-2|MT047-C09-2]] — The US index has become less cyclical as financials and energy shrank and technology grew, so cross-period valuation comparisons need adjustment.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C11-5|MT047-C11-5]] — Stock-bond correlation was persistently positive until the 1990s, turned negative from the late 1990s to the 2000s, an outlier period, and is positive again.
