- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C11-6|MT047-C11-6]] — US inflation averaged 2.9% over 129 years vs 2.1% for Switzerland, so a 3% base rate is a better five-year forecast than the Fed's 2% target.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C14-1|MT047-C14-1]] — Commodities respond to idiosyncratic supply-demand drivers, so trend following in commodities has lower correlation to financial markets and is the main diversifier in a trend portfolio.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C15-1|MT047-C15-1]] — Average correlation is lower when small markets such as cocoa and zinc are included, versus energy contracts that are derivatives of the same market, explaining the Sharpe gain from breadth.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C15-3|MT047-C15-3]] — Trend-following ETFs trade only a fraction of markets, with structural drag up to 4% per year before fees (measured on commodities), which can outweigh their low-fee advantage.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C16-5|MT047-C16-5]] — Liquid commodities (oil, gold, copper) are most sensitive to economic conditions, so crisis alpha may come from them rather than from illiquid softs; this is unverified.
- [[MT047 - ETFs, Size, and the Commodity Problem - Systematic Investor 340#^MT047-C17-1|MT047-C17-1]] — A smaller set of diversifying markets can beat a larger set of correlated ones, so how risk is allocated matters more than market count.

## [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge]]
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C01-2|MT048-C01-2]] — Public market valuations and appetite set exit prices for private companies, so venture investors must follow public markets.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C07-4|MT048-C07-4]] — The Fed is not a thermostat; many other variables (debt, isolationism and de-globalisation inflation, deficits) determine markets, and markets were once addicted to rate cuts as bailouts.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C10-3|MT048-C10-3]] — Nvidia's data-centre growth is mostly supply built by cloud providers and not end-user demand; real demand is the cloud customers' usage.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C10-4|MT048-C10-4]] — Unlike past tech shifts, incumbents (Microsoft, Google, Meta) are moving fast in AI, rewarded by the market, so they will keep spending.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C11-2|MT048-C11-2]] — AI startup take-under deals (Inflection, Character) signal that independents cannot fund the compute race against incumbents with huge cash flows.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C11-4|MT048-C11-4]] — Non-LLM applied AI, such as Tesla full self-driving, has clear value, while language-based customer service and search products are less proven.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C16-1|MT048-C16-1]] — The Google payment is a major income stream for Apple (about $26bn), and no competitor such as Bing can replace it, so the deal is win-win not a block.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C17-1|MT048-C17-1]] — Gurley proposes a monopoly test: whether a firm can impose one-sided terms no other partner could, which would extend its monopoly under Sherman section 2.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C17-2|MT048-C17-2]] — Brad expects consumer choice remedy with Google keeping 90% of searches unless AI products such as Perplexity and SearchGPT disrupt.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C17-3|MT048-C17-3]] — Perplexity and SearchGPT run a search first and feed results into the LLM context (RAG), solving the freshness problem of models and explaining why they show links.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C18-1|MT048-C18-1]] — Weekly-to-monthly active user ratio for ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini is about 40% versus over 80% for Spotify, Instagram and WhatsApp, and apps with over 80% engagement reach billions of users while low-engagement apps cap at tens or 100 million.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C18-2|MT048-C18-2]] — One-month retention is about 65% for ChatGPT vs over 90% for WhatsApp, Instagram and Chrome, and 75% for Gmail, so gen-AI apps retain worse (Gurley notes paid and unpaid are mixed).
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C18-3|MT048-C18-3]] — Because Google copied AI search quickly and engagement is flat, AI chat search is not the product that reaches a billion users.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C19-1|MT048-C19-1]] — The next breakout AI moment is likely a personalised agent with memory that can act, not AI-enabled search.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C19-2|MT048-C19-2]] — If free AI search with fewer ads becomes competitive, the disruptor earns less, but incumbents' margin is also pressured; and 12-month retention of about 35% is low.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C19-3|MT048-C19-3]] — Google has the best asset set for a personal assistant (Gmail, docs, Android, workspace) if it integrates them, putting it at an advantage in agents.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C20-1|MT048-C20-1]] — Google's main risk in AI is execution, and the current consumer AI product is not enough to dislodge Google search.
- [[MT048 - Ep14. Public Market Volatility, AI Air Pocket, $GOOG Ruling - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ge#^MT048-C20-2|MT048-C20-2]] — Independent model builders face a compute-capex burden, while Google, Microsoft and Meta cannot acquire due to regulation but can pay for talent, leading to licence-and-hire take-under deals.

## [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C06-2|MT049-C06-2]] — A strong dollar hurts US exporters like Caterpillar because foreign competitors can undercut them, causing layoffs.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C06-3|MT049-C06-3]] — Oil-dependent regions (Tulsa) boom or bust with oil prices regardless of individual performance, so local economies are whipped by commodity prices.
- [[MT049 - Episode 12- Bob Prince - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Associates#^MT049-C11-2|MT049-C11-2]] — Globalisation created imbalances that produced leaders favouring government involvement, an inward focus and competition instead of cooperation (modern mercantilism, led by China, then Biden and Trump), a philosophical change that will last.

## [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting]]
- [[MT050 - Episode 15- Felix Zulauf - Founder of Zulauf Consulting#^MT050-C05-7|MT050-C05-7]] — The Trump trade changes are a regime break comparable to the Berlin Wall, Bretton Woods and the gold standard, ending the globalisation wave of 30 years and creating a more difficult environment for companies.

## [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management]]
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C02-1|MT051-C02-1]] — Obscure, inefficient niches (e.g. convertible bonds then) let hard work and skill earn an edge, whereas large-cap equities offer almost no such edge because they are widely covered.
- [[MT051 - Episode 17- Howard Marks - Co-Chairman of Oaktree Capital Management#^MT051-C05-X1|MT051-C05-X1]] — Markets have become more efficient since the Nifty Fifty era: price-agnostic buying of quality is rarer and data is widely shared, so edges from simple data or quality screens have shrunk.

## [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York]]
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C03-1|MT052-C03-1]] — From the 1934 Reciprocal Trade Agreements Act through GATT rounds, US trade policy was rule-bound and tariffs fell gradually to very low levels by 2016; a rule-violating policy was not even considered, so the pre-2016 regime was stable and the current one is a break.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C04-4|MT052-C04-4]] — Finance has grown about three points of GDP and shaped the whole economy and policy, in contrast to the boring and tightly controlled banking system of the 1970s.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C05-1|MT052-C05-1]] — Financial innovation such as subprime lending and CDOs mostly brought costs and paved the way to the financial crisis; he cites Samuelson that the ATM was the only clear benefit.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C05-2|MT052-C05-2]] — Extreme wealth concentration, visible in campaign finance after Citizens United, influences legislation and threatens democracy.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C06-1|MT052-C06-1]] — Populism is not explained only by economics: left-behind blue-collar workers and stranded regions contribute, but strong nationalist right movements also arise in Denmark with a generous safety net.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C07-3|MT052-C07-3]] — A fall in US credibility opens opportunities for China (which looks more credible than the US despite autocratic contract risk) and gives big developing countries such as Brazil somewhat more independence.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C08-1|MT052-C08-1]] — The college wage premium rose in the 1980s-90s and has been flat since, so the value of formal education is ambiguous.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C08-2|MT052-C08-2]] — The economic impact of AI is unknown: it could produce a sustained productivity boom or job displacement, which he doubts.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C08-3|MT052-C08-3]] — Zero or negative immigration would be a real blow to future living standards of native-born Americans.
- [[MT052 - Episode 28- Paul Krugman - Professor of Economics at The City University of New York#^MT052-C08-X1|MT052-C08-X1]] — Only the US, China and the EU are economic superpowers; the EU remains a massive economic force despite disappointments but is less politically cohesive (though more so than a few years ago), and no other country is big enough to replace a less credible US.

## [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates#^MT053-C02-3|MT053-C02-3]] — Good hedge funds exploit niches that are inefficient for structural reasons, but the returns and capacity there are small.
- [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates#^MT053-C02-X1|MT053-C02-X1]] — Market efficiency is not uniformly rising: some markets have become more efficient, others less, and inefficiency migrates from one area to another over time, so where edges exist must be reassessed.
- [[MT053 - Episode 4- Noah Yechiely - Former Head of Equities at Bridgewater Associates#^MT053-C03-2|MT053-C03-2]] — Data and analysis tools once available to a few are now public and cheap, so data- and technology-dependent edges in markets have become more efficient.

## [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund]]
- [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund#^MT054-C04-2|MT054-C04-2]] — The Quantum approach was a global multi-asset long/short portfolio across stocks, bonds, currencies and commodities, with commodities seen as important inputs (for example copper for power plants).
- [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund#^MT054-C05-1|MT054-C05-1]] — Everything in the world (commodity prices of copper and wheat, technology) affects investments, so a successful investor must know as much as possible about global developments.
- [[MT054 - Episode 5- Jim Rogers - Legendary Investor & Co-Founder of Quantum Fund#^MT054-C08-X1|MT054-C08-X1]] — Investment success comes from identifying structural change early (which countries and sectors become important, e.g. Australia, Uzbekistan, agriculture) and positioning for it, so the investable universe must keep being updated.

## [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann]]
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C01-2|MT055-C01-2]] — The real risk to US solvency is a collapse in wealth (e.g. from $230 trillion to $100 trillion, where it was 10-15 years ago) caused by bad policy; as long as the growth engine of business creation continues there is no need to worry about debt.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C02-1|MT055-C02-1]] — Regulation that blocks business start-ups and very high taxes that stop capital raising would sharply cut growth; the US is 50% richer than Europe with similar population, so modest policy changes could make it much poorer.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C02-2|MT055-C02-2]] — Entrepreneurs capture only about 10% of the value they create (Bezos about 2% of Amazon's value once consumer surplus of $10 trillion is counted) and the rest goes to consumers as consumer surplus.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C03-1|MT055-C03-1]] — Because most benefit goes to consumers, taxing and vilifying entrepreneurs is counterproductive; the zero-sum 'one big pizza pie' view of wealth is a misconception.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C04-1|MT055-C04-1]] — Billionaires do pay tax: the company pays corporate tax on shareholders' behalf (20% of $30 billion is $6 billion for Bezos), and unrealised gains build a future tax liability, so claims that they pay zero are factually wrong.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C05-1|MT055-C05-1]] — School vouchers would give education at about half the price, since New York spends $43,000 per pupil while the best Catholic schools cost less than half; competition would improve results and reduce waste and fraud.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C06-1|MT055-C06-1]] — Welfare should be delivered by vouchers (as food stamps) for schools and health care instead of government-run systems, a far more efficient welfare state.
- [[MT055 - Episode 55- Economic Myths with Jeff Yass - Co-Founder and Managing Director of Susquehann#^MT055-C06-X1|MT055-C06-X1]] — Constitutional limits on government powers (9th and 10th Amendments) kept US government small and less corrupt, which he credits for American prosperity; he concedes a return to this has zero chance.

## [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395]]
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C02-1|MT056-C02-1]] — Earlier crises (GFC, pandemic) were fixed by Fed or fiscal action, but this ideology-driven geopolitical crisis cannot be, so it is a different regime that will persist.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C03-1|MT056-C03-1]] — AI tools that cost hundreds of billions to build are cheap to use, giving asset managers large efficiency gains in programming and trade reconciliation rather than in the investment strategy itself.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C08-1|MT056-C08-1]] — The CTA product universe (hedge funds, then mutual funds and UCITS, then ETFs) falls in complexity, with ETFs simplified to plain trend with fewer instruments.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C09-3|MT056-C09-3]] — Most dollars made in CTAs come from a handful of big cross-asset trades, not from many orthogonal positions, so replication focuses on clusters and cross-asset relationships.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C10-1|MT056-C10-1]] — Replication still needs complex underlying managers to produce the returns it replicates; complexity must exist somewhere even if the investor does not pay for it.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C14-2|MT056-C14-2]] — Active ETFs are now 31% of the US mutual fund plus ETF business as mutual funds shrink, supporting wrapper migration.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C14-X1|MT056-C14-X1]] — A Goldman Sachs paper shows fund flows going heavily to passive and away from active managers, though Beer notes rising markets over the period and that active assets in dollars remain larger.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C15-3|MT056-C15-3]] — Bank QIS products took off in 2012-13 after hedge funds embarrassed themselves in the GFC; allocators wanted cheaper, more liquid exposure.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C17-3|MT056-C17-3]] — Swap-based QIS trend products report no fees and can be layered as portable alpha, making them a bigger competitor to hedge funds than replication is.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C18-1|MT056-C18-1]] — A swap-based ETF on an index can be 35 bp reported and more tax efficient, because derivatives allow a cheaper and tax-advantaged structure.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C20-1|MT056-C20-1]] — Tax efficiency (futures held in the entity, not Cayman subs; swap structure) could save about 150 bp a year in after-tax returns.
- [[MT056 - Finding Alpha in the Strait of Chaos ft. Andrew Beer - Systematic Investor - Ep.395#^MT056-C20-2|MT056-C20-2]] — Model-portfolio allocators with 25 bp expense ratios need a low-cost, tax-efficient, ETF-style trend allocation sized at 3-5%.

## [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300]]
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C11-1|MT057-C11-1]] — To avoid competing with AI, favor medium-to-long-term trends, since the short-term space will become much more competitive as AI improves.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C11-3|MT057-C11-3]] — Technology advances repeatedly predicted the end of trend following, yet core rules have worked for 50 years; AI is a helpful tool, not a revolution.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C12-1|MT057-C12-1]] — Medallion operates on one-day to one-week horizons using many data sources and Markov models with a top-level allocator by market state.
- [[MT057 - Finding the Optimal Trend Following Rules - Systematic Investor 300#^MT057-C13-2|MT057-C13-2]] — AI will quickly take over short-term prediction, but CTAs have the advantage in medium-to-long-term trends; mean reversion and HFT are more at risk.

## [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates]]
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C07-X1|MT058-C07-X1]] — China's rise since 1984 (per-capita income up 28 times, life expectancy up 10 years, poverty from 88% to under 1%) is the economic base of a rising power that now challenges the existing world order, which in history leads to conflict.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C08-1|MT058-C08-1]] — Like Japan before WWII (oil embargo, frozen assets), the US now uses chip embargoes and possible debt sanctions on China as economic war, as part of the great-power conflict.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C08-2|MT058-C08-2]] — Technology leadership (chips) determines who wins the broader world order contest.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C08-3|MT058-C08-3]] — In conflict periods countries move toward autocracy and force internal alignment, as in WWII.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C09-1|MT058-C09-1]] — Economic interdependence (22% of US manufactured imports from China) makes a full economic or military break extremely costly, which restrains both sides.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C10-4|MT058-C10-4]] — Dalio favors neutral, rising places such as ASEAN/Singapore, India and Gulf states (UAE, Saudi) while diversifying asset classes.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C11-X1|MT058-C11-X1]] — During the energy transition, underinvestment in old energy sources cuts supply before new sources replace them, making the transition costly (supply squeeze) on top of infrastructure and damage costs.
- [[MT058 - Five Forces Shaping the Global Economy - With Ray Dalio, Founder of Bridgewater Associates#^MT058-C12-1|MT058-C12-1]] — AI raises productivity but disrupts who gains: inventors win and those replacing labor (robotics plus AI) shift wealth, which feeds political conflict.

## [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming]]
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C01-1|MT059-C01-1]] — Power trading is a market built on both economics and physical grid constraints, a partly artificial construct with no perfect market design, which creates opportunities because few understand the details.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C01-X1|MT059-C01-X1]] — Traded US power markets only date from late-1990s/early-2000s deregulation (alongside gas), so market designs are still evolving and usable price histories are short.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C04-1|MT059-C04-1]] — Historical analysis of 8-10 year old power data gives recency-biased insight because grid topology and price formation change dramatically; trades built on it are ones to fade.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C04-2|MT059-C04-2]] — When gas replaced coal, power flows built for coal plants reversed and shifted, producing downstream price effects the market did not predict.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C04-4|MT059-C04-4]] — After years of flat or falling power demand (efficiency, offshoring), demand is rising due to data centers, industrialization, EVs and heat pumps, a new paradigm for power prices.
- [[MT059 - GBE Founder Cory Paddock- Great Traders Know When a Regime Change Is Coming#^MT059-C05-1|MT059-C05-1]] — Both demand volatility and supply volatility (wind and solar penetration) are rising, a new paradigm in which the edge is to see changes early and pivot quickly.

## [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C05-1|MT060-C05-1]] — AI may follow prior technological revolutions, with short-term job disruption and displacement followed by new job creation in the longer term.
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C09-1|MT060-C09-1]] — As a classic liberal economist, Garrett views the shrinking of global markets (more transactional, competing countries) as a major economic net negative.
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C10-1|MT060-C10-1]] — Reshoring and tariffs carry a real cost in higher prices, and since globalization lowered prices and interest rates for decades, reversing it pushes both up.
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C10-2|MT060-C10-2]] — Following Kant, closer economic ties lower the probability of war between countries, so decoupling raises geopolitical risk.
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C10-4|MT060-C10-4]] — The rise of Chinese EV makers (BYD, Xiaomi) is a transformative force for the global economy, and Europe and the US must decide whether to protect domestic automakers with tariffs.
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C11-2|MT060-C11-2]] — EVs are simpler to assemble, so a shift to EVs cuts manufacturing and auto employment, which affects political reactions in Europe and the US.
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C11-3|MT060-C11-3]] — China dominates the green energy supply chain, which is power-intensive and dirty, and metals such as Indonesian nickel are needed for long-range EVs.
- [[MT060 - Geoffrey Garrett - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT060-C11-X1|MT060-C11-X1]] — Garrett sees a possible phase-out of the internal combustion engine, the defining technology of the 20th century, within about two decades, a structural shift for autos, oil demand and metals supply chains.

## [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit]]
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C04-5|MT061-C04-5]] — Japan (asset bubble, flat GDP per capita, QE, high debt/GDP, stock buying) may be a preview of Western economies.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C05-3|MT061-C05-3]] — Printing and deficits inflate asset prices faster than wages, which prices young people out of housing, delaying marriage and fertility and bending demographic curves down.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C05-4|MT061-C05-4]] — Chinese home prices reached 26x median income, and Xi has not stimulated the property market because financial security is national security.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C05-5|MT061-C05-5]] — Official chain-weighted inflation understates the rise in asset and housing prices, so real wages are worse than reported.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C05-X1|MT061-C05-X1]] — Ageing populations with no native population growth (Japan, Italy) cannot grow their way out of sovereign debt, which is why they rely on low rates and printing rather than growth.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C06-1|MT061-C06-1]] — Real US house prices were flat from the late 1800s to the 1970s-80s and rose steeply afterward, so the affordability problem is recent.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C06-2|MT061-C06-2]] — The FHFA unchained house price index rose 50% from 2020 to 2024, far above wage growth.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C06-3|MT061-C06-3]] — Asset inflation hurts the poor through food, rent and gas and immobilises the middle class, widening the gap and raising social tension.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C06-4|MT061-C06-4]] — He expects more wars as inflation, asset-price gaps and falling fertility tear at social fabric while central banks keep growing balance sheets.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C07-2|MT061-C07-2]] — Understanding China's system, history and incentives shows its grand strategy is incompatible with the US, so conflict is likely.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C07-3|MT061-C07-3]] — Annual growth in Chinese air-defense-zone incursions into Taiwan is a chartable signal that an invasion is approaching.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C07-5|MT061-C07-5]] — He expects a Chinese move on Taiwan that permanently reshapes West-China economic ties.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C07-6|MT061-C07-6]] — Israel's strike on Iran's nuclear sites was predictable because Iran would not accept inspection of new centrifuges.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C08-1|MT061-C08-1]] — China's current account was heading toward zero in late 2019 amid the Hong Kong crisis; COVID travel and tuition restrictions swung it by about $250bn, easing the external constraint.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C08-2|MT061-C08-2]] — He sees three routes to Taiwan: soft (KMT election win in 2028), blockade, or all-out assault; a blockade would force US action.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C08-3|MT061-C08-3]] — Taiwan has about two weeks of energy on island, so a blockade of its ports would be effective and requires US unblocking, leading to kinetic conflict.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C09-1|MT061-C09-1]] — Investors in China since the BRIC era 2000s-2010s would have roughly flat returns today.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C09-2|MT061-C09-2]] — The US military has plans for blockade and assault scenarios but not for a soft power takeover of Taiwan.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C10-1|MT061-C10-1]] — Taiwan shows what open capital and Western institutions could do for China, which is why it threatens Xi.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C10-2|MT061-C10-2]] — Chinese economic statecraft (IP theft estimated at $300bn a year, VC infiltration) makes China an adversary rather than a trading partner.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C11-1|MT061-C11-1]] — He expects a Chinese move on Taiwan within two to three years with direct US involvement and possible strikes on Fujian installations.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C11-3|MT061-C11-3]] — China exposure was a good bet when westernisation was expected to lift productivity, but turned bad when that assumption failed.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C12-3|MT061-C12-3]] — China, Russia, Iran and North Korea are cooperating as an authoritarian axis this time.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C14-1|MT061-C14-1]] — The US (4% of population, 26% of GDP, 60% of world capital markets) is the best place for long-term capital because of its deep liquid markets and entrepreneurship.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C14-2|MT061-C14-2]] — US outperformance and flat GDP per capita elsewhere is driven largely by tech, and EU regulation holds European tech back.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C14-4|MT061-C14-4]] — Europe's regulation (GDPR) and repeated self-inflicted policy errors make it a poor place to deploy capital.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C15-1|MT061-C15-1]] — An investor in the CSI 300 18 years ago lost a third of capital while China's GDP grew 505%, so GDP growth in a state-controlled economy does not accrue to foreign shareholders.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C15-4|MT061-C15-4]] — Repeated forecasts that the USSR, Japan or China would overtake the US failed, so betting against the US has been a bad strategy.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C15-X1|MT061-C15-X1]] — Europe's weakness is policy-made, so a turn to deregulation (dropping GDPR), pro-entrepreneur policy and tax incentives for relocating firms is the condition under which his negative view on Europe would change.
- [[MT061 - Global Macro Investing And Geoeconomics With Hedge Fund Investor Kyle Bass - Hoover Instit#^MT061-C15-X2|MT061-C15-X2]] — Within Europe the investable strength is branded luxury (LVMH) selling to the world's rich, while growth and innovation sit in the US.

## [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin]]
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C02-X1|MT062-C02-X1]] — Part of the early-2025 wall of worry is a fear that China exports deflation, which together with tariffs and a strong dollar adds to cross-market volatility that macro traders can use.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C04-4|MT062-C04-4]] — Over 50% of CFM models now use large unstructured alt data (ingesting about five terabytes a day), which few firms can exploit.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C04-X1|MT062-C04-X1]] — Data volume is growing exponentially (a quoted figure, which the speaker himself says is probably incorrect), so the edge goes to firms with the technology stack and experience to process unstructured data at scale.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-4|MT062-C07-4]] — LLMs are useful for classification and compressing unstructured data into sentiment signals and for coding.
- [[MT062 - Global Macro- Discretionary vs. Systematic - Who Wins- - Global Alts 2025 - Pablo Calderin#^MT062-C07-X1|MT062-C07-X1]] — Classical machine learning has been used in systematic investing for about 20 years, so the new element is the large language model, which is the genuinely disruptive tool because it handles unstructured text.

## [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324]]
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C09-1|MT063-C09-1]] — Outliers are transition events between regimes: an abrupt break, then a new regime after the outlier settles.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C15-2|MT063-C15-2]] — World fertility fell from over five children per woman in the 1960s to 2.3, with South Korea very low, Australia 1.5 and Japan 1.4.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C15-3|MT063-C15-3]] — Citing the WHO, he says infertility will be a leading disease and 97% of countries will face severe population shortages by 2100.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C15-4|MT063-C15-4]] — Housing, education and health care costs discourage large families, as seen in South Korea's high house prices and record-low fertility.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C15-5|MT063-C15-5]] — Microplastics act as endocrine disruptors and lower fertility.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C15-6|MT063-C15-6]] — Obesity causes hormonal imbalances and pregnancy complications, lowering fertility; cultural shifts to career and delayed family planning also contribute (Japan about 1.4).
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C15-X1|MT063-C15-X1]] — As baby boomers age while birth rates fall, a shrinking younger cohort must support a growing older population, which he expects to cause large economic problems.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C16-2|MT063-C16-2]] — Feedback loops such as microplastics lowering fertility or rising living costs encouraging smaller families self-regulate population and economy sizes.
- [[MT063 - Global Market Risks, Fertility Crisis & Navigating Uncertainty - Systematic Investor 324#^MT063-C16-3|MT063-C16-3]] — Falling fertility brings economic stagnation, ageing and social instability, which must be handled as a complex adaptive system.

## [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD]]
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C02-4|MT064-C02-4]] — Bloomberg estimates multi-manager pass-through fund clients effectively pay 7 and 20 up to 15 and 20 once pass-through expenses are counted, versus a 2 and 20 benchmark.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C02-X1|MT064-C02-X1]] — Institutional pressure pushed hedge fund fees down from 2 and 20, but multi-strategy pass-through funds that charge expenses on top now make 2 and 20 look cheap by comparison.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C03-1|MT064-C03-1]] — A multi-strategy pass-through fund earned about 15.2% before fees in 2023 but the client received only 2.8% (caption ambiguous), illustrating large fee drag.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C03-2|MT064-C03-2]] — Multi-strategy pod funds charge expenses to client accounts outside headline fees, so institutions comparing headline fees underestimate costs; transparency has gone backwards compared with retail UCITS.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C03-3|MT064-C03-3]] — In fund-of-funds and pod models, winning managers are paid performance fees even when the overall portfolio loses, ratcheting up costs.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C08-3|MT064-C08-3]] — An ETF wrapper usually costs more than futures: fees, provider profit and higher slippage from size.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C09-2|MT064-C09-2]] — ETFs are useful for market access and small contract size (fractional shares) when capital is limited, but with enough capital futures are better.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C11-2|MT064-C11-2]] — Alternative data is a growth area for research.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C21-4|MT064-C21-4]] — Supply chains are fragile, so deportations and tariffs may cause shortages and inflation.
- [[MT064 - Gold, Futures Pricing & Market Trends - Systematic Investor 335 REUPLOAD#^MT064-C23-2|MT064-C23-2]] — Erosion of trust in institutions over decades makes a disconnect between value and price.

## [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar]]
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C01-1|MT065-C01-1]] — The 2022 downturn is unusual: it is triggered by supply-side inflation while labor markets stay very strong, with many economies already below trend or near recession.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C02-3|MT065-C02-3]] — Inflation comes from both strong post-pandemic demand and supply shocks (energy, labor, mismatched supply), the latter not seen since the 1970s.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C02-X1|MT065-C02-X1]] — Inflation acts as a cumulative tax on savings and purchasing power, so persistent inflation erodes the real value of financial assets year after year.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C02-X2|MT065-C02-X2]] — Cumulative UK inflation since 2000 means 1.87 pounds are needed in 2022 to buy what one pound bought in 2000, an illustration of how inflation compounds.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C06-6|MT065-C06-6]] — Growth versus value labels are no longer useful as each has split into sub-groups.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C07-4|MT065-C07-4]] — Without falling rates there is less valuation upside, lower index returns and more idiosyncratic returns, so stock selection and alpha matter more.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C08-3|MT065-C08-3]] — The FTSE 100 is not a UK-economy proxy: about 75% of revenues are foreign and about 50% of dividends are in dollars, so weak sterling helps it.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C09-3|MT065-C09-3]] — European gas prices after the Ukraine war pushed German inflation above 10%, forcing the ECB to hike despite weak growth.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C09-5|MT065-C09-5]] — Europe's energy transition gains urgency, building renewable infrastructure that can later generate jobs and growth, though costly.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C10-3|MT065-C10-3]] — Electrification for net zero requires much more copper, supporting prices.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C10-4|MT065-C10-4]] — A higher cost of capital after the artificial post-GFC low-rate era means lower returns.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C10-5|MT065-C10-5]] — Geopolitics and decarbonization mean less globalization and more regional production of food, chips and batteries.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C11-1|MT065-C11-1]] — Higher energy and labor costs push firms to invest in efficiency, automation and AI, which could lift productivity.
- [[MT065 - Goldman Sachs’s Chief Global Equity Strategist, Peter Oppenheimer, Discusses Macro and Mar#^MT065-C11-X1|MT065-C11-X1]] — The digital-revolution era came at the cost of physical infrastructure, which has deteriorated, so the coming era shifts investment from the virtual to the physical (defense, decarbonisation, retooling), a structural change in what capital is spent on.

## [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It]]
- [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It#^MT066-C02-4|MT066-C02-4]] — Supply-chain and energy-price driven inflation is outside Fed control, so rate hikes cannot fully remove it.
- [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It#^MT066-C03-3|MT066-C03-3]] — European inflation is very high and supply-side driven by power costs, while US inflation is partly supply and partly labour-driven, which widens central bank divergence.
- [[MT066 - Graham Capital Management's Tropin on Why Macro is Killing It#^MT066-C05-4|MT066-C05-4]] — Underinvestment in conventional energy during the green transition plus OPEC supply cuts keeps energy (crude in the 90s) and headline inflation high, especially in Europe.

## [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel]]
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C01-X1|MT067-C01-X1]] — The past decade rewarded passive stock and bond beta, but the coming years look much less constructive for traditional investments and better for macro because volatility is expected across FX, rates, equities and commodities.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C03-4|MT067-C03-4]] — The negative stock-bond correlation underpinning 60/40 portfolios has gone in the inflation regime, so diversification between them fails.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C03-5|MT067-C03-5]] — Secular changes - deglobalisation and labour regaining power vs capital - support structurally higher inflation.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C04-1|MT067-C04-1]] — Risk parity worked for decades on falling rates and QE tamped volatility, peaking when $7 trillion of debt was negative-yielding and equity valuations at highs; it has since been thrown out.
- [[MT067 - Graham's 2022 Virtual Investment Symposium- Discretionary Strategies Panel#^MT067-C07-3|MT067-C07-3]] — Key variables: European winter weather, China zero-covid policy after party congress.

## [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem]]
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C01-1|MT068-C01-1]] — Three macro themes: a shift to modern mercantilism (reaction to China's rise), the AI technology revolution, and record concentration of capital in US equities and illiquid assets.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C01-3|MT068-C01-3]] — Regime shift analogy: the 1980s turn to small government and free markets is now being replaced by mercantilism (tariffs, state control, trade as zero-sum), so investors must adapt rather than assume the old economy.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C04-1|MT068-C04-1]] — AI capex is driven by strategic, non-profit motives of firms whose businesses are threatened, so spending persists beyond normal ROI discipline.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C05-1|MT068-C05-1]] — Vendor financing is not bubble-style demand creation but Nvidia building a controlled ecosystem versus Google's full stack, with scarce chips as leverage.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C05-2|MT068-C05-2]] — Cutting-edge AI scientists number under a thousand, making talent the binding constraint; poaching slows scientific progress.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C06-1|MT068-C06-1]] — Mercantilist shift: US no longer pursues international cooperation as before; the rest of the world has not retaliated against tariffs (picked off one by one), but is showing home bias and domestic investment.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C07-1|MT068-C07-1]] — Collapse in US goodwill drives populist, mercantilist politics (shift to populist right then left), with less international cooperation.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C07-2|MT068-C07-2]] — Mercantilism is inflationary: tariffs and resilient supply chains replace cheapest sourcing, reversing 30 years of productivity gains.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C07-4|MT068-C07-4]] — AI investment gives growth with low labour intensity, so growth is concentrated while the labour market is weak and the effect is initially disinflationary; AI adds ~1pt of 2% US growth.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C08-X1|MT068-C08-X1]] — AI investment follows a J-curve: in the first phase it absorbs large resources with little output beyond the investment itself, and only later becomes strongly disinflationary, so the disinflation is further out than many assume.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C08-X2|MT068-C08-X2]] — Forecast: US growth next year a little above potential at about 2.5%, with roughly 1.5 points from AI investment, producing a two-track economy of a concentrated AI boom alongside a weak labour market.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C09-X2|MT068-C09-X2]] — International institutions (e.g. WTO) have lost their function and US domestic institutions are weakening fast, part of why the US is becoming a less exceptional, higher-risk-premium place to invest.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C10-2|MT068-C10-2]] — China matters for diversification; its leadership moved to support the private sector and equity market to compete on AI; main risk is political access.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C10-3|MT068-C10-3]] — Bonds are no longer the 30-year diversifier because fiscal limits shift correlations, and fiscal policy responds to disinflation, so bonds do not hedge deflation as well.
- [[MT068 - Greg Jensen - Co-CIO of Bridgewater Podcast In Good Company Norges Bank Investment Managem#^MT068-C14-2|MT068-C14-2]] — AI adoption works when processes are redesigned around the technology rather than replicating human tasks (Amazon robots example).

## [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate]]
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C03-2|MT069-C03-2]] — Massive disruption ahead makes macro opportunity rich for 3-4 years after a dead decade.
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C03-4|MT069-C03-4]] — Long copper: tight supply for years plus demand from AI data centres; trade is consensus; owns front-end exposure rather than miners.
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C03-X1|MT069-C03-X1]] — Positioning view: after three years of an AI-driven book, he would now hold a more eclectic equity basket, with AI only in small amounts and large positions in Japan and Korea.
- [[MT069 - Hard Lessons- Stan Druckenmiller- Invest, then investigate#^MT069-C04-2|MT069-C04-2]] — Talent flow signal: where bright young people go (Stanford students shifting from crypto to AI; Palantir in 2008-09) indicates big themes.

## [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns]]
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C02-2|MT070-C02-2]] — Markets are an evolving ecosystem, not a perfectly efficient system; edges decay but new ones emerge, so innovation is needed.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C03-2|MT070-C03-2]] — Quant investing has a bigger future because markets will not become efficient; competition is fierce but spurs progress and finance is not a natural monopoly.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C04-4|MT070-C04-4]] — Futures trend-following systems applied unchanged to equities fail; equity strategies need event, organizational/governance metrics and customised technical strategies across 7000 stocks.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C09-3|MT070-C09-3]] — Active managers without real edge face pressure from data and performance evaluation; many discretionary strategies are simpler than thought and can be automated, as trend following was.
- [[MT070 - Harding Rewrites Winton's Quant Playbook in Search for Returns#^MT070-C12-1|MT070-C12-1]] — No IP protection and no publication in quant finance means advances are kept secret and knowledge progresses slowly; he expects the word quant to disappear as everyone uses computers.

## [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408]]
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C02-4|MT071-C02-4]] — US retailers selling smaller pack sizes signals stretched consumers who cannot pay normal prices.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C05-2|MT071-C05-2]] — Copper demand reflects the AI/data-center capex theme, a demand-side story that supports the commodity trend independent of supply disruptions.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C08-4|MT071-C08-4]] — The AI productivity shock raises prices now via demand (chips, memory, electricity) while its disinflationary supply effect arrives later, creating a dilemma like Greenspan's 1990s.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C09-2|MT071-C09-2]] — Large Apple price increases will show up in inflation data as AI hardware cost pass-through.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C09-4|MT071-C09-4]] — Aspect argues the last 25 years were an anomaly of cheap energy and globalization peace dividend, now ending with structural breaks that favor trend following.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C09-5|MT071-C09-5]] — Bonds are now less reliable diversifiers for equities, while gold demand from central banks and the dollar's store-of-value role are reassessed.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C09-6|MT071-C09-6]] — Trend following is an adaptive diversifier for regime change and a managed way to gain commodity exposure.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C10-1|MT071-C10-1]] — Supply shocks in copper, food or other commodities can also hurt bonds and equities, so oil is not the only relevant scenario.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C12-1|MT071-C12-1]] — Electronification does not explain the decline since rate futures electronified without a profitability loss.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C12-2|MT071-C12-2]] — The CFM paper finds volatility-adjusted tick size is the key variable for whether a market suits fast trend following: large-tick markets (bonds, rates, some commodities) work, small-tick ones (currencies) do not.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C13-3|MT071-C13-3]] — Fast trend still works in large-tick contracts (bonds, rates, some commodities); the weakness is concentrated in equities and currencies, so the headline overstates the decline.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C13-5|MT071-C13-5]] — Counter-trend trades have become more dominant, so after breaks there is a reversal rather than follow-through, making markets more mean reverting at short horizons.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C14-1|MT071-C14-1]] — In large-tick markets there is no CTA winter at any lookback, and fast trend did better over 1995 onward, implying the winter is concentrated in small-tick markets.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C16-1|MT071-C16-1]] — Fast trend does better in low volatility, and what has vanished is the ability to profit from large one-day directional moves.
- [[MT071 - Has Trend Following Changed Forever- - Systematic Investor - Ep.408#^MT071-C17-2|MT071-C17-2]] — Execution now goes from signal to exchange via algorithms that behave differently from human traders, which may contribute to changes in short-term dynamics.

## [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350]]
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C08-3|MT072-C08-3]] — Productivity gains from new technology (AI) typically lag adoption by 10-15 years, so growth will not fix the debt soon.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C09-2|MT072-C09-2]] — The Stargate AI data center in Texas will use energy equal to 750,000 homes, an example of AI-driven power demand.
- [[MT072 - Higher Yields Are Here to Stay - Systematic Investor - Ep.350#^MT072-C10-1|MT072-C10-1]] — Reindustrializing a full-employment US economy creates bottlenecks, skill shortages and wage pressure that may spill over to broad inflation.

## [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362]]
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C03-5|MT073-C03-5]] — A commodity is not one object: contracts for different delivery months (old crop vs new crop) differ by supply, demand, regulation, so curve points can be traded separately.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C04-2|MT073-C04-2]] — The front of the commodity curve is more volatile and driven by macro news, while long-dated prices follow long-term supply and demand.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C05-6|MT073-C05-6]] — If two contracts are fungible they converge; a supply shock to one crop year breaks that and produces divergence.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C06-2|MT073-C06-2]] — Refinery outages make crack spreads trend, a transient and diversifying risk factor unrelated to long-term oil.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C13-3|MT073-C13-3]] — The FX derivative market is star-shaped: the top five dealers and five market makers dominate, much more than 20 years ago.
- [[MT073 - How Alpha Hides in Plain Sight - Systematic Investor - Ep.362#^MT073-C14-1|MT073-C14-1]] — FX spot is structured differently, with non-bank market makers, while derivatives are concentrated in few counterparties which may be a cause for concern.

## [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149]]
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C04-X1|MT074-C04-X1]] — Portable alpha with CTAs is not for everyone: most allocators have strict rules against re-introducing beta via overlays, so demand comes mainly from mid-size allocators that lack bespoke or notional-funding capability and invest through funds.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C09-4|MT074-C09-4]] — The 2010s of secular stagnation and low macro volatility gave way to higher macro volatility, favoring CTAs.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C12-1|MT074-C12-1]] — Organizations unable to adopt TPA use portable alpha within existing sleeves to move toward the same result without restructuring.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C12-2|MT074-C12-2]] — A CTA does not fit a single asset-class bucket (hedge fund team wants low beta, equity team wants equities), hence the bucket problem.
- [[MT074 - How CTAs and Portable Alpha Could Reshape Investing in 2025 - Top Traders Unplugged 149#^MT074-C13-R1|MT074-C13-R1]] — Replication-based businesses spend more on marketing than research, whereas Aspect concentrates resources on research (about 90 people in research and development).

## [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293]]
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C01-3|MT076-C01-3]] — A record-low Chinese 10-year yield signals a weaker Chinese economy than it appears.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C04-5|MT076-C04-5]] — Options let a trader bet on specific paths (time, price and moneyness) instead of two-dimensional direction, which is where the opportunity lies.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C09-3|MT076-C09-3]] — Stocks and bonds only offer direction while options express the full distribution of outcomes, so options are the superior technology for betting on path.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C10-1|MT076-C10-1]] — Options volume and exchange (Cboe) volumes are at record highs, driven by product proliferation, retail access and education.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C10-X1|MT076-C10-X1]] — In a regime change with more relative-value opportunities (as seen at macro pods), the need to express multi-dimensional views rises, which drives options volume growth.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C11-4|MT076-C11-4]] — Structural buyers (Fed, Treasury, buybacks) make demand tilted over supply, so equity trends tend to be upward.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C15-5|MT076-C15-5]] — European energy problems are reversing as commodity prices decline, giving a reason for European rebalancing despite pessimism.
- [[MT076 - How Global Conflicts affect the Economy & 2024 Outlook - Systematic Investor 293#^MT076-C16-1|MT076-C16-1]] — Europe and China should hold up better than expected and US weakness will be structural, so start speculating on them on a relative basis.

## [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment]]
- [[MT078 - Howard Marks - Co-founder of Oaktree - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment#^MT078-C01-2|MT078-C01-2]] — Target less efficient markets (credit, niches) because in efficient markets one cannot know more than others; specialization in a few things is required.

## [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306]]
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C02-3|MT079-C02-3]] — The 2016 money market fund rules raised structural demand for short-term instruments, so Treasury has room to issue more bills.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C10-3|MT079-C10-3]] — The one-year rolling stock-bond correlation was at a multi-year high in mid-2024, exposing portfolios to higher risk.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C12-1|MT079-C12-1]] — In the GIC/JPM framework managed futures ranked second for alpha after beta and global macro first.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C12-2|MT079-C12-2]] — Trend following meets all three criteria (low equity correlation, drawdown mitigation, positive alpha); not an equity substitute.
- [[MT079 - If Trend Following was an Olympic Sport - Systematic Investor 306#^MT079-C15-1|MT079-C15-1]] — Every technology wave in 30 years was predicted to hurt trend following, and it has not.

## [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337]]
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C08-1|MT080-C08-1]] — CTA ETFs attract mostly individuals and RIA model portfolios in the US, and institutions use them as an easy, no-paperwork liquid allocation.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C08-2|MT080-C08-2]] — Institutions like ETFs for fast allocation changes and parking assets in CTA-like returns.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C11-1|MT080-C11-1]] — CTA indices include non-investable markets so you cannot build the index from building blocks as in equities; ETF beta must be approximated.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C15-1|MT080-C15-1]] — Some new CTA ETFs trade only a couple of sectors, unlike the diversified managed futures norm, so results may differ widely.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C16-1|MT080-C16-1]] — CTA ETF products need new nomenclature; portable alpha, beta and different asset class are the key distinctions in this innovation phase.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C16-2|MT080-C16-2]] — Early 40-Act managed futures funds had low correlation to the industry index and later converged to high correlation, suggesting ETFs will also settle into buckets.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C16-3|MT080-C16-3]] — ETF versions are simplified because daily intraday liquidity favours less turnover and markets that market makers can hedge.
- [[MT080 - Index vs. Mechanical Replication in Trend Following - Systematic Investor 337#^MT080-C17-1|MT080-C17-1]] — Mutual funds only need a daily mark so a wide market range is fine, while ETFs fit liquid markets with low turnover to limit slippage.

## [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod]]
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C01-1|MT081-C01-1]] — Hyperscaler AI capex (over $400B) is nearly as large as expected US GDP growth ($530B at 1.8% on $29.18T), so the economy excluding AI spending is roughly flat.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C01-2|MT081-C01-2]] — Long-run market and economy analysis should be framed as how the GDP pie is split between corporations and people, not by technical charts and momentum; the market and the economy are different things.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C02-3|MT081-C02-3]] — Investment excluding AI is flat to down, because of tariffs, so the strength in aggregate investment and equities is concentrated in AI.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C02-5|MT081-C02-5]] — Small businesses lack the staff to find alternative suppliers, check tariff rates or obtain exemptions, so tariffs hit the engine of growth harder than large firms.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C02-6|MT081-C02-6]] — The K-shaped economy has an upward leg, a downward leg and a large flat middle, so the shape is not simply a split between winners and losers.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C03-3|MT081-C03-3]] — Tariffs and more repair demand from owners keeping old cars are both raising car parts costs, which worsens the burden on stretched drivers.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C03-5|MT081-C03-5]] — Cruise lines kept prices flat while hotel prices rose multiples after COVID, so cruises are cheap relative to hotels and have pricing power left.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C04-1|MT081-C04-1]] — Youth unemployment (around 11%) is far above the headline rate (4.5%), and recent computer science graduates report difficulty finding jobs.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C04-2|MT081-C04-2]] — AI is replacing entry-level and even experienced coding work, so careers once recommended for retraining no longer absorb workers.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C04-3|MT081-C04-3]] — Computerised machine tools once cut a turbine repair from 6 months to 6 weeks, an example of general-purpose technology producing a generational productivity gain; AI could do the same.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C04-4|MT081-C04-4]] — The AI boom only pays off if profits accrue to the users of the technology, not just its suppliers; he wants the market to pay multiples on users before being convinced.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C04-5|MT081-C04-5]] — Nvidia's argument is that trillions in AI spend will return tens of trillions.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C05-3|MT081-C05-3]] — AI returns have not yet appeared: the product is better but not by a multiple that justifies the spending, so the payoff is still awaited.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C06-4|MT081-C06-4]] — Tariff-hit industries have fallen sharply but index-level results are masked by AI stocks, producing a K-shaped market.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C07-2|MT081-C07-2]] — The dynamism of US businesses to re-source and pivot cushions tariff shocks.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C07-3|MT081-C07-3]] — A small business earning $200k may not survive 15% tariffs on its inputs.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C07-4|MT081-C07-4]] — Tariffs on inputs hurt manufacturers that add value domestically, contradicting economists' advice.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C07-5|MT081-C07-5]] — Firms lower tariff bills by shifting cost attribution to the domestic entity through intercompany pricing, so the effective tariff rate falls over time.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C07-X1|MT081-C07-X1]] — Importers of goods containing metal pay twice, the country tariff plus the tariff on metal content, so the effective burden on such products is higher than the headline country rate.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C09-1|MT081-C09-1]] — Bernanke made the Fed more transparent (press conferences, forecasts, dots), which Powell extended to every meeting, so policy is now guided by communication.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C10-1|MT081-C10-1]] — Inequality is driven mainly by returns to technology, not by the Fed.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C12-2|MT081-C12-2]] — Institutions such as legal codes cannot be imported; foreign-written codes and myths fail, one reason Russian reform failed.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C13-2|MT081-C13-2]] — Privatising Russian oil companies reversed a 20-year decline in output within two years because of ownership incentives.
- [[MT081 - Inside America’s Economic Divide- AI, Tariffs, & The Fed - The Real Eisman Playbook Episod#^MT081-C14-1|MT081-C14-1]] — Officials who had never lived with market prices could not grasp that letting prices float would move oil to a region, illustrating price-signal allocation.

## [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1]]
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C02-5|MT082-C02-5]] — Deglobalisation moves production closer to home, which needs a lot of commodities and could raise some prices.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C03-1|MT082-C03-1]] — The energy transition raises power demand (EVs, data centers, cooling), so it supports copper, aluminium, lithium, cobalt, nickel and silver.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C03-2|MT082-C03-2]] — An ageing population with accumulated savings will spend that money, adding to consumption and commodity demand.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C03-3|MT082-C03-3]] — Continuing urbanisation in emerging markets underpins commodity demand.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C03-4|MT082-C03-4]] — Mining projects take many years, so miners must guess prices 10 to 12 years ahead, which holds back investment and can cause future metal shortfalls.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C03-5|MT082-C03-5]] — Softs are produced in small geographic areas, so weather in West Africa (cocoa) and Brazil (coffee) drives large price moves.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C03-6|MT082-C03-6]] — Rising US gas exports, domestic demand and European LNG demand underpin a long-term bullish case for natural gas.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C04-4|MT082-C04-4]] — Chinese copper demand has held up despite the construction downturn, and data centers and power transmission add demand beyond EVs.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C04-5|MT082-C04-5]] — In the gold rush the shovel supplier made the money, so power-grid builders such as Siemens Energy (up 300%) capture the AI and electrification boom, as Nvidia did.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C10-2|MT082-C10-2]] — Cocoa was not in the Bloomberg Commodity Index because of its size, so the index missed the cocoa rally, while natural gas, a member, dragged it down.
- [[MT082 - Inside Calculo Capital- Crushing Commodity Markets with Machine Learning - Open Interest 1#^MT082-C10-X1|MT082-C10-X1]] — First-generation long-only commodity indices (e.g. BCOM) are concentrated (caption says equity; likely energy) and metals heavy and exclude small markets, while newer variants diversify more and spread exposure along the curve, so index choice drives commodity beta returns.

## [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394]]
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C06-3|MT083-C06-3]] — The market universe can be widened from traditional cleared futures (crude, gold) to alternative markets (Nordic hydropower, iron ore, Chinese bitumen and glass) and synthetic markets (equity factors) with different return drivers.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C07-1|MT083-C07-1]] — Traditional markets load more on macro factors (risk on/off, growth, inflation, monetary policy) while alternative markets have more idiosyncratic drivers.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C10-1|MT083-C10-1]] — Alpha evaporation in active equities shifted investors to passive, so portable alpha splits the decision: own the index via swap or future and separately find a repeatable alpha.
