- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C15-3|MT083-C15-3]] — Change hurts convergent strategies while divergent strategies such as trend are designed for it.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C18-1|MT083-C18-1]] — Oil shocks weaken the consumer and create inflation shocks, so geopolitical risk transmits to managed futures through energy.
- [[MT083 - Inside Q1’s Chaos - Systematic Investor - Ep.394#^MT083-C18-3|MT083-C18-3]] — Allocations to trend and non-correlated strategies are too small for the risks in 2026 (recovery or deeper crisis).

## [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377]]
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C03-2|MT084-C03-2]] — Trend's tough late-2010s period eased from 2019, and COVID shifted the economy out of secular stagnation, restoring trend conditions.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C05-2|MT084-C05-2]] — SocGen index AUM cutoffs for 2026: about $1.9B for the CTA index and $4.64B in one strategy for the Trend index, which favours large managers.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C05-3|MT084-C05-3]] — Crabel's largest strategy is now its trend program, not short-term, suggesting long-term strategies are more viable than short-term.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C06-1|MT084-C06-1]] — The high AUM cutoff suggests the largest trend followers are consolidating their position in the index.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C07-3|MT084-C07-3]] — Global yields are rising (JGB, German 25-year, no news) because stronger investment demand (AI data centers, defense) shifted the savings-investment balance from excess savings to scarcer capital; the US 10-year (4.1%) is the outlier.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C07-4|MT084-C07-4]] — In the 1990s productivity came from diffusion of earlier tech (PCs) while internet infrastructure gave demand, so the AI parallel is superficial.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C08-1|MT084-C08-1]] — AI spending adds to GDP arithmetically but evidence of productivity gains is mixed (McKinsey: more trying than use; St. Louis Fed: marginal gains).
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C08-3|MT084-C08-3]] — In the McKinsey survey AI use rose from 20% in 2017 to 88% today, showing adoption is widespread.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C10-1|MT084-C10-1]] — From the 1980s a 40-year disinflation (Volcker, China trade, Soviet collapse, free-market acceptance, central bank independence) created a great moderation.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C10-2|MT084-C10-2]] — Since 2020 the regime is higher, stickier inflation with shocks (COVID, Ukraine) and a changed bond-equity correlation.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C10-4|MT084-C10-4]] — With households owning about $40T of equities, an equity bear market could wipe out $20T of wealth, so the economy is more levered to equities.
- [[MT084 - Inside the Regime-Adaptive Portfolio - Systematic Investor - Ep.377#^MT084-C15-1|MT084-C15-1]] — In 1966-82 high inflation derated equities, so equities are not a reliable inflation hedge.

## [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318]]
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C03-1|MT086-C03-1]] — Trend following is a medium to long horizon strategy, so it needs less intense trader stress management than high-frequency trading.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C05-2|MT086-C05-2]] — Kaminski's macro view: inflation matters more now, the post-2008 low-rate regime was historically abnormal and the baseline of rates may stay higher with more inflation volatility.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C07-X1|MT086-C07-X1]] — The protection 60/40 investors got from negative stock-bond correlation depended, largely unknowingly, on a secular falling-rate cycle, so it need not persist once that cycle ends.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C09-2|MT086-C09-2]] — Niels doubts stock-bond correlation will revert to the persistent negative correlation of the first decades of this century.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C10-2|MT086-C10-2]] — Her 2018 factor analysis of the SG Trend index showed that over time index constituents slowed down and picked up non-trend premia exposure (value, carry, cross-sectional momentum), especially post-2008.
- [[MT086 - Interest Rates & Trend Following - Systematic Investor 318#^MT086-C10-3|MT086-C10-3]] — In a higher-rate regime pure momentum has become more desirable, so the index may drift back toward faster, purer trend.

## [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352]]
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C05-2|MT087-C05-2]] — Open question whether V-shaped events once a year (SVB 2023, Aug 2024, April 2025) reflect a changed regime of shorter-lived, noisier information.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C11-2|MT087-C11-2]] — Institutional constraints (liquidity, governance, regulation, leverage limits, benchmarks, reputation) confine the portfolio space; an unconstrained investor would hold more private equity, CTAs, infrastructure.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C15-1|MT087-C15-1]] — Trend following does not show positive skew at daily frequency, only over longer windows, so judging it day to day is unfair; discipline and expectation-setting matter.
- [[MT087 - Investing in the Age of AI, Uncertainty, and Data Overload - Systematic Investor - Ep.352#^MT087-C15-3|MT087-C15-3]] — Hedge Nordic editorial question: markets moving on tweets and statements may mean trend rules are breaking down or merely paused; whether to add discretionary input.

## [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap]]
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C02-1|MT088-C02-1]] — US economic growth depends on AI capex, so a stop in AI spending would trigger a recession almost immediately.
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C02-2|MT088-C02-2]] — Hyperscalers' free cash flow has collapsed under AI capex and they are raising debt and equity; this is a concern but not fatal if AI generates large returns.
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C03-1|MT088-C03-1]] — Hyperscaler capex of about $700bn is about half of projected 2026 US GDP growth of 2%, so the economy depends on hyperscaler capex, which depends on Anthropic and OpenAI.
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C03-2|MT088-C03-2]] — OpenAI's June 2026 quarter revenue was $6.7bn (+18% q/q) against Anthropic's $11bn+ (over +100% q/q), and OpenAI's costs rose $3bn to $12.3bn, so losses are widening.
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C03-3|MT088-C03-3]] — OpenAI made a strategic mistake chasing B2C with underpriced product, and now plays catch-up in B2B where Anthropic started.
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C04-3|MT088-C04-3]] — Eisman's chain: OpenAI failure leads hyperscalers to cut capex, cloud growth slows, tech and AI-linked industrials and banks correct and GDP falls; he stresses this is preparation, not a prediction.
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C04-5|MT088-C04-5]] — Nvidia's July 2026 quarter revenue growth exceeded 100%, up from 85% in the April quarter, showing the AI story continues.
- [[MT088 - Is OpenAI the Achilles’ Heel of the US Economy- - The Weekly Wrap#^MT088-C05-2|MT088-C05-2]] — OpenAI's announced $1bn advertising ARR is below the $2.4bn projected for 2026 and small against $12.3bn quarterly costs, so apparently positive news is negative.

## [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive]]
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C01-2|MT089-C01-2]] — Early CTAs/macro earned high returns because markets were less efficient and an immature industry offered edge.
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C04-3|MT089-C04-3]] — Macro's share of hedge fund AUM fell from about 50-70% in the 1990s to about 15-20% now.
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C06-4|MT089-C06-4]] — Pod-shop multi-manager structures have pass-through fees and long lockups that some clients dislike; Graham launched its own macro-focused multi-strategy fund to compete for talent.
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C07-1|MT089-C07-1]] — AI is powerful, but Tropin sees human strategic judgment as an edge not yet replaceable.
- [[MT089 - Ken Tropin on How to Run a Successful Macro Hedge Fund by Bilal Hafeez, Macro Hive#^MT089-C08-X1|MT089-C08-X1]] — Despite polarised US politics, the US stays the most competitive economy over a 5-10 year view because Europe and Asia have their own problems, a relative rather than absolute judgment.

## [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next]]
- [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next#^MT090-C02-1|MT090-C02-1]] — Multi-strategy funds give only 10-15% of risk to macro; Graham offers 75-80% macro risk and custom-designed funds for clients.
- [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next#^MT090-C02-2|MT090-C02-2]] — Quant approaches must evolve; computing power and AI are radically different, so firms must be at least competitive with the best thinking on AI and new quantitative opportunities.
- [[MT090 - Ken Tropin on Macro Hive Podcast- Markets, Risk, AI-Driven Technology, and What Comes Next#^MT090-C07-2|MT090-C07-2]] — US-China relationship is interdependent, not a Cold War paradigm, so escalation is limited (Taiwan tail risk remains); Russia/Ukraine resolution is naive to expect.

## [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C01-1|MT091-C01-1]] — The current policy regime (tariffs, fracturing globalization, pressure on institutions) is analogous to the 1970s Nixon era, which implies a wild macro ride and an inflation-prone backdrop.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C01-2|MT091-C01-2]] — Undermining rule of law and the credibility of US commitments (e.g. paying debts) erodes the foundation of US exceptionalism and the premium assets in the US enjoy.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C01-3|MT091-C01-3]] — Reshoring via protectionism may add some manufacturing jobs but raises costs and lowers incomes versus an integrated global economy.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C02-X2|MT091-C02-X2]] — Protectionism is bipartisan in the US (Bernie Sanders ran on similar trade positions in 2016), so tariffs are not a one-person quirk and are more likely to persist after the current administration.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C03-1|MT091-C03-1]] — Tariffs give a short-term price level hit (higher prices, lower quality) but the bigger cost is slower innovation and lower long-run growth, not lasting inflation.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C03-2|MT091-C03-2]] — US consumers bear tariffs dollar for dollar through higher prices, as studies of the 2018 tariffs show.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C03-3|MT091-C03-3]] — European tech independence and defence build-up are linked because military spending funds early-stage tech, per the Draghi report logic.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C03-4|MT091-C03-4]] — US trade pressure is a forcing event pushing Europe toward greater strategic and economic independence.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C03-X1|MT091-C03-X1]] — Europe's institutional stability (single elections change a lot but not everything) makes it a more stable geopolitical counterweight to a US whose direction can reverse with each presidential election.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C04-2|MT091-C04-2]] — Tariffs and subsidies are both weak ways to attract investment; a good regulatory and business environment works better and subsidy wars are effectively trade war.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C05-1|MT091-C05-1]] — The dollar's global footprint (reserves, transactions, invoicing) peaked around 2015 and is slowly declining.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C05-2|MT091-C05-2]] — Challengers have fallen short: the euro is only regional, the yen barely used and the renminbi less, so the dollar keeps top position despite its decline.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C05-3|MT091-C05-3]] — Reserve-currency transitions are slow, taking about a century (Spanish peso example), so the dollar fades gradually and a sudden loss of status is unlikely.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C05-4|MT091-C05-4]] — China is expanding renminbi pricing and lending and building its own clearing rails to reduce exposure to US sanction power.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C06-1|MT091-C06-1]] — Dollar clearing and messaging control give the US visibility and sanction leverage, which motivates China (after Russia sanctions) to build alternative clearing mechanisms.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C06-3|MT091-C06-3]] — A China slowdown hits energy and commodity exporters (Norway, US as energy producer, Brazil, Argentina); a growth rebound to 8% would help them.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C07-2|MT091-C07-2]] — Crushing the private sector (Jack Ma, party members on boards) harms Chinese growth and makes Chinese tech hard to invest in despite its quality.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C07-3|MT091-C07-3]] — Manufacturing and new investment diversifying away from China (China plus one) benefits Japan, Korea, Singapore, Malaysia, Indonesia and India.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C07-5|MT091-C07-5]] — India grows faster than China with new infrastructure but monopoly concentration, tariffs and governance difficulty cap enthusiasm.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C08-2|MT091-C08-2]] — The low-inflation era resulted partly from tailwinds (globalization, China's rise, anti-populist consensus, hands-off politics) not just central bank skill; those tailwinds have faded.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C09-5|MT091-C09-5]] — Worsening storms and climate extremes are arriving sooner and reduce output while raising prices.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C09-7|MT091-C09-7]] — Central bank independence is an experiment only about 40 years old; historically banks sat inside treasuries, so it is politically reversible.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C13-7|MT091-C13-7]] — The 10-year real (TIPS) yield swung from about -1 to +2, a 3-point change, ending the secular-stagnation low-rate regime.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C15-3|MT091-C15-3]] — AI may exert disinflationary pressure like globalization did, though also bring worker displacement and political tension.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C15-4|MT091-C15-4]] — The risk of AI is the speed of change, which could cause chaotic adjustment in workplaces and warfare.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C15-5|MT091-C15-5]] — NBIM saw about 15% efficiency gain from AI-assisted coding last year and expects 20% this year.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C15-X2|MT091-C15-X2]] — Some technologists (Vinod Khosla, per the host) argue that AI and robots will make computing and labor effectively free, an extreme deflationary view that the host raises against Rogoff's expectation of lasting inflation.
- [[MT091 - Kenneth Rogoff - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT091-C16-1|MT091-C16-1]] — Do not ignore inflation because of AI: political pressure on central banks is strong, so inflation will be a problem for decades even as AI transforms life.

## [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast]]
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C01-1|MT092-C01-1]] — Central-bank activity (BOE, Fed, ECB) rose from 22 quarter-point changes over 2010-2021 to 76 since 2022, about nine times the annual opportunity set for macro and fixed income traders.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C03-5|MT092-C03-5]] — Even colleagues disagree on whether AI destroys or creates jobs, so AI's labor effect is a big unknown for equity valuation.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C06-X1|MT092-C06-X1]] — The 'dollar diminishment' narrative is overdone because there is no credible alternative home for capital (China, crypto), so moving out of the dollar is itself a big risk.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C07-1|MT092-C07-1]] — Crypto infrastructure and underlying technology may be a stronger investment than the price of Bitcoin, akin to a Swensen-like alternatives moment.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C07-X1|MT092-C07-X1]] — Ken says he would rather invest in the United States for its regulatory infrastructure, resources and transparency, even while advocating some diversification beyond US and traditional assets.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C10-2|MT092-C10-2]] — Citigroup is reportedly looking to buy a brokerage (Stifel at roughly 11 billion or Raymond James at about 38 billion market cap), or build one organically by hiring teams.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C10-3|MT092-C10-3]] — Brokerages offer stable earnings and a distribution channel for investment products and hot IPOs, completing a multi-strategy investment bank model.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C11-1|MT092-C11-1]] — Warner Bros Discovery stock roughly doubled from penny-stock levels as CEO Zaslav cut debt and split the company into two units that are easier to sell.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C11-2|MT092-C11-2]] — Warner Bros Discovery is the one traditional media company showing a profit in streaming, a counterintuitive value story.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C11-X1|MT092-C11-X1]] — Citigroup is positioned to buy a brokerage because CEO Jane Fraser fixed the balance sheet, reorganized and sold businesses, and the stock has outperformed JPMorgan over the past year.
- [[MT092 - Kenneth Tropin, Chairman and Founder of Graham, Featured on the Risk and Return Podcast#^MT092-C11-X2|MT092-C11-X2]] — Warner Bros Discovery's recovery came from balance-sheet discipline (cutting debt, cutting content spending, refusing to pay up for NBA rights), which alienates Hollywood but raised profit and doubled the stock.

## [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -]]
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C03-1|MT093-C03-1]] — The Fidelity paper argues managed futures dynamically follow trends, keep low correlation to traditional assets and protect capital in crises, though not outperforming in every environment.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C12-X1|MT093-C12-X1]] — The share of assets in passive or index strategies has risen persistently over a long period, so the passive-versus-active debate is largely won by passive.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C14-X1|MT093-C14-X1]] — Plausible 'the world has changed' stories for weak CTA returns (Trump policy, more algorithmic trading, markets becoming more efficient so less alpha) are narratives that cannot be separated from bad luck without a testable method.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C16-1|MT093-C16-1]] — Trend following has survived 51 years of change because it adapts through research, though 1970s rules should not be applied unchanged.
- [[MT093 - Lehman’s Legacy, ETF Performance & What Investors Still Get Wrong - Systematic Investor -#^MT093-C16-3|MT093-C16-3]] — Early trend-following results had fewer markets and less diversification, which complicates volatility-adjusted comparisons over time.

## [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2]]
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C03-X1|MT094-C03-X1]] — Tariffs are a delivered campaign promise to blue-collar and union voters (autos, UAW on stage), which gives the administration a political reason to keep them and shows how the parties have swapped trade positions.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C03-X2|MT094-C03-X2]] — The administration cites about $6 trillion of new US investment pledges (Nvidia, Apple, TSMC, SoftBank, OpenAI) as the payoff from tariffs, which is the administration's justification for the policy.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C04-1|MT094-C04-1]] — Comparative advantage theory implies trade walls reduce efficiency in both countries in the long run.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C04-2|MT094-C04-2]] — Reshoring a factory from Asia takes years and costs more because US labor is not globally competitive, so tariffs cannot quickly shift production.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C05-1|MT094-C05-1]] — Retaliation effects: customers cancelling US vendors and China, Korea and Japan (and Europe) coordinating a response, shifting trade ties toward China.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C06-4|MT094-C06-4]] — Chinese support for open source is long-standing (not forced on DeepSeek), since open source removes IP-theft accusations.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C07-1|MT094-C07-1]] — Large companies use open source defensively when behind (Google/Kubernetes vs AWS, Android vs Apple, Meta/Llama), which commoditizes the leader's advantage.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C07-2|MT094-C07-2]] — Open source creates the most fluid competition and lowest consumer prices versus proprietary code.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C07-3|MT094-C07-3]] — DeepSeek is prolific in enterprise (hosted by AWS and Google, forked 1500+ times), so Washington may act to limit its use.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C07-4|MT094-C07-4]] — A short window exists for a US lab (Google or Meta; not Anthropic) to out-open DeepSeek, as an offensive or defensive move.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C08-1|MT094-C08-1]] — OpenAI committed to an open-weight reasoning model without Llama's 700M-user licensing cap, putting pressure on Meta before LlamaCon.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C08-2|MT094-C08-2]] — Llama 4 is rumored to be a 400bn-parameter mixture-of-experts with a 10-million context window, launching before LlamaCon.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C09-1|MT094-C09-1]] — Open-sourcing a model protects the competitive flank like Google's Kubernetes move: others cannot make an API business, the model spreads globally, and compute is borne by users.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C09-2|MT094-C09-2]] — Models are commoditizing; the contest is around products and services, with OpenAI focused on consumer (ChatGPT) and a leading enterprise business.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C09-3|MT094-C09-3]] — ChatGPT's consumer base drives enterprise adoption (like iPhones displacing BlackBerry), with roughly 80-90% consumer share.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C09-X1|MT094-C09-X1]] — Even if Washington restricts DeepSeek, a Western lab that wants to lead open source globally has to beat DeepSeek on openness and capability worldwide, not only be more open than other Western labs.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C09-X2|MT094-C09-X2]] — OpenAI's open-weight release serves three aims: bringing developers into its ecosystem, building products at scale, and acknowledging that models are commoditizing, so platform lock-in comes from products rather than the model.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C11-2|MT094-C11-2]] — Capital-intensive winner-take-all competition postpones unit economics, requiring belief in power laws; Bill compares it to Uber/Lyft and a sport of kings.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C11-4|MT094-C11-4]] — Serving a free ChatGPT user costs roughly 15-20 dollars a year, which advertising or paid conversion may eventually cover.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C11-5|MT094-C11-5]] — Uber was called unprofitable in 2020 yet now makes about 6bn FCF, so losses along the way do not disprove winner-take-most dynamics.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C11-X2|MT094-C11-X2]] — A challenger chasing ChatGPT must be prepared to fund the compute cost of free users itself, which makes the race a sport of kings and worrying for investors in smaller rivals such as Anthropic.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C12-1|MT094-C12-1]] — ChatGPT reached 20 million paid subscribers and 500 million weekly users, adding a million users in an hour.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C12-3|MT094-C12-3]] — Viral demand for image generation (anime photos) is a sign of consumer pull: the next big thing often first appears as a toy.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C12-4|MT094-C12-4]] — ChatGPT's demand curves and cohort retention resemble early Google and Meta, so its consumer business will ultimately be valuable despite poor unit economics.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C13-1|MT094-C13-1]] — With more compute, upgraded models and pricing tiers would raise paid conversion above the roughly 4%, and a single smart model would replace the long model menu.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C13-2|MT094-C13-2]] — In an agent world (booking hotels) value to users or merchants creates mixed models of ads, tiers and commissions.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C13-3|MT094-C13-3]] — Google's maximized revenue per visitor makes it vulnerable, since agent partners cannot pay fees close to what advertisers pay Google.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C13-X1|MT094-C13-X1]] — ChatGPT has a big moat because network effects are kicking in: it is an order of magnitude bigger than rivals (even with Grok a strong model) and growing faster, so the consumer business will be valuable despite poor early unit economics.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C14-1|MT094-C14-1]] — Travel advertisers like Booking pay Google about 50 per 100-dollar room using LTV math, while an agent-underlying service could share at most about 10, a big disruption advantage for OpenAI.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C14-2|MT094-C14-2]] — Open standards like MCP (started at Anthropic, now supported by OpenAI) let the most aggressive adopters define the agent ecosystem.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C14-3|MT094-C14-3]] — The next 3-6 months are a critical window, with executive shifts and anxiety at Google, Meta and Apple, ending 20 years of search paradigm dominance.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C14-X1|MT094-C14-X1]] — Google's share price falling from about 200 to about 150 shows the market already pricing disruption of search advertising by AI agents.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C14-X2|MT094-C14-X2]] — Antitrust action to break up Google's search monopoly arrives just as real competition from AI appears, so the market itself is correcting the monopoly and remedies are the last of Google's problems.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C15-1|MT094-C15-1]] — CoreWeave diversified customers beyond Microsoft (Google deal on Nvidia Grace Blackwell), falling to about 37 around IPO then rising; it emerged as leading AI cloud.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C16-2|MT094-C16-2]] — GPUs keep value past the first two training years because they are reused for inference, so a two-year depreciation (implied by Jensen's 'chief revenue destroyer' remark) is too aggressive.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C16-3|MT094-C16-3]] — CoreWeave contracts (four-year Microsoft, OpenAI, Google) repay capex, opex and GPUs in three years so year four is the margin; consensus EBIT around 25% gives no credit beyond contracts.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C17-1|MT094-C17-1]] — Evidence of long GPU lives: A100s (2020) still widely used and V100s retired after seven years; CUDA software updates improve GPUs over time.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C18-2|MT094-C18-2]] — CoreWeave's series C put right at 38.75 likely influenced the IPO pricing at 40.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C19-1|MT094-C19-1]] — ByteDance trades near $300bn secondary but many see $800bn-1tn, so a TikTok deal unlocks hundreds of billions of venture DPI as a condition to take ByteDance out; TikTok US is about 10% of value.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C19-X1|MT094-C19-X1]] — The reported breakthrough is that TikTok US licenses the same global algorithm, because ByteDance had said the algorithm cannot be split and would sooner shut down US TikTok than separate it.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C20-3|MT094-C20-3]] — US-China bilateral trade relations will dictate the direction of global and both countries' growth for the next few years.
- [[MT094 - Liberation Day, Tariffs, US v China Open Source, OpenAI, $CRWV, TikTok - BG2#^MT094-C20-X1|MT094-C20-X1]] — For ByteDance shareholders the dilution from creating TikTok US is small relative to total company value (about 90% of value is outside TikTok US), so what matters is certainty of a deal.

## [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369]]
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C01-1|MT095-C01-1]] — Safe-asset status is relative and changing: French, UK and even US Treasury debt (shutdown, high debt/GDP) are no longer clearly safe, which is relevant to gold at $4,000.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C02-X1|MT095-C02-X1]] — The 2025 gold rally cannot be explained by inflation, which is lower than two years earlier, so central-bank reserve diversification away from traditional safe assets is the better explanation than inflation hedging or speculation.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C03-1|MT095-C03-1]] — Gold's fundamentals are unstable: real rates, inflation and uncertainty (VIX) each link to gold only loosely and the drivers are in flux.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C03-4|MT095-C03-4]] — Gold ETFs and CME acceptance of gold as margin collateral financialize gold and give it safe-asset characteristics.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C06-X1|MT095-C06-X1]] — With ample reserves and interest paid on reserves, banks no longer need to borrow reserves, so the Fed funds market has shrunk to foreign-bank buyers and Federal Home Loan Bank providers and no longer represents true money-market funding rates.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C09-1|MT095-C09-1]] — CME and FanDuel/Robinhood push prediction markets and retail into futures; financial innovation can have unintended consequences.
- [[MT095 - Liquidity, Leverage, and the End of Safety - Systematic Investor - Ep.369#^MT095-C10-R1|MT095-C10-R1]] — Trade finance is an old banking business, so the novelty behind the First Brands losses is the packaging of it as a stable-return fund product rather than a new instrument.

## [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355]]
- [[MT096 - Liquidity, Volatility, and a Dangerous Calm in Markets - Systematic Investor - Ep.355#^MT096-C10-2|MT096-C10-2]] — The 2022 trend gains came from a one-off rate adjustment from zero, with managers positioning early with large size, so 2022 performance should not be extrapolated.

## [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)]]
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C01-1|MT098-C01-1]] — Passive indexing is effectively a macro approach (buy the whole market rather than pick needles) which has risen because information moves faster and the world economy is interconnected.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C01-X1|MT098-C01-X1]] — Graham late in life judged that elaborate security analysis no longer found superior value because markets had changed (become more efficient), an early statement of alpha decay in stock picking used to motivate a top-down macro view.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C02-1|MT098-C02-1]] — Because companies and banks are globally interconnected, macro and government policy matter more than in Buffett's era, e.g. trouble at a European bank can force the Fed to act through payment-system links.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C02-2|MT098-C02-2]] — Stock picking is like finding the best swimmer, but the macro 'river' determines how fast everyone swims, so top-down macro understanding should come before security selection.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C04-2|MT098-C04-2]] — In macroeconomics investment means spending for future production (e.g. a firm builds a factory), whereas buying stocks and bonds in secondary markets is technically reallocating savings with little impact on the firm.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C08-1|MT098-C08-1]] — A home bought by a typical owner earns only about 1-2% real return after tax, maintenance and inflation, because owners do not count upkeep and taxes; outcomes depend on entry timing (2006 vs 2012).
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C10-X1|MT098-C10-X1]] — Structural regimes that seem fixed can reverse (US corporate tax at 52.8% in 1968-69, a global minimum corporate tax, war in Europe, supply chains disintegrating between the US, Europe and China), so US multinationals' foreign revenues are not a reliable substitute for owning international equities.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C11-2|MT098-C11-2]] — Reserve currencies and superpowers rise and fall, so holding only S&P 500 stocks is a bet on continued US dominance; international revenues of US firms give partial diversification that might shrink with deglobalization.
- [[MT098 - Macro Investing - Understanding How And Why w- Cullen Roche (TIP486)#^MT098-C17-X1|MT098-C17-X1]] — A retail central bank digital currency with central bank lending would let government directly control credit and money creation, politicizing central banks and greatly reducing the role of private banks, which today create money competitively via loans.

## [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308]]
- [[MT099 - Major Market Moves for Trend Followers - Systematic Investor 308#^MT099-C08-X1|MT099-C08-X1]] — Bond futures were historically the main driver of trend-following returns, but after 2022 rates stopped trending and contributed little, which changes how a trend book's returns are composed.

## [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag]]
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C01-2|MT100-C01-2]] — The prohibited Siemens-Alstom rail merger was justified by high EU market shares; later evidence showed Chinese rail firms dominate only China, while Siemens and Alstom now have full order books, contradicting the claim that blocking the merger would cede the global market.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C01-X1|MT100-C01-X1]] — Europe regulates new technology more risk-aversely than other regions (e.g. requiring a human in the loop when municipalities use AI for welfare decisions, after a first scandal), a structural difference that shapes how fast tech products and AI uses reach the EU market.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C02-3|MT100-C02-3]] — Combining businesses to create scale in an unbalanced market (Airbus) is pro-competitive; the next Commission should look at consolidating the defense industry since defense budgets are rising and mostly spent outside Europe.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C03-1|MT100-C03-1]] — Europe lacks a true single capital and telecom market, so cross-border mergers cannot harvest efficiencies; the top four telecom operators serve about 60% of customers, but national regulation fragments scale, so a single market would produce larger telecom and bank groups.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C05-1|MT100-C05-1]] — European competitiveness is eroding slowly (a slow crisis unlike pandemic, war and energy crises where Europe acted fast) and requires market access, safe supply chains, permitting speed, research access and capital, not just competition policy.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C05-2|MT100-C05-2]] — Regulators (FTC, DOJ, CMA, EU) expect AI to accelerate market dynamics as seen in the first digitization wave, and coordinate on how AI will affect competition.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C06-2|MT100-C06-2]] — A single European capital market with EU rather than national enforcement would enable bank consolidation and larger banks able to serve huge conglomerates.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C07-1|MT100-C07-1]] — Europe's cheap Russian gas dependence carried a security premium; since 2022 it diversified to 10-12 suppliers with rapid LNG buildout, showing Europe can move fast in a crisis, and gas remains a transition fuel.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C07-2|MT100-C07-2]] — China's playbook for solar (joint ventures, then takeovers, closing its market as a build ground, then low-cost mass production) is being repeated in wind and batteries, threatening European producers; the foreign subsidies regulation lets the EU investigate subsidized tenders.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C07-3|MT100-C07-3]] — European banks recovered slowly from the financial crisis partly due to digitization, with no European champion banks (the biggest is about 20th globally).
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C07-X1|MT100-C07-X1]] — In European banking, consolidation seems to happen only after failure (Credit Suisse had to collapse before UBS could buy it), leaving Europe without global champion banks; its largest bank ranks about 20th worldwide.
- [[MT100 - Margrethe Vestager - Commissioner for Competition in the EU - Norges Bank Investment Manag#^MT100-C08-1|MT100-C08-1]] — The EU's China strategy of partnership, competition and systemic rivalry has merged competition and rivalry, shifting policy toward economic security (export controls, research due diligence in AI, semiconductors, biotech) as geopolitics became more hostile than ten years ago.

## [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322]]
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C06-2|MT101-C06-2]] — China's balance sheet recession means firms use cash to pay down debt, so its fiscal stimulus does not boost spending as much.
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C10-1|MT101-C10-1]] — US consumers hold fixed low-rate mortgages (housing gridlock), while floating-rate countries outside the US are more exposed to higher rates.
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C13-1|MT101-C13-1]] — Causal macro relations flip over time (bad news is good news, then good news is good news).
- [[MT101 - Market Narratives and the Unpredictable Alpha - Systematic Investor 322#^MT101-C16-2|MT101-C16-2]] — Trend following is dynamic beta (positive, negative or zero) rather than crisis alpha or unpredictable alpha.

## [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56]]
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C08-4|MT102-C08-4]] — Patterns decay: the September reverse barometer and small-cap January effect no longer work (small-cap outperformance now starts late December).
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C09-3|MT102-C09-3]] — January barometer exists because Congress, inauguration and White House priorities begin in January, so markets reflect the forecast for the year.
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C10-2|MT102-C10-2]] — Bitcoin acts opposite to its thesis (a levered Nasdaq with unclear closing price and no recourse on theft).
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C10-X10|MT102-C10-X10]] — DeFi failed to displace card networks because the cost of rebuilding a network like Visa's (200 million merchants, billions of users) from scratch is prohibitive.
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C11-1|MT102-C11-1]] — Super boom thesis: after war and inflation the market makes ~500% moves, enabled by a paradigm-shifting technology (car, interstate, microprocessor, now AI).
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C12-X13|MT102-C12-X13]] — Taking the 2009/2011 lows as the end of the last secular bear, a 500% move implies Dow about 62,430, reachable around 2030 at the Dow's long-run 8.5% average annual gain, with AI as the Windows-95-like productivity driver.
- [[MT102 - Market Patterns Work and Jeffrey Hirsch Explains Why - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep 56#^MT102-C13-1|MT102-C13-1]] — Almost all post-GFC US loan growth was in private credit, so a credit cycle tightening could slow the economy though banks are not at risk.

## [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G]]
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C02-X1|MT103-C02-X1]] — The 2025 bull case: lower taxes, deregulation, strong GDP and employment, plus AI, robotics and self-driving productivity gains, with productivity a key driver of GDP growth.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C03-3|MT103-C03-3]] — In 2024 mag-5 earnings grew 44% while the other 495 S&P companies grew 2%; consensus 2025 expects mag-5 21% and the rest 11%.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C04-2|MT103-C04-2]] — Big-tech capex rose from about 160bn to 260bn in 2024, absorbing most incremental free cash flow, and is forecast to keep growing.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C05-1|MT103-C05-1]] — Reasoning (chain of thought) models make inference demand jump enormously; Jensen expects inference to rise by 100,000x to a billion x, which requires new compute build-out.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C06-2|MT103-C06-2]] — A decade of unprecedented mag-7 free cash flow now goes to capex; what matters per Mike Mauboussin is return on incremental investment.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C06-X4|MT103-C06-X4]] — If AI can do every human cognitive task within 3-4 years (Elon), the value of replaced labor is in the trillions, which is why CEOs conclude they cannot avoid this level of investment.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C08-2|MT103-C08-2]] — Frontier labs need robust revenue (OpenAI about 4-5bn run-rate growing triple digits); smaller model companies have conceded they cannot keep up.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C08-X6|MT103-C08-X6]] — If Google and xAI follow OpenAI with paid premium tiers, subscription pricing becomes the industry norm, which is positive for OpenAI since it removes the assumption that frontier AI must be free like search.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C10-1|MT103-C10-1]] — Nvidia rose because earnings rose; consensus data center revenue of about 61bn in 2023 to near 200bn in 2025, a 60bn rise equal to hyperscaler capex increases.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C10-2|MT103-C10-2]] — Semiconductor returns diverged: Nvidia +140-150% while Intel and AMD fell; AI beneficiaries are concentrated, with memory (SK Hynix, Micron) seen as a bottleneck.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C10-X9|MT103-C10-X9]] — A shift of spend from pre-training to inference could favour distributed clusters and non-GPU chips (TPUs, startups); Brad replies these cannot ramp to scale soon and Google still buys GPUs. Nvidia's stock was flat from June 2024 on such debates (pre-training wall, DeepSeek).
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C11-1|MT103-C11-1]] — SK Hynix at about 6x forward earnings is priced as cyclical commodity memory; the thesis is secular demand, with rerating potential.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C11-2|MT103-C11-2]] — Power is the binding constraint on data center build-out; China builds 100x more power; regulatory relief and extending Diablo Canyon matter.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C17-1|MT103-C17-1]] — Expect more open-sourcing of models in 2025 after fears about regulatory capture.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C18-1|MT103-C18-1]] — Google faces an innovator's dilemma replacing a 99% margin search monopoly; Gemini integrated with Android and Google assets could be strong, but margins will be lower.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C18-2|MT103-C18-2]] — xAI's largest cluster tests the pre-training scaling argument; outcomes in H1 2025 (Grok 3) would be a new data point.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C19-1|MT103-C19-1]] — Large coherent clusters can be reused for reasoning training and inference, so GPU spending continues as workloads move to accelerated compute.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C19-X14|MT103-C19-X14]] — xAI's edge is not owning hardware but competence at standing up working compute quickly, plus Elon's low cost of capital and the ability to use its clusters across his companies (robotics, autonomy).
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C20-1|MT103-C20-1]] — AI labs needed hyperscaler capital, producing partial ownership by Google, Amazon and Microsoft; competition and co-opetition are aggressive.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C20-X15|MT103-C20-X15]] — Compute alone does not win: the lab with the better research and reasoning-model architecture wins even against a bigger cluster.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C21-1|MT103-C21-1]] — Nearly 300bn of corporate R&D/capex is bullish for US strategic position, answering earlier criticism about buybacks.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C21-2|MT103-C21-2]] — Reasoning models scale with test-time compute; labs say they are around the 'GPT-2' stage of log scaling, with big gains expected in 2025-2026; falling compute prices encourage more passes.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C21-X16|MT103-C21-X16]] — Chain-of-thought models are less efficient, which raises compute demand; if compute prices keep falling, running many passes (e.g. 20) per task becomes worthwhile, pushing usage up further.
- [[MT103 - Market Predictions, Rates & Inflation, DOGE, CES, AI Compute - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad G#^MT103-C22-1|MT103-C22-1]] — Expect coding agents in 2025 to be meaningfully better, then consumer agents with memory and actions as reasoning models improve; use case expands from researchers.

## [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368]]
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C03-3|MT104-C03-3]] — Differences vs 1999: US deficit not surplus, tariffs, weaker productivity, loss of US hegemony; yet government has bigger toolset and market structure supports.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C04-X1|MT104-C04-X1]] — Compared with 1998-99 the risks are bigger but so are the counter-forces: stronger government control and a larger toolset, plus market structure (structured products, flows into non-correlated vehicles) that reflexively supports prices.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C05-3|MT104-C05-3]] — Stock-bond correlation breakdown since 2022 removed bond diversification, driving flows into non-correlated strategies.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C06-2|MT104-C06-2]] — AI will make options and structured products accessible, boosting exponential growth and reflexive effects on markets.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C09-X1|MT104-C09-X1]] — Central-bank independence produced the Great Moderation and very low rates, which let governments borrow excessively and now lead people to give up that independence, an example of one regime creating the next.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C10-X1|MT104-C10-X1]] — Confidence in US institutions (rule of law, central-bank credibility, 'shining city on a hill') is eroding and is hard to rebuild, and Jem sees this as only the beginning of a loss of faith.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C11-3|MT104-C11-3]] — During 1968-1982 inflation era the best asset was gold (also most volatile, two-way); equities went nowhere because inflation hits valuation multiples; FX and precious metals are related.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C12-2|MT104-C12-2]] — AI betting is first-order thinking, like 1999 socks.com; winners are unknown, profits probably will not accrue to AI itself (commoditized) but to unique data sets and inputs (energy, power, data).
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C13-1|MT104-C13-1]] — The AI platform winner likely controls data; cheap sectors with unique data sets, e.g. UnitedHealth/healthcare, offer asymmetric payoff.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C13-2|MT104-C13-2]] — Options give a full distribution (3D trading) and will become accessible through AI; beneficiaries are brokers, exchanges (margin up with inflation), market makers and platforms.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C13-X1|MT104-C13-X1]] — Healthcare is inefficient because strict data-privacy rules lock up data, so owners of large unique healthcare data sets (such as UnitedHealth) hold undervalued AI-relevant assets.
- [[MT104 - Market Structure, Liquidity, and Reflexivity in 2025 - Systematic Investor - Ep.368#^MT104-C14-1|MT104-C14-1]] — AI commoditizes data edges for pod shops but a tide of new AI entrants may widen spreads, so winners and losers change.

## [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371]]
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C02-X1|MT105-C02-X1]] — AI models do only the abstract model-building (left-hemisphere) work and lack the feedback loop that checks models against the external environment, so they cannot recalibrate the way a brain does when misaligned (pain, fear).
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C03-1|MT105-C03-1]] — Prediction horizon in markets is short, like weather, because of reflexivity; AI adoption will compress prediction horizons further, defeating itself and favouring trends from structure.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C03-X1|MT105-C03-X1]] — Niels recalls well-known hedge fund managers (possibly Ken Griffin, Paul Tudor Jones) saying AI is far from producing alpha, with gains mainly in programming efficiency rather than alpha production.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C04-1|MT105-C04-1]] — Quoting Saxo's former chief strategist: electricity is the successor to oil as the core input to growth; shortage of power, cooling and grid raises inflation and stalls productivity.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C04-4|MT105-C04-4]] — Post-GFC to 2018 was the anomaly for trend; current decoupled, less coordinated central-bank environment is 'trend heaven', as long as everyday policy changes do not cause whipsaw ('death by a thousand cuts').
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C05-1|MT105-C05-1]] — QE and central bank coordination suppressed trends (compression, buy-the-dip) and stored energy released from 2020 like an avalanche; risk never went away.
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C08-2|MT105-C08-2]] — The S&P 500 futures had 32 five-sigma events in 30 years versus one per 170,000 years under Gaussian; all liquid markets show fat tails, consistent with power laws and fractal behavior (Mandelbrot).
- [[MT105 - Markets Aren’t Random- The Drivers Behind Trend Formation - Systematic Investor - Ep.371#^MT105-C17-2|MT105-C17-2]] — Investors should distinguish types of trend follower by objective; varieties differ in philosophy, which is gaining traction among allocators.

## [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39]]
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C02-3|MT106-C02-3]] — A physical-delivery futures contract is tied to a specified grade and delivery location, so futures and local spot prices can diverge.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C03-X1|MT106-C03-X1]] — Long-term core allocations do not need the intraday dealing an ETF wrapper offers (it can cost more than waiting for the daily NAV of a UCITS fund); the case for ETF versions of CTA strategies is practical: tickers fit model portfolios and tax-sheltered UK accounts.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C04-1|MT106-C04-1]] — UK tax-sheltered accounts (ISAs, SIPPs) limit holdings to shares and ETFs, creating demand for ETF versions of CTA strategies despite platform fees.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C08-1|MT106-C08-1]] — Many large London CTAs share the AHL origin and similar methodology.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C09-X1|MT106-C09-X1]] — Origins shape style: European CTAs descending from AHL were designed by physicists (Oxford/Cambridge) with a scientific approach, whereas many US CTAs grew out of floor trading, which gives a different way of looking at markets; neither is better or worse.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C11-1|MT106-C11-1]] — Slow trading means little alpha decay, so one model can run for years without a large research team finding new signals.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C11-3|MT106-C11-3]] — PhD-level skills are needed when diversifying into options, equities or HFT.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C14-X2|MT106-C14-X2]] — Portable alpha / return stacking is not new (1990s; Corey Hoffstein; Standpoint's 2020 launch proved the concept), yet few investors hold a meaningful trend allocation alongside equities, so adoption is still low.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C19-2|MT106-C19-2]] — Replication ETFs trade off cost savings against a simplification tax from limited contracts.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C20-1|MT106-C20-1]] — ETFs are reasonable as a poor man's CTA; no evidence either way versus real CTAs.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C21-2|MT106-C21-2]] — After adjustment, replication CTAs show no outperformance.
- [[MT106 - Markets Look Calm… But Something Feels Off... ft. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.39#^MT106-C21-3|MT106-C21-3]] — Simplification removes the edge; replication is very slow.

## [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333]]
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C04-1|MT107-C04-1]] — Markets are non-linear: long muted regimes alternate with explosive moves and large drawdowns, so average volatility is a poor description of risk.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C08-2|MT107-C08-2]] — The 60/40 portfolio rested on a negative or zero stock-bond correlation which turned positive, leaving it with no brakes.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C09-1|MT107-C09-1]] — Convexity depends on horizon; short-term managers and tail funds claim to offer more convexity, and trend followers' skew has reportedly declined.
- [[MT107 - Mastering Convexity! - Systematic Investor 333#^MT107-C13-2|MT107-C13-2]] — The range and probability of extreme events is judged higher now.

## [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12]]
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C04-3|MT108-C04-3]] — Equity indices have survival bias (losers removed) that truncates signals, so equities are excluded from long-term trend following.
- [[MT108 - Mastering Trend Following, Arbitrage, and Carry with J8 Capital - Open Interest 12#^MT108-C05-1|MT108-C05-1]] — Commodities have clear supply and demand fundamentals; shares are mainly speculative instruments once issued.

## [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the]]
- [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the#^MT109-C05-X2|MT109-C05-X2]] — China's economic strategy draws in US firms to acquire technology know-how and management expertise (it still lacks Western technological depth) and to gain leverage over US policy, so corporate exposure to China doubles as a political lever.
- [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the#^MT109-C06-X2|MT109-C06-X2]] — The post-WWII order built on sovereign nation-states and trade delivered record prosperity (GDP per capita at an all-time high), which Pompeo uses to argue for defending sovereignty and basic trade rules.
- [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the#^MT109-C08-1|MT109-C08-1]] — Industrial hard-tech investing now carries a geopolitical component in almost every project.
- [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the#^MT109-C10-1|MT109-C10-1]] — Security and property rights, including protection from inflation, underlie social mobility and prosperity.
- [[MT109 - Mike Pompeo, Former US Secretary of State & CIA Director, on Geopolitics, Leadership & the#^MT109-C12-1|MT109-C12-1]] — Pompeo expects US growth to come from entrepreneurs rather than government.

## [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C01-X1|MT110-C01-X1]] — The Greenspan era (late 1980s to 2006) is remembered as the Great Moderation, a long stretch of low inflation and steady growth that ran almost to the financial crisis, and the crisis is linked by many to the Fed's policy in the run-up.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C03-4|MT110-C03-4]] — Trend-following profits concentrate in a few markets each year (a dirty secret of diversification).
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C04-5|MT110-C04-5]] — Regression replicators can express exposure with 10-12 markets, but running a real trend system needs a much broader set for risk and opportunity.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C07-1|MT110-C07-1]] — NLP/LLMs convert earnings calls and Fed text into numerical signals, changing how markets react to announcements.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C08-3|MT110-C08-3]] — US data quality is falling (low survey response), which favours price-based over fundamental systems.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C10-2|MT110-C10-2]] — With poor data, quasi-fundamental value falls and pure price-based trading becomes more valuable.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C10-X2|MT110-C10-X2]] — Bond prices are more informative when the marginal buyers and sellers are profit-motivated rather than driven by policy or regulation, which is the market structure that favours trend followers and price discovery.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C10-X3|MT110-C10-X3]] — In the long run better official data reduces noise and tightens the link between economic fundamentals and prices, which would help data-driven systems, even though poor data hurts them in the short run.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C11-1|MT110-C11-1]] — AI productivity effect on trend following is neutral/unclear, though it could lift growth.
- [[MT110 - Most Investors Fear Uncertainty, But Systematic Traders Don’t - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT110-C11-X1|MT110-C11-X1]] — Global growth is currently relatively weak, so a productivity boom from AI would matter for fundamental prices more than for trend followers directly.

## [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336]]
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C03-1|MT111-C03-1]] — A lower coffee crop (estimated 4-5% below) is pushing coffee prices up, giving trend followers a new soft-commodity trend after cocoa.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C03-3|MT111-C03-3]] — The DAX trended up despite weak German economy because its constituents are multinationals earning abroad, so index trends can diverge from local macro.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C03-6|MT111-C03-6]] — European gas and power trended up in early 2025 because of low inventories, very cold weather and little wind output from German renewables, a supply shock after a range-bound 2024.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C04-2|MT111-C04-2]] — Markets with idiosyncratic drivers (Nord Pool hydro power driven by rainfall) give trends uncorrelated with macro markets, and more distinct price drivers improve trend-following diversification.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C05-4|MT111-C05-4]] — Alternative markets (per other managers' study) do not trend better than liquid futures, they trend at different times, which is the diversification benefit.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C06-2|MT111-C06-2]] — Alternative markets show a higher long-run Sharpe, attributed to better diversification rather than stronger trending.
- [[MT111 - Navigating Market Chaos - Systematic Investor 336#^MT111-C14-3|MT111-C14-3]] — Trend funds delevered after the GFC as investors wanted low-vol products; with higher rates and vol, some investors may now want more return.

## [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C02-1|MT112-C02-1]] — Modern trade rests on integrated global supply chains, so tariffs aimed at reshoring final-goods manufacturing cannot easily bring production back (Apple cannot build iPhones outside China), a headwind for tariff-driven policy aims.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C02-2|MT112-C02-2]] — Long-term inflation is inversely related to social trust in institutions (cited from Vincent Deluard on Jesse Felder's podcast), e.g. Switzerland has high trust and low inflation.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C02-R2|MT112-C02-R2]] — Policy makers rely on textbook comparative advantage and miss that modern trade is driven by integrated supply chains, which explains flawed tariff thinking.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C07-2|MT112-C07-2]] — Volatility differs by market (cocoa vs S&P), so a single volatility regime across a multi-asset trend portfolio is not well defined.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C08-1|MT112-C08-1]] — 2025 uncertainty was created by the state (tariffs, man-made), unlike past crises built on market excesses like housing and tech bubbles; hence different behaviour from earlier trend-friendly crises.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C09-1|MT112-C09-1]] — Low cross-asset dispersion (one driver, here tariffs) reduces diversification and trend profit; dispersion rose in 2025 but not to an extreme.
- [[MT112 - Navigating Uncertainty- Trend Following and Market Distortions - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT112-C15-3|MT112-C15-3]] — The safe-asset status of Treasury bills depends on issuer behaviour and changes over time, so substitutes such as trend following can serve as safe haven.

## [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini]]
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C02-1|MT113-C02-1]] — Inflation is no longer seen as a secular low (supply disruptions, geopolitics, fiscal expenditure), so central banks will be shyer about accommodative policy, leading to higher volatility and a harder time for 60/40.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C03-3|MT113-C03-3]] — The beta of asset returns to inflation is non-linear: higher inflation helps in deflation and hurts in high inflation, which is why regression coefficients look inconclusive.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C03-4|MT113-C03-4]] — A Wharton study splitting inflation by drivers finds core inflation is a large negative shock for asset prices while energy/headline ex-core is nearly unimportant, because energy inflation is volatile and mean-reverting.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C04-4|MT113-C04-4]] — Going forward stock-bond correlation is expected near zero, not the -0.5 of the previous 20 years that made 60/40 a bonanza.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C05-2|MT113-C05-2]] — Bonds with low returns and zero correlation to stocks in a period of active monetary policy are not the diversifiers they were.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C08-1|MT113-C08-1]] — Quant macro blends price-based signals with fundamental data, giving a more dynamically evolving positioning than pure trend.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C08-3|MT113-C08-3]] — The quant macro program has four sub-strategies: trend, value (long-run reversion), carry and macro fundamentals, combined by mean-variance; running eight years with Sharpe above one.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C08-4|MT113-C08-4]] — Fundamentals affect markets non-linearly and regime-dependently, so the macro signal uses about 900,000 time series, NLP sentiment and data compression with non-linear regression.
- [[MT113 - Navigating a New World Order- Macro Strategies for Uncertain Times - Pablo Calderini#^MT113-C08-R1|MT113-C08-R1]] — About 15% of the absolute-return business (trend and quant macro programs) is quantitative.

## [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311]]
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C05-1|MT114-C05-1]] — Because a replicator charges no incentive fees, it looks more volatile than the net-of-fee index it is compared with; fee and expense intermediation provides a tailwind.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C06-4|MT114-C06-4]] — Over a one-week window most CTA exposures reduce to a handful of large liquid instruments (10-year Treasury has gravitational pull), capturing about 90% of what they do.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C06-5|MT114-C06-5]] — Idiosyncratic markets such as cocoa are missed by the replicator, producing multi-week tracking gaps like Jan-Feb 2023, but this is not a structural failure.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C07-1|MT114-C07-1]] — Critics argued that 2016-22 was a Fed-dominated period of tightly linked instruments, after which idiosyncratic dispersion would produce unreplicable alpha; the replicator's results contradicted this.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C08-2|MT114-C08-2]] — The CTA index cannot be invested in directly; allocating to 20 managers at full fees or choosing a few funds are alternatives, hence replication.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C09-1|MT114-C09-1]] — In 2024 many managers made new highs early so performance fees kicked in, and with quarterly crystallisation no fees came back in Q2, so replicators with no incentive fee outperform in such years.
- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C13-X1|MT114-C13-X1]] — Daily-liquidity regulated wrappers (UCITS, US ETFs) bring extra independent checks and let investors exit quickly, so allocators run lighter and shorter due diligence on them than on hedge funds.
