- [[MT114 - New CTA Index being revealed! - Systematic Investor 311#^MT114-C16-2|MT114-C16-2]] — Institutional allocators often pay less than full fees and thus beat the CTA index; fee disintermediation in an investable index (hundreds of bp better) could support larger allocations.

## [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014]]
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C01-1|MT115-C01-1]] — Brent and WTI are sympathetic not parallel markets; their spread tends to trend, with different characteristics (Cushing delivery location).
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C02-3|MT115-C02-3]] — Chinese gasoil demand growth, a proxy for construction activity, has dropped sharply, signalling the Chinese slowdown.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C02-4|MT115-C02-4]] — Shale technology lifts oil recovered per rig (Bakken) and cuts costs, so supply growth accelerates and is cost-effective.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C02-5|MT115-C02-5]] — Rising LNG, LPG and ethane exports from the Gulf Coast displace crude in petrochemicals, adding to the demand story.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C03-4|MT115-C03-4]] — Oil stayed at $110 for 3.5 years because about 2 mb/d of supply disruption (Libya, Syria, Iraq, Iran sanctions) offset US shale growth while OPEC covered about 1 mb/d annual demand growth from spare capacity.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C05-1|MT115-C05-1]] — Marginal cost falls with technology: natural gas was said to need $7 in 2008 and now $3, so shale oil's marginal cost will also fall, implying a bear market.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C05-3|MT115-C05-3]] — Short-run oil demand is very inelastic: a 10% price move shifts demand about 0.15%, so price must move a lot to rebalance.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C06-1|MT115-C06-1]] — Asian refining is oversupplied and under pressure; 80-85% of new refinery investment is government strategic, so private firms compete with non-economic decision makers.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C06-2|MT115-C06-2]] — Australia's refining system is likely to shut down, turning it into a product importer, a surprise for product trade flows.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C06-3|MT115-C06-3]] — US refineries benefit from cheap crude but the advantage has a shelf life of three to seven years.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C06-X1|MT115-C06-X1]] — Even high-quality Asian refiners (Korea) must export at least half their output, and with gasoline-oriented capacity, a slowdown in Chinese and Indian demand growth and new refineries about to start in Abu Dhabi and Saudi Arabia, Asian refining margins are under pressure before the new capacity even arrives.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C07-1|MT115-C07-1]] — Large exporters like Aramco may have more products than crude, shifting pricing power from crude to products.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C07-2|MT115-C07-2]] — The arbitrage between Asian gas ($15-19/mmBtu) and US gas ($3) and the BTU value dislocation between crude, gas and coal is unsustainable and will reshape refining and LPG economics.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C07-3|MT115-C07-3]] — At $60-70 oil few new projects (Canada, Africa, Russia) are economic, constraining future supply; renewables need higher subsidies.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C07-4|MT115-C07-4]] — No single barrel (West African etc.) is the marginal barrel; supply constantly reshuffles regionally.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C07-X1|MT115-C07-X1]] — Lower crude cuts the price of oil-indexed gas automatically but not enough to close the gap between Asian and US gas, so the regional gas arbitrage will have to adjust further and affects the economics of new projects.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C08-1|MT115-C08-1]] — Gas is like marriage and oil like dating: LNG volumes are sold under long contracts, so US LNG will come regardless of price and oil-indexed contracts last to 2035, creating dual markets.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C08-3|MT115-C08-3]] — Dollar strength offset the crude price collapse in emerging markets, so expected demand elasticity has not yet appeared.
- [[MT115 - New Challenges in the High Stakes World of Oil Trading - Oil & Money 2014#^MT115-C08-X1|MT115-C08-X1]] — Asian LNG buyers honour long-term contracts (no arbitration or court case in 40 years), unlike European buyers, so dual oil-indexed and spot gas prices can persist for years and US LNG volumes already sold are safe despite lower oil.

## [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo]]
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C03-1|MT116-C03-1]] — Asset reactions to a narrative or macro theme are conditional, appearing only beyond a threshold, so exposure should be measured conditionally.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C04-2|MT116-C04-2]] — Dispersion in 2025 trend programs came mainly from which markets were traded (universe breadth) and trend speed; faster and more diversified programs lagged.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C06-1|MT116-C06-1]] — If volatilities and correlations are stable, trend lookback speed drives more performance dispersion between programs than sizing method (dynamic vs static, correlation use).
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C06-3|MT116-C06-3]] — Over roughly the last four years a concentrated universe (DBMF-like) outperformed a broad 80 to 100 market universe, which Baltas had not seen before in the 25-year history.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C09-1|MT116-C09-1]] — Over about 20 years every trend sector has been profitable but contribution rotates over time, so a full-breadth portfolio is the more intuitive design.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C10-4|MT116-C10-4]] — Institutions can now access trend through CTAs, mutual funds, UCITS, ETFs and QIS, reflecting more mainstream demand.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C14-2|MT116-C14-2]] — Trend following can profit even when returns are independent if volatility is serially dependent, so autocorrelation of returns is not required.
- [[MT116 - Nick Baltas on Trend Following in 2026- Signals, Structure & Strategy - Systematic Investo#^MT116-C15-1|MT116-C15-1]] — Even when returns are iid, a trend follower earns positive expected payoff conditional on a deep drawdown beyond a threshold, which explains crisis alpha without serial correlation.

## [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv]]
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C01-1|MT117-C01-1]] — Cocoa was historically one of the largest negative contributors to trend following, yet its 2024 rally helped, which defends holding markets that have never trended well.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C12-1|MT117-C12-1]] — A price process can look trending with zero serial correlation when volatility clusters and creates skewness, and this alone supports trend following.
- [[MT117 - Nick Baltas talks Signal Strength, Trend Following & Portfolio Strategies - Systematic Inv#^MT117-C12-2|MT117-C12-2]] — High volatility is not a necessary condition for strong trend results: among conventional asset classes lower-volatility assets such as bond duration have historically delivered stronger trend Sharpe ratios.

## [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11]]
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C02-1|MT118-C02-1]] — Mathematical models simplify a complex reality driven by supply, demand and constraints, so a single momentum driver is not enough and other approaches (relative value, behavioural models) can add return.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C06-2|MT118-C06-2]] — G10 FX returns versus USD are about 80 to 90% explained by three stable factors (all-vs-dollar, carry, Europe vs rest), a rare stationary stylised fact.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C10-3|MT118-C10-3]] — A universe of about 75 futures contains only about 19 independent degrees of freedom, a compression that has grown since 2008 as markets became globally linked and liquidated together.
- [[MT118 - Non-Trend Strategies, Trader Flows, and Risk Management at DCM - Open Interest 11#^MT118-C12-3|MT118-C12-3]] — Markets lack stationarity because participants change behaviour and arbitrage away patterns, so patterns from 10 years ago may no longer hold, unlike protein folding rules.

## [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341]]
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C02-3|MT119-C02-3]] — Tariff gaps between New York and London delivery (e.g. gold, copper) make contract specification and delivery location matter for trend portfolio construction, not just liquidity.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C03-2|MT119-C03-2]] — Cycles have lengthened because the economy is more service-oriented, so inventory-driven swings matter less.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C03-X2|MT119-C03-X2]] — Since World War II recoveries have lasted longer and less time is spent in recession than before, which Mark attributes to fewer forced policy errors.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C06-3|MT119-C06-3]] — Gold's rise despite lower inflation reflects gold ETFs lowering transaction costs (financialisation, per Harvey research) and large central-bank buying, especially China, as demand for an asset governments cannot appropriate.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C06-X3|MT119-C06-X3]] — A key macro-finance question is what counts as a safe asset and whether the supply of safe assets such as T-bills is large enough; broader safe assets (not seizable, usable to hide wealth from sanctions) help explain gold demand.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C08-X2|MT119-C08-X2]] — The market reaction reversed: December 2024 saw the Trump trade with equities up and gold down, the opposite of the first months of 2025, which is a reason to doubt stable cross-asset relationships in a new regime.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C14-2|MT119-C14-2]] — Portable alpha has come and gone because the alpha component often disappointed, not the portability; the trend is towards efficient capital use and factor rather than asset-class allocation.
- [[MT119 - Nothing Good Happens Below the 200-Day Moving Average - Systematic Investor 341#^MT119-C15-X1|MT119-C15-X1]] — Portable alpha and return-stacking has become more attractive because fees have fallen, the market is more competitive and alpha products are better, so for large institutions it beats handing all cash directly to a manager.

## [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3]]
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C03-1|MT120-C03-1]] — The Hawaii pension fund stopped using asset buckets and evaluated investments individually for portfolio contribution.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C03-2|MT120-C03-2]] — AI data centres drive large electricity demand (US 183 TWh, expected to double in 4 to 5 years; 26% of Virginia supply), making electricity prices a plausible new inflation source.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C03-X1|MT120-C03-X1]] — Ed Yardeni's post reports data-centre build spending up about 400% in two years, with the Microsoft CEO saying the bottleneck is not chips but the facilities to house them (caption says 'sensors'), underpinning the electricity demand story.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C03-X2|MT120-C03-X2]] — Inflation can re-emerge in pockets such as electricity, US healthcare and insurance costs; price increases arrive unevenly but drift upward, so inflation remains an important topic for the next few years.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C07-1|MT120-C07-1]] — Advanced or machine-learning parameter adaptation may add some value over classic moving-average rules, but the past may not resemble the present (e.g. bonds under a divided Fed).
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C08-2|MT120-C08-2]] — Liberation Day was the first time in the studied history that magnitude surprise lasted three consecutive days, a sustained shock that is hard for trend systems because it is not in the backtest.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C10-1|MT120-C10-1]] — Many macro managers' returns correlate highly with trend following because both capture global trends across asset classes by different entry methods.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C11-3|MT120-C11-3]] — A Capgemini article projects alternatives rising from about $4 trillion to $12 trillion by 2034 (Everest Group sees $30 trillion by 2030), with high-net-worth investors at 20 to 25% and family offices at 30 to 50% allocation.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C12-1|MT120-C12-1]] — Fiduciaries giving trend zero or 1% allocation are hard to justify since no paper argues trend fails to add value to a portfolio, and the shift toward private wealth access may raise allocations.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C12-2|MT120-C12-2]] — Private wealth and model-portfolio investors are gaining access to alternatives through ETFs and new products as concern over 60/40 grows.
- [[MT120 - October Market Recap- Missing Data, Strong Equities, and more - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT120-C13-X1|MT120-C13-X1]] — Private equity and private credit are not truly alternative, being familiar equity and credit exposure in a different wrapper, whereas true alternatives such as trend following offer a different return source.

## [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P]]
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C03-1|MT121-C03-1]] — Allocators moved from share classes of manager funds to funds of one to get bespoke terms, leverage, concentration or removal of exposures, which is the first step of customization.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C03-3|MT121-C03-3]] — An internal managed-account platform gives the allocator control of the portfolio and scale efficiencies with prime brokers and counterparties while managers only trade.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-1|MT121-C04-1]] — Separately managed accounts became favored because investors wanted to own assets segregated, a preference reinforced after the GFC when managers failed and portfolio construction burden fell on investors.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-2|MT121-C04-2]] — A long equity bull market made anything competing with equities look poor, which pushed allocators to find new ways of building portfolios and thus customization.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-3|MT121-C04-3]] — As asset management proliferated, much former alpha became cheap beta and alpha is zero-sum with too many managers chasing it, making alpha harder and costlier to harvest.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-4|MT121-C04-4]] — The shift to a total fund approach moved value added from manager selection to total-portfolio construction, which also drives customization.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-5|MT121-C04-5]] — If the equity bull market continues, active managers struggle to add on the upside so manager selection matters less and index-type beta strategies grow.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-6|MT121-C04-6]] — Surviving managers will be those who integrate with allocators' internal teams as an extension of them, since the fee pie available to managers shrinks.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-X1|MT121-C04-X1]] — Ontario Teachers was an early driver of manager customization in absolute return: before the GFC it teamed up with managers and expected them to step up on risk management, liquidity, portfolio construction and risk-adjusted returns.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C04-X2|MT121-C04-X2]] — Until about five or six years ago most institutional hedge fund assets stayed in commingled vehicles; since then investors have shifted to customization, direct investments and strategic relationships run by dedicated teams, especially at Canadian pension plans.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C05-1|MT121-C05-1]] — In the alternative scenario of a decent market correction or decoupling, manager selection regains importance and customization progresses more slowly.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C05-2|MT121-C05-2]] — Minimum useful size for a separately managed account rose from under 4 million in the 2000s to above 50 million now, showing the industry matured.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C05-5|MT121-C05-5]] — In the 2000s boards allocated to hedge funds as one asset class with little strategy-level customization; post-GFC investors began specifying which strategies they wanted.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C05-X1|MT121-C05-X1]] — About half of Graham's business is managed accounts, which shows customised accounts have become mainstream for systematic macro managers.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C05-X2|MT121-C05-X2]] — In the 1990s a trade journal's managed-account reports covered only managers with more than 50 million of assets and accounts could be as small as 5 or 10 million, illustrating how much the industry has matured.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C07-1|MT121-C07-1]] — A total fund approach changes organization: senior staff are generalists who think about the overall portfolio rather than their own asset-class bucket.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C07-2|MT121-C07-2]] — Total fund portfolios hold fewer external managers because exposures that overlap across asset-class buckets are visible and need not be duplicated.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C07-4|MT121-C07-4]] — Large investors using a total fund approach report significant cost savings from fewer, larger manager relationships with better fee terms.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C08-3|MT121-C08-3]] — Trend following can now be obtained as cheap beta, so only very differentiated managers justify 2 and 20 fees.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C08-4|MT121-C08-4]] — SAA is driven by asset-class benchmarks that managers aim to beat by 50 to 100 bps, whereas total portfolio approach is holistic and judged on the whole portfolio.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C08-5|MT121-C08-5]] — A total portfolio approach organizes the portfolio by macro risk factors (inflation, real rates, credit) and treats asset classes as secondary labels.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C08-X1|MT121-C08-X1]] — The total portfolio approach spread partly because institutions grew internal investment teams, so allocators can integrate risk, construction and performance across areas; allocators now deal with multiple specialist teams.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C08-X2|MT121-C08-X2]] — The total portfolio approach is dynamic, an ongoing asset-allocation process, whereas strategic asset allocation is a forward-looking fixed-assumption exercise based on long-term capital market assumptions.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C09-4|MT121-C09-4]] — Completion portfolios invert the manager-investor relationship into a partnership where much time is spent formulating the problem before finding the solution.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C09-X1|MT121-C09-X1]] — Caveat: only a few plans (a handful) use the total portfolio approach today, consistent with the poll in which strategic asset allocation was used more, but it is expected to spread as the landscape grows more complex and internal teams more skilled.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C10-5|MT121-C10-5]] — Interest in completion-type customized solutions cannot be predicted by allocator size because many small plans are sophisticated.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C11-1|MT121-C11-1]] — A peer review found total-portfolio management and risk mitigation are applied by relatively small and mid-size plans worldwide, not only the largest ones.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C11-7|MT121-C11-7]] — ESG equity index futures are now available for systematic macro and trend followers to implement ESG preferences in futures.
- [[MT121 - Optimizing Investment Alpha with Customized Completion Portfolios Featuring Chris Jones, P#^MT121-C13-X1|MT121-C13-X1]] — The CFA Institute published the first global voluntary ESG disclosure standards on 1 November, but with so many frameworks the standards themselves need standardizing, so ESG impact remains hard to compare.

## [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C01-2|MT122-C01-2]] — The shift to index investing means fewer owners scrutinize companies, which creates the room for activists to push change.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C01-3|MT122-C01-3]] — As index investing grows, Wall Street research coverage declines, so firms that do their own primary research become better informed.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C01-X1|MT122-C01-X1]] — Before acting, Elliott studies the local culture of capitalism and corporate control in each country, plus how the board and management got there and why they are failing, because receptiveness to shareholder-driven change varies by market.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C02-2|MT122-C02-2]] — Scale of a single large fund (about 72 billion) gives the capital and legal capacity to back a thesis and make engagement credible.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C03-1|MT122-C03-1]] — Fewer shareholders act as owners and fewer companies accept owner input, which makes accountability-demanding investors rare and gives them an edge.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C03-X1|MT122-C03-X1]] — Modern activism differs from greenmail: when the activist wins, all shareholders gain, and accountability campaigns are rare because few investors still act as owners.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C07-6|MT122-C07-6]] — Government support for crypto creates an alternative to sovereign money and thus to the dollar's reserve currency role, which other countries may welcome.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C07-7|MT122-C07-7]] — Many countries resent the privilege of the US as the reserve currency issuer and would like alternatives.
- [[MT122 - Paul Singer - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT122-C07-X2|MT122-C07-X2]] — Central bank money, though created from nothing, has sovereign backing because taxes and the army are paid in it, so government embrace of cryptocurrencies means endorsing alternatives to sovereign money and risks undermining the dollar's reserve role; Singer finds it paradoxical that the US itself supports an alternative.

## [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C02-6|MT123-C02-6]] — Cocoa demand is extremely inelastic historically, so the price was capped at $2,500-3,000 by supply, not demand, and a supply shortfall must be cleared by price alone.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C02-7|MT123-C02-7]] — Back-of-envelope: 5 million tons of demand over 8 billion people means a few cents per person per day even at very high cocoa prices, so higher prices barely hit consumers' budgets and do not cut consumption.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C02-X1|MT123-C02-X1]] — Pass-through is muted because milk chocolate contains under 10% cocoa: a tenfold rise in cocoa roughly doubles a bar's price, and even a heavy high-cocoa chocolate eater pays only about $10 a month more (a moderate eater $2 to $5), so higher cocoa prices do not reduce consumption.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C03-1|MT123-C03-1]] — Cocoa production is concentrated near a latitude band needing specific temperature, rainfall and humidity: 70% West Africa, 21% Latin America, 5% Asia and Oceania, so local shocks hit global supply.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C03-3|MT123-C03-3]] — Climate change steadily shifts weather patterns unfavorably for cocoa, a structural drag on supply that does not reverse with a good season.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C03-4|MT123-C03-4]] — Swollen shoot virus (known since 1936, spread by mealybugs) cuts yield 25% in a year and 50% in two and kills trees in 3 to 4 years; its spread over the last year is a structural supply loss.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C03-5|MT123-C03-5]] — Black pod fungus spreads by rain splash in wet seasons, so heavy rain such as in El Nino years raises disease losses.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C03-6|MT123-C03-6]] — Ivory Coast farmers used less fertilizer due to high prices and Russia-Ukraine shortages, cutting yields; it will not reverse soon because farmers still receive low prices.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C03-7|MT123-C03-7]] — Farmer-gate prices stayed low, so farmers cannot afford inputs and no supply response from fertilizer is expected: Andurand sees the supply problems as largely structural rather than weather.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C04-3|MT123-C04-3]] — If a price rise no longer triggers a quick supply response (cocoa trees take 4-5 years), the market can clear only through demand, requiring a much larger price rise.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C06-4|MT123-C06-4]] — Because inventories are very low, prices cannot drop back to old levels; stocks must be rebuilt across the supply chain, which keeps a demand floor.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C07-1|MT123-C07-1]] — Beyond random weather, climate change creates a trending structural impact on soft commodity production that offers theme opportunities beyond cocoa.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C07-2|MT123-C07-2]] — Tree crops take 3-5 years to respond to price versus immediate replanting for grains, so supply response is slow.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C07-4|MT123-C07-4]] — Cocoa is easier to analyze than grains because production is concentrated in one mega producer and one large producer, so a few data points give a good read on next year's crop.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C07-5|MT123-C07-5]] — For 15-20 years copper was balanced, with demand and supply each growing about 0.5 million tons a year.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C08-1|MT123-C08-1]] — Electrification of transport, grid expansion for renewables (solar, wind) and data centers add new copper demand, much copper-intensive versus nuclear or fossil power.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C08-2|MT123-C08-2]] — Despite weaker Chinese property demand, copper demand grows about 1 million tons a year while mine supply growth goes to zero or negative within 12-18 months, producing a widening multi-year deficit.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C08-4|MT123-C08-4]] — New copper mines take 10-15 years and growth is shifting to less stable countries, so supply cannot respond quickly to price.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C08-5|MT123-C08-5]] — Copper demand is inelastic because copper is a small fraction of end-product cost and the energy transition must proceed regardless of price, so the price move needed to balance the market is large.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C09-2|MT123-C09-2]] — Scrap supply is the only quick supply response to higher copper prices, while mine supply does not respond for years; demand shifting to later is limited.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C09-5|MT123-C09-5]] — Deficits accumulate to more than 1 million tons in 2026 and over 3 million tons by 2030, exceeding inventory buffers.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C11-2|MT123-C11-2]] — US oil supply growth is set by geology and capital rather than who is president, and US output is probably near peak, so the election matters marginally for production.
- [[MT123 - Pierre Andurand Says the World Could Run Out of Cocoa Inventories - Odd Lots#^MT123-C12-2|MT123-C12-2]] — Tree-based commodities have different market structure from field crops because labor intensity and difficulty of automation shape supply response.

## [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C01-X1|MT124-C01-X1]] — The combined shock (very tight inventories, war, sanctions and self-sanctions) has no close precedent in recent decades, so historical analogues give little guidance on how far prices can move.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C02-1|MT124-C02-1]] — Before the invasion, oil already had very low inventories, little OPEC spare capacity, low expected supply growth and strong post-Covid demand, so fundamentals were very strong independent of Russia.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C04-2|MT124-C04-2]] — US shale has already drilled much of the easy oil and fields are maturing, so about 10 more years of strong supply is possible but not decades of high growth.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C04-3|MT124-C04-3]] — US shale producers burned about $600 billion of cash while growing production, so shareholders lost heavily.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C04-4|MT124-C04-4]] — Now profitable, shale producers face shareholder and climate pressure to keep capital discipline, which limits the supply response to high prices.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C04-5|MT124-C04-5]] — Pressure on oil supply to fall has outrun alternatives for consumers, so oil demand cannot shift quickly and shortages result.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C05-1|MT124-C05-1]] — Replacing combustion cars with EVs is limited by grid power supply, especially in Europe, which slows decarbonization of oil demand.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C05-2|MT124-C05-2]] — Metal supply is too small for fast electrification because miners underinvested under ESG pressure, and renewables, batteries and EVs are copper-intensive.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C05-3|MT124-C05-3]] — Decarbonization needs much higher metals prices to induce the supply growth required.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C05-X1|MT124-C05-X1]] — The decarbonization build-out (renewables, EVs, charging) is a roughly 20-year transition that cannot replace lost fossil supply overnight, so a sudden loss of Russian oil must be met by other fossil supply or by lower demand; the system is already at capacity in the short term.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C06-4|MT124-C06-4]] — Europe, especially Germany and Italy, depends on Russian gas and may ration demand and accelerate the energy transition, which in turn needs metals.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C07-3|MT124-C07-3]] — Short-run oil demand is inelastic because consumers cannot replace cars quickly, so demand destruction comes from economic crises, not price alone.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C07-X1|MT124-C07-X1]] — Accelerating the European energy transition to cut Russian gas use needs more metals, but Russia is a big metals exporter, so the sanctions shock tightens the very supply the substitution depends on.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C09-1|MT124-C09-1]] — Many OPEC+ members, especially African ones, cannot fill quotas because of underinvestment, so quota increases do not translate into barrels.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C09-2|MT124-C09-2]] — Real spare production capacity (sustainable for 1-2 years) is only about 1.5 mb/d and sits with Saudi Arabia, Kuwait and the UAE.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C09-4|MT124-C09-4]] — Gold-backed or crypto-based currency does not protect against sanctions because reserves can be frozen, so the dollar's reserve status is not easily displaced.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C09-5|MT124-C09-5]] — Strong currencies need rule of law, low corruption, freedom and deep financial markets, which China lacks as it holds large US Treasuries and capital controls.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C10-3|MT124-C10-3]] — Supply response time differs: agriculture within a year, metals 7-15 years, and new oil projects about 7 years, so long-lead supply cannot fix near-term shortages.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C10-4|MT124-C10-4]] — New non-shale oil projects take about 7 years, and hesitation about demand in 2029-30 discourages long-lead investment.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C10-X1|MT124-C10-X1]] — Metals and minerals supply cannot respond for at least five years after a production decision; permitting will probably be faster at much higher prices, but there is no short-term fix for next year or two years out.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C11-2|MT124-C11-2]] — Over the next decade commodity supply, not just price, will constrain economic growth; economic models that assume unlimited commodity supply are wrong.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C11-3|MT124-C11-3]] — Bottlenecks in other commodities (steel, sand), labour shortages and low unemployment hinder new oil supply, so tight markets reinforce each other.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C11-4|MT124-C11-4]] — Unlike 2008-2011, when shale then helped, there may be no equivalent supply response now, so the current setup is more bullish than 2008.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C11-X1|MT124-C11-X1]] — Because supply cannot respond quickly, oil prices have to stay high enough for a prolonged period to keep demand down, treating oil as a luxury product and pushing the energy transition forward.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C11-X2|MT124-C11-X2]] — US shale responds fastest, about 12 months from capex decision to oil, because the wells, infrastructure and service-company capacity already exist; other supply regions lack these so respond later.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C12-1|MT124-C12-1]] — Oil use per unit of GDP is 15% lower than in 2008, so the economic impact of $150 in 2008 is closer to that of about $250 today.
- [[MT124 - Pierre Andurand on How We Might Get $200 a Barrel Oil - Odd Lots#^MT124-C12-X1|MT124-C12-X1]] — Money cannot compress supply lead times (drilling, mines, renewables), so a large shock produces long lead times for supply to come back and keeps the market destabilized longer than financing alone would suggest.

## [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots]]
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C01-1|MT125-C01-1]] — Price caps and subsidies do not add gas or oil supply, so the energy crisis depends on physical shortage and weather, not financial measures.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C02-1|MT125-C02-1]] — Europe nearly doubled LNG imports, covering about two thirds of lost Russian gas, and new import capacity is coming, so import capacity is not the constraint.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C02-3|MT125-C02-3]] — If Europe captures about 40% of global LNG additions (a big bump in 2025) it can replace much of the Russian gas, though not all within two years, so a demand response is needed.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C02-4|MT125-C02-4]] — Gas demand split is about 50% residential/commercial (mostly heating), 25% industrial and 20% power, which shows where cuts can be made.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C03-2|MT125-C03-2]] — European LNG import terminals were running at about 71% of capacity with floating units arriving, so terminals are not binding; the constraint is global LNG supply.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C03-3|MT125-C03-3]] — Global LNG supply of about 550 bcm grows only 15 bcm per year for two years and then about 60 bcm a year, so Europe would have to take about 40% of additions and live with cooler temperatures for two years.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C04-2|MT125-C04-2]] — Some industrial uses (chemicals, glass, petrochemicals) need gas as feedstock and cannot switch, but many can use naphtha or oil, so industrial demand destruction is partial; strategic industries need government support.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C04-4|MT125-C04-4]] — Europe needs massive investment in nuclear for base-load low-carbon capacity, but plants take years to build; French reactors at 40 years need maintenance.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C04-5|MT125-C04-5]] — Hot summers force nuclear reactors cooled by rivers or sea to shut down, reducing power supply in heat waves worldwide.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C07-2|MT125-C07-2]] — There is almost no spare capacity; Saudi Arabia is producing 11 mb/d, a level never sustained for more than a month before, and most OPEC members are at maximum.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C07-3|MT125-C07-3]] — US shale growth should slow from 1-1.5 mb/d a year to about 0.5 mb/d a year, so supply growth is limited.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C07-4|MT125-C07-4]] — Shale response is weak due to depleted best acreage, supply-chain and fracking-equipment constraints and shareholder pressure for restraint.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C07-5|MT125-C07-5]] — Shale wells decline about 80% in the first year, so producers must keep drilling just to hold output, and cutting drilling quickly reduces production.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C09-1|MT125-C09-1]] — The US should supply about half of global LNG additions over three to four years, so US gas producers with export access benefit.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C09-2|MT125-C09-2]] — Until new US export terminals open (2026-27) energy prices stay high and moving cargoes remains highly profitable.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C09-3|MT125-C09-3]] — Thematic positioning for the European energy redesign: long US gas producers with export capacity.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C09-4|MT125-C09-4]] — Energy security should boost uranium, nuclear reactor builders, solar and wind as Europe re-architects supply.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C11-X1|MT125-C11-X1]] — Gas-to-oil switching is the cheapest substitution route because oil is now even cheaper than coal per unit of energy, so switching away from gas supports oil demand rather than coal.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C12-1|MT125-C12-1]] — Russian production will decline with time because sanctions block equipment, expertise and investment, plus mobilization effects.
- [[MT125 - Pierre Andurand on What Europe Needs to Do This Winter - Odd Lots#^MT125-C12-3|MT125-C12-3]] — Over almost 30 years energy took a falling share of income, so households heated homes more warmly, meaning there is room to cut without much hardship.

## [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338]]
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C01-1|MT126-C01-1]] — Non-US markets have started to outperform as the US-exceptionalism premium cracks.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C02-1|MT126-C02-1]] — The world is shifting from cooperative give-now-for-later-gain relations to transactional bargaining, driven by a transactional US leader.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C02-2|MT126-C02-2]] — Power is being used simply because it can be, a broader populism-driven loss of norms with wide consequences.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C07-2|MT126-C07-2]] — Demographic turnover from boomers to millennials makes populism a 10 to 15 year theme that policies cannot easily reverse.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C07-X1|MT126-C07-X1]] — Responding to populist anger with supply-side policy that favours the top deepens the anger it came from, and voters stayed unhappy even during better years because of accumulated inflation, so the political cycle (10 to 15 years) keeps swinging against incumbents.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C10-2|MT126-C10-2]] — Gold's relevance for solving US debt is limited today.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C10-4|MT126-C10-4]] — Europe's reliance on the US made it complacent and unwilling to make hard decisions; the defence shock forces resilience.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C11-2|MT126-C11-2]] — Rearmament and harder decisions are expected to get Europe out of its stagnation.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C11-X1|MT126-C11-X1]] — The world is expected to be dangerous for about 20 years, so allies such as Europe and Japan need their own larger militaries; the rearmament in Europe is a necessary long-run adjustment.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C12-1|MT126-C12-1]] — Large TSMC US investments may reduce the US appetite to defend Taiwan.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C12-3|MT126-C12-3]] — China could move on Taiwan (embargo or pressure rather than military) around Q4 2025, once the Ukraine deal is done and the US is in recession.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C12-X1|MT126-C12-X1]] — The US withdrawing military support from Ukraine signals it will not defend Taiwan either, removing deterrence on China; the main remaining constraints are China's timing and incentives, so risk is higher once the Ukraine deal is done.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C14-1|MT126-C14-1]] — Russia has supplied Turkey energy on credit, building a debt Putin can call as leverage.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C14-2|MT126-C14-2]] — If Russia and the West reconcile, swing states like Turkey and India lose bargaining power and market support.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C15-2|MT126-C15-2]] — Theory: the Biden administration let Turkish-backed forces take Damascus to give Turkey a counterweight to Russia in Syria.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C16-3|MT126-C16-3]] — Oil is bullish multi-year due to supply issues, geopolitics and deglobalisation, despite drill-baby-drill pressure.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C16-5|MT126-C16-5]] — Gold behaves as a currency rather than an industrial commodity and is unrelated to demand cycles, unlike oil.
- [[MT126 - Politics, Markets & Power Plays - Systematic Investor 338#^MT126-C16-X1|MT126-C16-X1]] — Oil is expected to grind higher back toward the middle of its multi-year range rather than spike, supported by Middle East unrest, Russia not stopping, a weaker dollar and China demand, with low volatility, which is why long calls on gold and short oil puts fit better than long oil volatility.

## [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep]]
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C05-4|MT127-C05-4]] — Alternative markets (Nordic and Italian power) were more rangebound than crude and heating oil in 2026, so market universe drives trend performance dispersion.
- [[MT127 - Portable Alpha, Trend Following & AI Investing ft. Harry Moore - Systematic Investor - Ep#^MT127-C10-1|MT127-C10-1]] — Trend on futures needs only about $20-25 of capital per $100 exposure, so allocators can add trend without selling existing holdings.

## [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386]]
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C07-1|MT128-C07-1]] — Only about 30% of silver supply is mined as silver, so supply is less controllable and the market more volatile than gold.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C07-4|MT128-C07-4]] — Commodities in a trend universe provide opportunities often independent of equity risk-off events.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C13-1|MT128-C13-1]] — The Man AHL paper classifies trend markets by liquidity and complexity into traditional, alternative and esoteric (China futures, physical crypto) and studies where returns come from.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C14-X1|MT128-C14-X1]] — January 2026 trend profits did not come from the risk-on/risk-off factor (bonds flat, equities slightly up) but from idiosyncratic sources such as gold, silver and natural gas, an example of returns from alternative-style markets.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C16-1|MT128-C16-1]] — Futures trading uses only about 20-30% of cash as margin, making CTAs cash efficient; alternative markets need more margin.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C17-3|MT128-C17-3]] — FT Alphaville thesis: finance quants and AI labs converge in workflow, hiring and technology stack.
- [[MT128 - Position Sizing When Markets Break feat. Rob Carver - Systematic Investor - Ep.386#^MT128-C18-2|MT128-C18-2]] — AI researchers now get non-competes, gardening leave and nine-figure offers, exceeding quant pay.

## [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321]]
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C01-1|MT129-C01-1]] — European autos (14 million jobs) face structural pressure from Chinese EV imports, shown by Volkswagen plant closure and pay-cut proposals.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C02-1|MT129-C02-1]] — Europe's bullish case is hard to make: policy paralysis, aging population, immigration reluctance and no tech leaders hurt productivity.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C02-2|MT129-C02-2]] — EU bureaucracy of 27 countries slows decisions and hurts competitiveness, so Europe is falling behind.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C04-X2|MT129-C04-X2]] — Elon Musk's expected role in government is read as a US competitiveness positive and, by contrast, a negative for Europe's relative position.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C12-3|MT129-C12-3]] — Goldman expects the equal-weighted S&P 500 to outperform the cap-weighted by 2-8% a year, because tech is 30%+ of the index.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C13-3|MT129-C13-3]] — S&P returns over the past decade came from 7% earnings growth, lower taxes, higher margins and multiple expansion; forecasts depend on margin normalisation and bond yields.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C13-X1|MT129-C13-X1]] — Goldman's equal-weighted S&P 500 forecast is about 8% a year (3% plus roughly 5% outperformance), a normal equity return, so its pessimism is about cap-weighted concentration rather than equities broadly.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C13-X3|MT129-C13-X3]] — Counter-argument to the concentration warning: mega-cap tech has deep moats, cash and AI investment capacity, so high concentration and earnings growth could persist even though Goldman finds this unlikely.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C14-1|MT129-C14-1]] — Index leadership changes, so assuming today's top 10 stays top is unreliable; concentration raises volatility but its return effect is unclear.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C14-X5|MT129-C14-X5]] — Investors extrapolate the current rally, but the last decade's equity gains rested on real earnings growth from industries that did not exist ten years ago (cloud, AI chips, smartphones), so it was not pure speculation; the question is where future earnings will come from.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C15-3|MT129-C15-3]] — Invesco 2024 survey: 80% of systematic multi-asset use is factor tilting, 67% asset-class/sector rotation models, 44% trend following, 41% volatility targeting.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C16-1|MT129-C16-1]] — 90% of Invesco survey respondents use AI, mostly pattern recognition, sentiment analysis (news, earnings calls, Twitter NLP) and real-time position monitoring.
- [[MT129 - Post-Election Trends - Systematic Investor 321#^MT129-C16-X1|MT129-C16-X1]] — The Invesco survey says systematic methods are spreading beyond equities to fixed income, commodities, private equity and real estate, with risk parity and machine learning also used; multi-asset funds are less vanilla than assumed.

## [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401]]
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C02-2|MT130-C02-2]] — QIS has shifted from designing an uncorrelated return stream atop SAA to expressing macro views (long equities, long oil, short bonds) through systematic wrappers.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C02-3|MT130-C02-3]] — The SpaceX IPO would increase S&P concentration, which already sits in a few Magnificent Seven stocks.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C02-X1|MT130-C02-X1]] — A macro view such as long equities, long oil and short bonds can be expressed either by a trend-following program or by a QIS wrapper, so QIS and trend following are alternative systematic expressions of the same macro exposure.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C04-5|MT130-C04-5]] — QIS is about $1 trillion (about $3 trillion with leverage); JPM QIS revenue is up 30% year to date and GSAM runs about $175 billion in QIS.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C05-1|MT130-C05-1]] — Banks deliver systematic exposure in many wrappers (swaps, notes, options, capital protected), making access easy.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C05-X1|MT130-C05-X1]] — The WSJ piece argues QIS revenue is nearly risk-free, reliable and capital-light for banks since trades are preset and banks charge according to trade complexity; Moritz says he would not necessarily agree.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C05-X2|MT130-C05-X2]] — QIS are convenient for institutions: outsourced infrastructure, bespoke implementation and easy operational access through swaps and other wrappers, so allocators can get an exposure or hedge without building it themselves.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C05-X3|MT130-C05-X3]] — QIS has become more widely used because technology, data and computing capability make systematic solutions cheaper to build and deliver, and banks add structuring expertise because they sit between buyers and sellers.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C11-2|MT130-C11-2]] — Economies adapt to lower supply, so the shortage effect is not static and hard to quantify.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C11-3|MT130-C11-3]] — In 2026 conventional benchmark markets outperformed in trend following, while non-benchmark commodities were poor for trend but good for reversion and skewness premia.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C12-X1|MT130-C12-X1]] — The CTA index is built from the largest 20 managers, which are heavily exposed to the big macro markets (equities, rates, FX, energy, metals), so it shows the benchmark-market trend result and hides managers trading alternative markets.
- [[MT130 - QIS & Trend Following in 2026 ft. Nick Baltas - Systematic Investor - Ep.401#^MT130-C16-X1|MT130-C16-X1]] — Chesapeake Capital's trend program trades single stocks in addition to the four main asset classes, a rare example of single-name trend inside a CTA (Nick says he would want to check before naming).

## [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363]]
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C04-3|MT131-C04-3]] — Apparent CTA slowing may be a change in structure (added features) rather than a change in signal speed.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C07-3|MT131-C07-3]] — Modern CTAs choose parameters dynamically so speed is not constant, which makes bucketing managers as short/medium/long term misleading.
- [[MT131 - Redefining Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.363#^MT131-C07-5|MT131-C07-5]] — Trend followers split into replicators, core diversifiers, crisis risk offsetters, outlier hunters and pure trend followers, each serving a different investor purpose.

## [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323]]
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C01-1|MT132-C01-1]] — The world has moved into a different regime from the last 40 years, and the shift is non-linear: slow for a long time and then sudden.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C02-1|MT132-C02-1]] — Markets are in a calm before an acceleration into a broader regime change (not necessarily a crash).
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C12-1|MT132-C12-1]] — Demographics (older capital owners vs younger labour) shift politics towards populism as the disadvantaged young gain dominance, over multiple election cycles from 2009 Tea Party/Occupy.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C14-3|MT132-C14-3]] — In populist periods the biggest volatility is in FX, bond yields and precious metals, not in equities or energy.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C14-X8|MT132-C14-X8]] — Forty years of Fed volatility suppression removed the crises needed to pass reforms and so built up inequality, which now feeds populism and ties the Fed's hands.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C15-1|MT132-C15-1]] — In 2021-22 long-dated gold options/upside call vol were cheap while oil vol was high; gold call skew later exploded.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C15-2|MT132-C15-2]] — Gold was the best asset in 1968-82 but suffered 50% drawdowns, so own long-dated calls rather than the underlying and expect big swings.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C15-X9|MT132-C15-X9]] — Deglobalisation, global conflict and reduced Fed control have been raising volatility in FX, bonds and precious metals for several years, and Trump's re-election accelerates it, so those markets are where to trade volatility.
- [[MT132 - Regime Shifts, Populism, and 2025 Predictions - Systematic Investor 323#^MT132-C16-1|MT132-C16-1]] — Bitcoin captures the belief of younger generations rising to political power and may cap some of gold's upside; regulatory risk is the main headwind and Trump's win removes the largest overhang for 4 years.

## [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini]]
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C03-3|MT133-C03-3]] — Cocoa quadrupled in months on West African weather extremes (rain then drought), a possible sign of climate-change commodity volatility.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C03-4|MT133-C03-4]] — Over 100 years the stock-bond correlation averages about zero but is almost never near zero, living in a bimodal distribution; the 60/40 relies on a negative correlation.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-1|MT133-C04-1]] — Stock-bond correlation was strongly positive (up to 60%) from 1960 to 1995 and negative for the last 20 years.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-3|MT133-C04-3]] — In 2024 stock-bond correlation had turned positive again, a bad development for 60/40 asset owners, and depends on inflation.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-4|MT133-C04-4]] — Macro funds track anything that moves global markets, e.g. Nvidia as an AI proxy that drove equities.
- [[MT133 - Resilient Strategies- Tailwinds in Left-Tail Scenarios - Pablo Calderini#^MT133-C04-X2|MT133-C04-X2]] — Discretionary macro sits between passive/long-only tactical allocation and pure arbitrage: it has low long-run correlation to asset classes but takes active directional exposures day to day.

## [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD]]
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C05-3|MT134-C05-3]] — Drivers of demand for non-correlation: higher rates, 2022 stock-bond correlation break, record valuations, rising debt and loss of US hegemony.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C06-2|MT134-C06-2]] — After 2022 (bond drawdown 4x prior 20 years) every asset-allocation model must incorporate positive stock-bond correlation; gradual allocator education drives demand for futures-based ETFs.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C06-X1|MT134-C06-X1]] — Allocators have created an alternatives bucket after 2022 but now have to work out what to put in it, so demand for products that fill the bucket (futures-based ETFs, replication, trend) is limited by knowledge, not by desire.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C06-X2|MT134-C06-X2]] — Buffered and defined-outcome ETFs are an early-stage growth area in a 13 trillion dollar ETF market, evidenced by Goldman paying about 2 billion dollars for Innovator.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C08-2|MT134-C08-2]] — 60/40 only became standard in the mid-1980s; stock-bond correlation was positive for the 80 years before 1982, and non-correlated strategies also offer capital efficiency which draws flows as rates rise.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C09-X2|MT134-C09-X2]] — Managed-futures assets sit mostly with institutions; ETFs and other wrappers are widening access to a different investor universe that has not held these strategies before.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C10-X1|MT134-C10-X1]] — A product niche exists for model-portfolio allocators who see the statistical benefit of a 3-5% managed-futures sleeve but will not spend a year learning how to evaluate dispersion; a replication ETF serves them.
- [[MT134 - Return Dispersion- The 2025 Story - Systematic Investor - Ep.380 REUPLOAD#^MT134-C19-X2|MT134-C19-X2]] — CTA fee structures have drifted from performance-related pay toward flat management fees; some allocators prefer low flat fees (ETFs, QIS), while others want managers to take performance fees and have skin in the game.

## [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16]]
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C02-X1|MT135-C02-X1]] — Portable alpha was an institutional tool for about 40 years and out of reach for individuals and wealth managers who will not run a derivatives book; return stacking packages it as a ticker so the capital-efficient structure can be bought without managing the derivatives.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C03-2|MT135-C03-2]] — Futures-based ETF trend products use a subset of the most liquid US and European markets versus the full set in their mutual fund and hedge fund versions.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C03-X1|MT135-C03-X1]] — A levered risk-parity bond and equity allocation (and some QIS products) already works as a portable-alpha product, so investors may hold the concept without knowing it; return stacking makes it explicit.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C03-X2|MT135-C03-X2]] — Wrapper choice is a trade-off: an ETF gives tax efficiency, intraday liquidity and easy platform access, whereas a mutual fund (end-of-day NAV) can trade a wider futures set; the same strategy can run in both.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C07-2|MT135-C07-2]] — Fitting to the SG Trend Index gives intermediate trend speeds and about equal 25% risk weights in equities, bonds, commodities and currencies, with commodity risk split evenly between metals and energies.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C09-X1|MT135-C09-X1]] — The ETF aims to deliver trend beta and beat the category over the long run through lower fees, while managers who want to add alpha should trade the fat tail of markets (which the mutual fund version does).
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C12-4|MT135-C12-4]] — Generic trend beta is available in QIS at 10-15 bp, so managers must differentiate through spreads, breakout, speed, or fat-tail markets.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C12-X1|MT135-C12-X1]] — Funds charging performance fees for generic trend will be squeezed out by cheaper implementations of the same beta, while fee room remains for managers with real innovation in fat-tail markets, spreads, breakouts and other differentiators.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C13-1|MT135-C13-1]] — Trend funds now run 8-12% volatility, so returns lack the punch of 1980s-90s CTAs, trading off stress payoff.
- [[MT135 - Return Stacking Explained - Open Interest - Ep.16#^MT135-C17-1|MT135-C17-1]] — A risk-balanced gold plus Bitcoin overlay on US equity offers a store-of-value diversifier to a stock-bond portfolio.

## [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367]]
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C03-1|MT136-C03-1]] — Skill is only monetised where the opportunity set is favourable; talent cannot overcome a structurally declining or efficient area, so market efficiency varies by segment.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C04-1|MT136-C04-1]] — 60/40 worked because of the stock-bond correlation regime of the long bond bull market (zero/negative correlation); it reinforced itself as a paradigm and would not have looked the same starting in 1990.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C04-2|MT136-C04-2]] — This decade bonds became highly correlated with equities (around 0.8 in his words, vol doubled or tripled) and fell with stocks in the inflation shock, breaking the diversification premise of 60/40.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C05-X1|MT136-C05-X1]] — Allocators' weight in managed futures is far below what its diversification statistics (low correlation to other strategies) would justify, so a portfolio-contribution lens would raise it.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C06-3|MT136-C06-3]] — US wealth managers moved from 60/40 to 60/20/20 with a 20% diversifying bucket because bonds had correlation to equities of about 0.8, vol up 2-3x and returned less than cash.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C06-X1|MT136-C06-X1]] — CalPERS shut its $4bn absolute-return bucket because a 4% return on about 1-2% of the fund could not move total results, so a diversifier sized too small to matter gets cut despite its portfolio benefit.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C16-1|MT136-C16-1]] — Multistrat funds act like bank proprietary desks with better information, financing and pay-out around 55% of revenue as compensation.
- [[MT136 - Risk, Replication & Portfolio Strategy - Systematic Investor - Ep.367#^MT136-C16-2|MT136-C16-2]] — Markets are efficient in segments like US large cap stocks, so fees of 100 bps are not recouped, but esoteric segments remain inefficient where talent can earn alpha.

## [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -]]
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C02-2|MT137-C02-2]] — Renaissance's closed Medallion fund returns differ massively from its outside funds, possibly because alpha has limited capacity or good ideas are kept inside, and its outside CTA-type fund underperformed.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C17-1|MT137-C17-1]] — CalPERS board adopted the total portfolio approach (from July 2026) replacing SAA, with a reference portfolio of 75% equities/25% bonds.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C18-1|MT137-C18-1]] — SAA is top-down: set return, risk and correlation expectations, build the efficient frontier, choose the max Sharpe or minimum risk meeting hurdle, then fill sleeves (hedge funds, private credit, CTAs) as boxes.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C19-2|MT137-C19-2]] — TPA evaluates additions holistically (add CTA, dial down equities, or replace), which should favour diversifying strategies.
- [[MT137 - Rob Carver on Trend Following, Skew, and the Total Portfolio Shift - Systematic Investor -#^MT137-C20-2|MT137-C20-2]] — If large quant-heavy allocators like ADIA adopt TPA with formal publications, it could be a game-changer for trend following allocations.

## [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025]]
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C01-1|MT138-C01-1]] — The last 15 years rested on globalisation, US security umbrella and deflationary easy money, making a plain S&P 500 the best asset; investment returns over decades are driven by such secular paradigms.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C01-2|MT138-C01-2]] — The new regime, modern mercantilism, treats trade deficits as wealth transfers, pursues self-sufficiency and treats allies coercively, implying a different environment for assets.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C02-1|MT138-C02-1]] — US private-sector exceptionalism (technology, financial services, capital markets) persists, so money leaving the US will not be easy.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C02-X2|MT138-C02-X2]] — Citrone expects the world to silo into three regional blocs (Americas, Europe, Asia), which creates regional dislocations and opportunities in places such as Latin America.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C06-1|MT138-C06-1]] — Fixed income capabilities were hollowed out in a zero-rate world as money moved to private credit, which carries equity-like risk rather than uncorrelated alpha.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C06-2|MT138-C06-2]] — With rates no longer zero, duration and currency selection across countries offer uncorrelated alpha again, though many mandates are limited to US Treasuries.
- [[MT138 - Rob Citrone and Karen Karniol-Tambour at Sohn 2025#^MT138-C06-4|MT138-C06-4]] — Commodities, including gold, are ignored but useful assets in a world with more inflation risk.

## [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,]]
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C03-3|MT139-C03-3]] — Broader household equity ownership (401ks) makes a market correction transmit more strongly to the economy than in 1987, so a substantial slowdown is likely.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C09-2|MT139-C09-2]] — China is potentially uninvestable due to a leader focused on power rather than growth, with over 30% of the EM equity index and much foreign money stuck; short CNH and equities tempered by about 15% odds Xi loses a second term.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C09-3|MT139-C09-3]] — A cold war of the West against China and Russia is bad for returns and assets prices.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C10-2|MT139-C10-2]] — Globalisation is reversing, implying lower growth and higher inflation.
- [[MT139 - Rob Citrone – Emerging Markets, Hedge Funds, and Staying in the Game (Capital Allocators,#^MT139-C10-X1|MT139-C10-X1]] — India is Citrone's long-term growth favourite: decades of reform, a large market, English language and good rule of law make reforms hard to reverse, with key-person political risk around Modi.

## [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests]]
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C07-3|MT140-C07-3]] — High-frequency alpha decays with half-life of 1.5-2 months and is detectable statistically in real time, requiring constant replacement.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C07-4|MT140-C07-4]] — For slow systems alpha decay with a half-life of 1-2 decades cannot be distinguished from noise, so it is not worth monitoring; basic trend following has decayed gradually over 50-60 years.
- [[MT140 - Robert Carver Ran a Multi-Billion Dollar Systematic Portfolio for Man AHL. Now He Invests#^MT140-C12-X1|MT140-C12-X1]] — Corrects the host's premise: Man Group is a public company owning several hedge funds, none of them pod shops, and AHL has only about 100-120 front-office staff, so the talent-and-capital consolidation argument about Citadel and Millennium does not apply directly to it.

## [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)]]
