- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C04-X1|MT141-C04-X1]] — Single-stock shorting was richer in 1988-90 because stock dispersion was high and there was no basket trading or index concentration, which implies that index concentration and basket trading compress idiosyncratic short opportunity.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C07-2|MT141-C07-2]] — A good external manager is one you never worry about; a great one brings a big trade idea a few times a year or proposes an SPV, so the core team can leverage its knowledge through specialist managers.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C08-1|MT141-C08-1]] — Large capital can be an advantage if it is unconstrained and decisions are fast (answer in three days versus an investment committee with five memos in three weeks).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C08-2|MT141-C08-2]] — A hub-and-spoke model (small internal core team plus outside managers who bring ideas without being bigfooted) worked well at scale.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C12-2|MT141-C12-2]] — Offer larger investors episodic SPVs for big ideas (once a year to once in three years) and return the capital when the opportunity ends.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C12-5|MT141-C12-5]] — China: tactical stabilisation only; Xi is a Leninist who will not allow market clearing, so no sustainable Chinese economy.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C13-3|MT141-C13-3]] — Oct 2024 view: Japan could be a secular bull market after Abe's policies and stability, now at risk from prime minister turnover.
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C13-4|MT141-C13-4]] — Europe is a policy question mark: populist backlash against trade and immigration plus German industrial policy mistakes (China sales, Russian energy, cheap currency, nuclear shutdown).
- [[MT141 - Scott Bessent - Macro Maven (EP.415)#^MT141-C13-6|MT141-C13-6]] — A Bretton Woods-type realignment of global policy and trade is coming.

## [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King]]
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C02-2|MT142-C02-2]] — The US Treasury market is the most important market for policy; Treasury keeps it resilient through transparency and operational and settlement robustness and monitors it daily.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C03-3|MT142-C03-3]] — After Fukushima Japan's nuclear shutdown forced fuel imports, turning a ~3% of GDP current account surplus into a deficit, an early catalyst for a weaker yen.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C04-3|MT142-C04-3]] — Abenomics started as a market success and then became structural: shareholder rights, return on capital, womenomics, labor mobility.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C05-1|MT142-C05-1]] — What hurts the US economy about an oil spike is duration rather than level (2008 reached 147), and record US liquids production insulates household energy bills; WTI had gone from under 60 to a 114-115 peak.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C07-2|MT142-C07-2]] — Sanctioning Russian barrels has little effect when the cargoes would reach China; a 30-day waiver for India uses barrels already on the water.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C07-3|MT142-C07-3]] — Latin American governments are shifting from far left to centre-right and market-friendly, creating an opportunity for US alliances.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C08-1|MT142-C08-1]] — Once the Iran conflict is resolved in US favour, oil could move to a substantially lower regime than the pre-war level below $60.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C11-2|MT142-C11-2]] — US relative strength rests on energy dominance (an exporter, not an importer), technology lead and a rebuilt military.
- [[MT142 - Scott Bessent- Inside Trump’s Treasury; War Costs; & Why Bond Market is King#^MT142-C12-1|MT142-C12-1]] — The US AI boom lifts its share of global compute to 70-80% (from 40-50%) and US growth should return to 3% versus 0.3% celebrated in the EU after the conflict.

## [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299]]
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C02-1|MT143-C02-1]] — Pre-2009 US real yields were about 2% above inflation but since then about 0.25% or less; the past 10-15 years may be the abnormal period, so rates dropping at the long end is not assured and many portfolios are not positioned for normal real yields.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C04-2|MT143-C04-2]] — Trend followers have no long bias so they were genuinely short bonds in 2022-2024, whereas bearish macro managers often held other positions instead of shorting bonds; trend followers may be the only true short sellers left.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C11-1|MT143-C11-1]] — Pivotal Path shows managed futures are about 6% of hedge fund assets versus equity long/short 37% and multi-strat 20%, so institutions are ridiculously underinvested in managed futures.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C13-X1|MT143-C13-X1]] — Visible external CTA assets understate trend-following adoption because large pension funds likely run trend following in-house, although the data does not show it.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C14-3|MT143-C14-3]] — Portfolios are shifting from 60/40 to 50/30/20 and the 20% alternatives bucket is where managed futures compete with gold and TIPS.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C17-1|MT143-C17-1]] — Concern that fee cutting turns CTA ETFs into the low-cost airline model, with less experienced and worse products.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C18-1|MT143-C18-1]] — ETF fees will not fall to zero: running an ETF costs 10-15bp at scale plus marketing and platform costs, and DBMF at 85bp is in line with active ETFs.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C18-2|MT143-C18-2]] — If a product charges about 80bp and costs are as high as stated, the manager earns only a few basis points.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C18-X1|MT143-C18-X1]] — In replication ETFs the market leader's track record is a moat: a pure copycat cannot win, so entrants adapt part of the model, add their own overlay and cut price, which fuels fee competition.
- [[MT143 - Sharing the Trend Following Pie - Systematic Investor 299#^MT143-C19-1|MT143-C19-1]] — Pricing is held up by distribution gatekeepers (platforms want a cut, hedge fund channels profit from high fees), so ETF fees will not go to zero unless Vanguard decides otherwise.

## [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312]]
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C05-3|MT144-C05-3]] — Theory behind macro momentum: unobservable latent factors form trends and momentum on individual markets is a window onto them; AQR proposes trending macro factors directly.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C10-1|MT144-C10-1]] — Below roughly $100,000 even micros and minis give a portfolio dominated by equity indices and agriculturals; smaller investors are better off with a CTA ETF or fund.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C10-3|MT144-C10-3]] — Bitcoin has lost its original claim as money and is viewed as a speculative asset.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C11-2|MT144-C11-2]] — Currencies derive value from tax acceptance and government creation; governments will not accept an asset they do not control for taxes, so Bitcoin cannot be money.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C12-X1|MT144-C12-X1]] — The dollar's status as the global reserve and trade-settlement currency is a huge advantage for the US (though hegemony is weaker than before), so officially adding Bitcoin to the monetary system risks frittering it away.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C13-1|MT144-C13-1]] — Active ETFs reached $1tn but only about 10-15% of the ETF universe, which is still mostly passive.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C15-X1|MT144-C15-X1]] — Creation and redemption by authorised participants requires the ETF's constituents to be known, a transparency constraint for active managers who do not want to disclose positions (partly avoidable).
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C17-1|MT144-C17-1]] — Transtrend paper argument: a stock index is indirect exposure to factors such as yen and metals, and a CTA index is likewise exposure to factors via many CTAs.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C17-X1|MT144-C17-X1]] — Sept 2024: a new investable SG CTA index (from a different part of SocGen than the usual SG CTA index publisher) is powered by the DBMF replication engine, which prompted the Transtrend paper 'Can't we just buy the index'.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C18-2|MT144-C18-2]] — Weakness in the stock-index analogy: firms hedge FX and commodity exposures.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C18-3|MT144-C18-3]] — CTA factor exposure is dynamic and not linear or one-directional, unlike a company's.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C19-1|MT144-C19-1]] — More markets are better than fewer, so replicating with a small number of futures is not ideal.
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C20-3|MT144-C20-3]] — An ETF with fewer than 20 markets may be correlated with the CTA index but lacks its diversification and carries single-manager risk (Transtrend).
- [[MT144 - Should You Just Buy a CTA Index- - Systematic Investor 312#^MT144-C20-4|MT144-C20-4]] — ETFs give retail access to alternative risk premia and trend following that would otherwise be unavailable.

## [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3]]
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C02-3|MT145-C02-3]] — US weight (about 60% of global equities, with the top 7 tech stocks about 40% of US cap) shows extreme concentration of the market in one country and a few names.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C05-X2|MT145-C05-X2]] — The economy has shifted from demand-constrained to supply-constrained with fiscal dominance, so a debasement trade now exists that did not a few years ago.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C06-2|MT145-C06-2]] — AI buildout needs real energy that cannot be printed, so energy, nuclear and uranium assets (and gold, which nearly doubled) will see price inflation.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C10-1|MT145-C10-1]] — 80-90% of CTA returns come from the long side; shorts rarely pay, so CTAs have become long-only-like and can be replicated.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C10-3|MT145-C10-3]] — CTAs moved from long to short tails, so the rolling skew of the CTA index is flat and it no longer diversifies portfolios.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C10-X1|MT145-C10-X1]] — CTA return dispersion is explained by style-drift dimensions: long versus short side, time frame, asset-class mix, long versus short volatility, and mixing mean reversion with momentum; these can be replicated to see which CTAs drifted.
- [[MT145 - Skew, Convexity, and the Hidden Risks in Systematic Investing - Systematic Investor - Ep.3#^MT145-C10-X2|MT145-C10-X2]] — Greater data, alternative markets and optimisation power have been used to justify moving CTAs into negatively skewed strategies in the name of diversification, which explains why CTAs went from long tails to short tails.

## [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358]]
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C01-1|MT146-C01-1]] — Multistrat hedge funds compete for talent via external SMAs and seeding, so large launches are spinouts or multistrat-anchored and an emerging manager now usually starts with a large multistrat ticket.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C02-1|MT146-C02-1]] — While the West turns protectionist, China is increasing outbound investment, a divergent trade/investment regime.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C04-2|MT146-C04-2]] — A correlation heat map shows CTAs drifted from medium-term to longer-term models over 15 years because longer horizons performed better (the 'speed factor').
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C09-1|MT146-C09-1]] — At industry level performance is driven by major themes (e.g. inflation) in the largest, most liquid markets, so trades in heating oil or small markets matter little.
- [[MT146 - Slower Models, Bigger Risks - Systematic Investor - Ep.358#^MT146-C19-2|MT146-C19-2]] — No market inherently trends better, e.g. cocoa went from worst to best within three years.

## [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour]]
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C01-1|MT147-C01-1]] — Aggregate corporate profit exists only if spending exceeds wage income, so profits are financed by borrowing, dissaving or government deficits rather than by wages.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C01-5|MT147-C01-5]] — Before 2008, secular forces (globalization, deregulation, fewer unions) drove the structural rise in profit share.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C02-1|MT147-C02-1]] — Globalization, deregulation and union decline cut worker bargaining power, so wages lagged and workers borrowed to keep spending, raising profits.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C02-3|MT147-C02-3]] — After the financial crisis private-sector leveraging ended and the government took over as the source of non-wage spending.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C02-X2|MT147-C02-X2]] — Before Covid the profit-support paradigm was at its limit: profits and inequality were at extremes, QE had been used and rates were already at zero, so monetary policy had no room left to extend the leverage-driven profit model.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C03-5|MT147-C03-5]] — The globalization tailwind to margins is exhausted, so no new push against labor bargaining power is available.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C04-2|MT147-C04-2]] — In the 1970s about 30% of the stock market was commodity producers who benefited from inflation, now only about 3%, so equities benefit far less from commodity inflation today.
- [[MT147 - Sohn 2022 - A Macro Approach with Karen Karniol-Tambour#^MT147-C04-3|MT147-C04-3]] — Spending on supply-chain resilience and decarbonization is low-return capex, so it will not lift profits like past investment.

## [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C06-2|MT148-C06-2]] — He expects energy supply to be short for 5-10 years because ESG limits investment, supporting energy longs.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C06-X2|MT148-C06-X2]] — Blockchain is likely to become a real economic force in 5-10 years and a disruptor of financial companies, creating winners among companies not yet founded; he will not bet on it with conviction now.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C13-1|MT148-C13-1]] — He expects macro-driven markets for the next 4-5 years.
- [[MT148 - Sohn 2022 - John Collison in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT148-C14-2|MT148-C14-2]] — His biggest equity profits came from growth stocks he expected to be much better in 3-5 years.

## [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller]]
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C04-5|MT149-C04-5]] — Coming out of a hard landing copper should benefit from EVs and infrastructure policy, so the question is entry timing.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C05-1|MT149-C05-1]] — US housing could be a beneficiary on the way out of a hard landing because of a structural shortage of single-family homes.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C05-2|MT149-C05-2]] — Druckenmiller thinks AI could be as impactful as the internet and a good opportunity in a hard landing; his firm owns Nvidia and Microsoft and a growth leader may not fall in recession.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C05-3|MT149-C05-3]] — He holds gold and silver against their poor hard-landing record because authorities are near the end of their rope and other countries are holding fewer dollar reserves.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C05-X1|MT149-C05-X1]] — After a long underperformance, biotech is a candidate growth area on the way out of a hard landing because scientific progress (cancer, antiviral use of viruses against bacteria) continues; he does not pick names.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C09-3|MT149-C09-3]] — Interviewer cites dollar overvaluation and central banks diversifying away after Russian reserves were frozen; Druckenmiller elsewhere says he holds a modest short dollar position.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C10-X2|MT149-C10-X2]] — AI may raise productivity as much as the PC did, unlike recent technology that went into social networks; applications built on top of the large-model builders could create $100bn companies even if the market is flat for ten years.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C11-1|MT149-C11-1]] — Geopolitical view: US estrangement of the Saudi crown prince risks pushing him to China during a 10-15 year oil transition bridge.
- [[MT149 - Sohn 2023 - Kiril Sokoloff in conversation with Stanley Druckenmiller#^MT149-C12-2|MT149-C12-2]] — Lung cancer 5-year survival at Sloan Kettering rose to 58% after targeted therapy and immunotherapy, an S-curve of innovation.

## [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347]]
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C02-1|MT150-C02-1]] — Negative-working-capital businesses such as insurance gain in inflation as margins rise and offset multiple contraction, which is why Buffett outperformed in the 1970s inflation.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C03-2|MT150-C03-2]] — Populist transfers from the top 10% to the bottom 40% raise velocity because lower-income households spend everything, while the top puts money into assets, so inflation rises.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C04-1|MT150-C04-1]] — Protectionism with populism gives more jobs but lower pay, higher inflation and lower corporate margins, so profits fall.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C04-4|MT150-C04-4]] — Because the top 10% account for half of spending, a wealth-effect hit would cut consumption, giving an earnings recession with margin compression while jobs stay stable.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C04-7|MT150-C04-7]] — Reshoring after tariffs raises prices and creates a rush of local job demand, so it is inflationary before it hits margins; timing of recession vs GDP is unclear.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C05-1|MT150-C05-1]] — Basket for structural inflation: insurance, value/DCF names, software and telecom, which raise revenue and margins; growth software relying on venture money suffers.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C05-X1|MT150-C05-X1]] — Niels adds that how reshored jobs are filled changes the inflation effect: automation would not be inflationary, while an immigration crackdown removes the low-paid labour that would otherwise fill them, adding wage pressure.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C07-1|MT150-C07-1]] — Inequality drives populism, so politics will keep moving toward spending on the bottom and away from supply-side policy.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C14-1|MT150-C14-1]] — Unity decides whether the West meets the coming crisis; disunity (including from outside actors) is a risk factor.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C14-R1|MT150-C14-R1]] — Jim says outside actors explicitly pursue disunity in the West, and that social media magnifies both disunity and unity.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C15-2|MT150-C15-2]] — As in 2007, crowds debate recession while a structural flaw (now Treasury market dysfunction) may be the real risk, so the real danger may be unspoken.
- [[MT150 - Stagflation, Regime Change, and the Clues We Missed - Systematic Investor 347#^MT150-C15-X1|MT150-C15-X1]] — Jim sees tariffs on allies, with a stated closeness to Russia, as disunifying the West, which he treats as a risk factor for the coming crisis (and a sign that the policy maker may be a 'useful fool').

## [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management]]
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C03-5|MT151-C03-5]] — AI capex persists because firms fear competitors who invest will gain advantage, so hyperscaler demand continues despite rich valuations.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C03-6|MT151-C03-6]] — An early signal of a technology wave was the shift of top engineering graduates from crypto to AI, followed by young analysts talking about it; he then bought Nvidia after it had fallen from 400 to 150.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C04-1|MT151-C04-1]] — Once a theme is identified, study the chain of beneficiaries (power, uranium, etc.) to trade second-order effects.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C04-2|MT151-C04-2]] — AI will produce four or five modelers spending massive capital without a single winner; the profits may be in unknown applications (like Uber and Facebook after the internet), so being neither long nor short the group was his stance.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C04-4|MT151-C04-4]] — Anti-obesity drugs were a recurring-revenue (razor blade) business because patients regain weight when they stop, which made the trend attractive.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C06-X1|MT151-C06-X1]] — A cyclical hardware industry (semiconductors, which he has studied since the 1970s) deserves a premium when the company has a durable software moat: Nvidia's CUDA and thousands of software engineers made him think it would keep growing, though he still sold at a $2tn valuation.
- [[MT151 - Stan Druckenmiller - Podcast - In Good Company - Norges Bank Investment Management#^MT151-C09-7|MT151-C09-7]] — Trade a theme through the concentric circles of dominoes it knocks over, not only the headline trade.

## [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview]]
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C02-5|MT152-C02-5]] — Because this cycle's bad credit sits in high-yield and leveraged loans rather than banks, there is no systemic financial crisis, but the economy is still hit by credit drying up.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C06-4|MT152-C06-4]] — Trade wars hurt business confidence and growth; he had not foreseen this risk three years earlier.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C08-2|MT152-C08-2]] — In slow-growth environments, 20-30% growers behave like long-duration bonds, which supports secular growth stocks.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C08-3|MT152-C08-3]] — The tax cut and strong economy gave cyclical companies 30% earnings growth at 9x earnings versus growth names at 25-30x, interrupting the secular growth trend.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C08-4|MT152-C08-4]] — Cloud software (Microsoft, ServiceNow, Workday, Salesforce) is in the second inning of a shift; it grows regardless of macro and may benefit from mild recession as firms cut costs.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C09-2|MT152-C09-2]] — Long the disruptors and short the disrupted in tech worked well, but whipsaws in sell-offs when value buyers buy the disrupted at 10x earnings.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C10-2|MT152-C10-2]] — Political risk is hard to forecast (the president thinks one or two days ahead), so his edge in economic puzzles is diluted; he expects the China dispute to be worked out but with low confidence.
- [[MT152 - Stanley Druckenmiller on Economy, Stocks, Bonds, Trump, Fed- Full Interview#^MT152-C12-2|MT152-C12-2]] — Competition from smarter participants has made markets more efficient and hedge fund alpha harder; only 10-20 discretionary funds will deserve 20% fees.

## [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets]]
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C03-5|MT153-C03-5]] — Long disruptors (cloud, internet) and short the disrupted (retail, staples) was a multi-year pair theme in his firm.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C04-1|MT153-C04-1]] — His view that Chinese internet firms were less exposed to regulation than US peers (because they partner with the government) was wrong in 2018 as Tencent and others dropped.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C05-4|MT153-C05-4]] — Currency drivers change regime every 15-20 years (current account, then interest differentials, then risk-on/risk-off).
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C08-5|MT153-C08-5]] — Google at about 20x earnings (15x after cash) is cheap relative to Campbell's Soup at 20x despite AI leadership; regulatory fears do not make him sell.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C14-1|MT153-C14-1]] — Populism is driven by wealth inequality rather than economic malaise; workers got no real wage growth for 30 years, so populist politics will persist and could force major dislocations.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C14-X2|MT153-C14-X2]] — Earlier populist waves needed deep economic distress, but today populism arises amid decent aggregate growth and unemployment, so good headline data will not defuse it because the trigger is distribution not the cycle.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C14-X3|MT153-C14-X3]] — Meritocratic elite universities became a self-sorting elite (intermarriage, shared zip codes; Murray's Coming Apart), entrenching inequality structurally; he sees no end until major political and economic dislocation.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C14-X4|MT153-C14-X4]] — Elites that concede a little early avoid revolution (China's post-2012 anti-corruption drive to save the Communist Party); America did not, so disparity kept widening.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C15-1|MT153-C15-1]] — Overuse of financial sanctions as a weapon (including against allies) threatens trust in the US-led system; a two-term hard-left or Trump repeat could escalate by 2024.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C15-2|MT153-C15-2]] — He estimates a 40-55% probability that the global trade system survives the Trump era intact.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C15-3|MT153-C15-3]] — Forced relocation of supply chains from China is immensely disruptive; Samsung would help China build its own semiconductor industry even if it cannibalizes them.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C15-X1|MT153-C15-X1]] — While the US shifts toward nationalism the rest of the world is building multilateral trade alignments (Japan-EU deal, TPP, China's Belt and Road), so trade and capital flows may reroute around the US.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C16-1|MT153-C16-1]] — Trade restrictions accelerate Chinese semiconductor self-sufficiency and push Europe toward China, trends that will not reverse.
- [[MT153 - Stanley F. Druckenmiller- Monetary Policy & Markets#^MT153-C16-X2|MT153-C16-X2]] — Confronting China can be defensible but only with a united front of allies; alienating Europe, Canada and other partners makes the policy self-defeating and pushes them toward China.

## [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328]]
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C07-X1|MT155-C07-X1]] — Cheap, basic CTA-type products for ordinary investors are scarce, especially in Europe, which is the gap replication or other wrappers could fill.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C08-X1|MT155-C08-X1]] — Niels is wary that the push to deliver exposures ever cheaper, which long pre-dates replication (flat-fee mutual funds), squeezes the economics of research-intensive managed futures.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C09-2|MT155-C09-2]] — Managed futures is underinvested: institutions hold about 8% in hedge funds, about 5% of which is managed futures (about 50 bp of the portfolio).
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C09-X2|MT155-C09-X2]] — Investor preference functions differ widely even among similar-looking investors, which drives product proliferation because even a narrow subset is large enough to support a scalable product.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C09-X3|MT155-C09-X3]] — Large managers have become portfolio consultants that assemble customised packages of products for each client, a symbiotic relationship expected to continue.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C10-1|MT155-C10-1]] — After 40 years of falling rates, higher rates cut expected returns on bonds and stocks, so true alternatives (non-correlated derivatives) are needed.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C10-4|MT155-C10-4]] — Multi-strats grew from a fraction of 2019 assets to dominate the hedge fund industry in five years, and return stacking, QIS and replication moved from skepticism to mainstream.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C10-X2|MT155-C10-X2]] — Historical analogy: derivatives entered investing in the 1960s-70s because higher rates made their capital efficiency and non-correlation attractive, so a return to higher rates should revive demand for them.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C11-2|MT155-C11-2]] — Investor perception of managed futures improved after 2021-22 from the weak 2010s, so allocations should rise over five years.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C11-4|MT155-C11-4]] — Multi-strats succeed through scale as operator and capital allocator (teams, infrastructure, best terms), a different model from funds of funds, so the 180th pod need not be worse.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C12-2|MT155-C12-2]] — Cross-asset risk premia and multi-strat strategies returned because investors needed diversifying returns when stagflation left conventional markets without return.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C12-3|MT155-C12-3]] — Barclay CTA index AUM has stayed near $350bn for about 14 years, so adoption of managed futures has been flat despite the evidence.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C12-X3|MT155-C12-X3]] — As more markets, systematic approaches and wrappers become available, investors allocate to defined objectives (yield enhancement, defence against flash corrections, defence against longer corrections) rather than a generic alternative bucket.
- [[MT155 - Systematic Investing in 2025- Trends, Predictions & Bold Debates - Systematic Investor 328#^MT155-C15-1|MT155-C15-1]] — Talk of BRICS de-dollarisation is widely discussed but may have no effect on the dollar's reserve role for now.

## [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151]]
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C02-1|MT156-C02-1]] — The post-Covid shift from too little to too much (but gradually falling) inflation is a structural regime change that alters the structure of strategies and portfolios.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C02-2|MT156-C02-2]] — Higher inflation challenges the stock-bond correlation, the key construct for portfolio construction, so bond diversification is less reliable.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C03-2|MT156-C03-2]] — The practical inflation risk is persistent above-target inflation (about 3%) rather than a return of Covid-level inflation.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C03-X1|MT156-C03-X1]] — The persistent-inflation concern is concentrated in the US; outside the US the factors that could drive resurgent inflation are not present, so the weakened stock-bond diversification story is mainly a US one.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C04-5|MT156-C04-5]] — With positive real yields and weaker diversification, investors use fixed income more for income than as a diversifier, opening room for alternative diversifiers.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C04-X1|MT156-C04-X1]] — Beyond a higher level, post-Covid inflation is likely to be less stable, which makes planning harder and keeps inflation uncertainty (and the term premium) elevated.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C05-1|MT156-C05-1]] — Disinflation in the 1990s-2000s gave negative stock-bond correlation plus positive real yields, the golden period for 60/40 and risk parity.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C05-3|MT156-C05-3]] — Weaker bond diversification opens space for systematic trend and liquid alternatives as diversifiers.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C06-1|MT156-C06-1]] — Inflation uncertainty reversed the long decline in term premium and real rates.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C08-X1|MT156-C08-X1]] — Pure trend following was historically ignored by the very large asset managers, but big houses (BlackRock, Fidelity, Invesco) have launched trend products in the last year or two, a structural shift in who offers trend and in what wrapper.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C16-2|MT156-C16-2]] — Uncorrelated return streams matter more than crisis alpha alone in concentrated markets.
- [[MT156 - Systematic Thinking, BlackRock Style - Top Traders Unplugged - Ep.151#^MT156-C16-X1|MT156-C16-X1]] — The shift to a whole-portfolio (total portfolio) lens, long used by institutions, is spreading to retail: products should be judged by their contribution in context, not in isolation, which favors uncorrelated strategies.

## [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421]]
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C01-1|MT157-C01-1]] — 60/40 was a historical accident of the 2000s, fueled by a 20-year bond bull market in which bonds were a near perfect diversifier for stocks, so extrapolating it forward is a mistake.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C02-1|MT157-C02-1]] — The dot-com mania lowered the perceived cost of starting businesses in the US, raising entrepreneurial risk tolerance and producing an inflection in US growth.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C02-2|MT157-C02-2]] — AI sharply lowers the capital and headcount needed to start a business, which should raise entrepreneurship much as the 1990s did, with large disruption.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C06-1|MT157-C06-1]] — A late-1990s futures-based commodity return series led allocators to add commodities, but the correlation to equities flipped positive in the mid-2000s, so an in-sample correlation is not a stable allocation rule.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C06-2|MT157-C06-2]] — 60/40 rested on a short historical window of negative stock-bond correlation and bonds that never fell.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C08-2|MT157-C08-2]] — The stock-bond breakdown pushes investors to replace the bond 40, or part of it, with alternatives.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C11-1|MT157-C11-1]] — The negative stock-bond correlation of 2000-2020 was an anomaly: over 27 years US IG +0.36, REITs 0.65, GSCI 0.3, gold ~0, CTAs slightly negative.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C12-3|MT157-C12-3]] — Bonds had max drawdown <4% in 2000-2020 (central bank policy), so CTA 5-10% drawdowns look bad by comparison; equities always recovered.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C13-X4|MT157-C13-X4]] — To go mainstream, CTA exposure should be offered in ETFs and mutual funds and compared with the rest of the liquid portfolio on liquidity and accessibility, not with hedge fund return targets.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C15-X1|MT157-C15-X1]] — He expects AI-generated portfolio advice to hold few bonds, so AI adoption by advisers and clients could accelerate the move away from 60/40.
- [[MT157 - Taking CTAs Mainstream - Systematic Investor - Ep.421#^MT157-C16-X1|MT157-C16-X1]] — In a total-portfolio approach the buckets are dropped and each holding must justify its marginal value, which favors CTAs if their diversification and crisis alpha are explained in those terms.

## [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304]]
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C03-X2|MT158-C03-X2]] — Access to Chinese markets has improved through internationalised contracts and the Q licence, so more markets are tradable at lower cost, which widens the diversification set for trend programs.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C07-3|MT158-C07-3]] — Liquid futures are tied to the macroeconomy so crises show up in them, while alternative markets like Nord Pool power have independent drivers, hence no crisis alpha; combine both for maximum diversification.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C09-X2|MT158-C09-X2]] — Demand varies by region: large sovereign investors are open everywhere, Europeans are more conservative (commodity restrictions in France and Germany), Asia prefers higher-risk versions, and Scandinavia applies stricter ESG and restricted lists.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C10-X1|MT158-C10-X1]] — ESG treatment of futures is inconsistent: equity indices can be decomposed into names and scored (ESG indices), but commodities draw the widest dispersion of opinion because the energy transition itself needs metals like copper and silver, and some European clients exclude all agricultural commodities.
- [[MT158 - Taking the Trend Following Temperature - Systematic Investor 304#^MT158-C12-1|MT158-C12-1]] — Packaging trend with long equities (return stacking) hides the standalone line-item problem and sells well, avoiding the word trend.

## [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger]]
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C02-2|MT159-C02-2]] — Trade war shocks propagate to high-margin tech revenue, e.g. an estimated $7B Meta ad revenue loss from Chinese merchants (Temu) cutting ad spend, which is nearly pure profit.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C03-1|MT159-C03-1]] — Tariffs raise inflation, protect firms from competition and reduce innovation and competitiveness, and invite retaliation (Reagan/Smoot-Hawley history), so they hurt long-run growth.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C04-1|MT159-C04-1]] — Reshoring manufacturing is constrained by labor availability and skills and by the US not being at the cutting edge of advanced manufacturing, so tariffs alone will not bring industry back.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C04-X1|MT159-C04-X1]] — The realist (Mearsheimer, voiced by Sacks) counter-argument is that in a great-power struggle with a peer rival, national security outranks free trade, so domestic capacity in rare earths, chips, autos, steel and aluminum justifies protection.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C04-X2|MT159-C04-X2]] — Where China leads in advanced manufacturing, the US gains more by learning through joint ventures (as it did from Japan in just-in-time autos) than by shutting Chinese firms out.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C04-X3|MT159-C04-X3]] — Supply-chain resilience can be achieved by diversifying to allied countries, not only by reshoring to the US, so it does not by itself justify high tariffs.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C05-X1|MT159-C05-X1]] — Regretting global trade compares today's reality with an imaginary counterfactual in which the US kept its manufacturing and was better off; Niall Ferguson calls that fanciful.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C05-X2|MT159-C05-X2]] — In the Cold War the US won by economic strength rather than by blockade, and targeted embargoes were reasonable; this supports an economy-first rather than a tariff-first approach to China.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C05-X4|MT159-C05-X4]] — Government subsidies add little where private capital is already abundant (AI), and public allocation invites picking winners and regulatory capture.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C06-1|MT159-C06-1]] — US nuclear build costs about 4x those of Korea and China, so removing regulatory friction (not picking winners with capital) is the lever that would lower costs.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C06-2|MT159-C06-2]] — China runs competitive state-backed industrial policy: it funds many startups (e.g. about 100 in solar) that compete down to a few efficient survivors, which makes its sectors cost-efficient.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C06-X1|MT159-C06-X1]] — Large Manhattan-style projects such as AI supercomputer clusters (Abilene and Denton, Texas) have been delivered more efficiently by private capital than by government in the past 40 to 50 years.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C07-1|MT159-C07-1]] — About 60% of rare earths are mined but about 90% are refined in China, so a Chinese export ban on them can disrupt magnets used in EVs, phones and machines and industrial production worldwide.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C07-3|MT159-C07-3]] — Banning Nvidia H20 sales to China hands the Chinese AI chip market to Huawei, which already competes near the frontier, and removes competition with CUDA.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C08-1|MT159-C08-1]] — Denying exports to a capable country raises its incentive to build domestic substitutes (Chinese aircraft after reliance on Boeing and Airbus), so export controls can accelerate rival development.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C08-2|MT159-C08-2]] — Export controls on goods sold in about 120 countries and with digital attributes leak because borders are porous and arbitrage finds a route, as with the failed Russian sanctions.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C08-X1|MT159-C08-X1]] — Cutting Nvidia out of China makes every Chinese AI firm build on Huawei, creating an isolated ecosystem that improves collectively and erodes Nvidia's CUDA lock-in.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C09-X2|MT159-C09-X2]] — Huawei can offset weaker chips with two to three times as many chips and power; China is building about 100 nuclear plants at a quarter of US cost, so if AI is power-limited China is advantaged.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C10-1|MT159-C10-1]] — US AI policy should focus on reshoring and on accelerating its own progress rather than slowing China, as keeping China from frontier AI is unlikely to work.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C10-X1|MT159-C10-X1]] — Private commitments (Nvidia's $500B US investment with Foxconn and TSMC, first GB200 off the Arizona line) show reshoring can happen through incentives without broad tariffs.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C10-X2|MT159-C10-X2]] — The US lead in AI is shrinking (Eric Schmidt, SemiAnalysis on Huawei), so a strategy built on widening the gap is unrealistic.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C10-X3|MT159-C10-X3]] — Global economic interdependence is a given, so policy should shape healthy interdependence rather than try to cut it (Thomas Friedman quote relayed by Gurley).
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C11-X1|MT159-C11-X1]] — Assuming China cannot innovate leads to bad policy, because Chinese engineers are trained in the same universities and, as even hawks agree, China will have a frontier compute stack and frontier models whether or not the US helps.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C13-1|MT159-C13-1]] — The KPI for AI and trade policy should be whether US firms widen their lead as in the internet era, i.e. winning via free trade and own acceleration rather than by slowing rivals (swimmer metaphor).
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C13-X2|MT159-C13-X2]] — The space-race belief that first to space would control the globe never materialized; the same zero-sum 'whoever controls AGI/ASI controls the globe' belief is likely equally wrong.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C13-X3|MT159-C13-X3]] — Innovation diffuses across firms and countries (OpenAI building on Google, DeepSeek on both), so calling copying theft is selective and prohibiting rivals from AI progress cannot succeed.
- [[MT159 - Tariffs, Free Trade, Export Controls, H20 & Rare Earth Ban - BG2 w- Bill Gurley & Brad Ger#^MT159-C17-1|MT159-C17-1]] — Comparative advantage makes free trade win-win and unwinding it makes lose-lose, supporting the free trade view.

## [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301]]
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C02-2|MT160-C02-2]] — Chip businesses (memory, CPUs, smartphone chips) have become commoditized with thin margins, so Nvidia's position is unusual and its highly rated stock reflects unpredictable shifts in leadership.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C13-X3|MT160-C13-X3]] — A BCG report puts global asset-management assets at $120 trillion in 2023 yet the industry faces fee compression, rising costs and passive competition.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C15-2|MT160-C15-2]] — Non-linear patterns in financial data may exist that classical statistics miss (Renaissance hires AI specialists), but that is very different from applying generic LLM tools.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C18-X1|MT160-C18-X1]] — Fee structure becomes two-tier: new funds charge less, established funds with track records keep high fees, and raising fees later is awkward for seed investors.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C18-X2|MT160-C18-X2]] — Niels disagrees with competing on the lowest fees: CTA fees have already fallen over 35 years to reasonable levels, strategies cost money to run, and net returns are what matter.
- [[MT160 - The AI Hype within Investing... a Step Too Far- - Systematic Investor 301#^MT160-C18-X3|MT160-C18-X3]] — Legacy fund fees were very high because they were launched in the early 1990s when interest rates were higher and trail fees to advisers were part of the model; the fee margin has come down since.

## [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo]]
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C01-1|MT161-C01-1]] — The S&P 500 is highly concentrated (top 10 about 39%, tech about 36%, over 50% with tech-adjacent names), so index risk depends on a few AI-linked stocks.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C04-1|MT161-C04-1]] — In 2026 the market is driven by earnings growth rather than multiple expansion: P/E is down two or three turns, which marks a more cyclical, earnings-dependent regime.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C04-2|MT161-C04-2]] — The strategists call this an alpha market where stock picking and active management matter more than passive, citing asset-manager stocks breaking out.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C05-1|MT161-C05-1]] — Oil supply disruptions will persist whether or not the war ends, so oil keeps a bid despite peace headlines.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C05-2|MT161-C05-2]] — Higher oil matters more for equities in regions without an AI capex impulse (Europe), while in the US capex dominates the war and oil effect.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C05-4|MT161-C05-4]] — A guest argues oil is in oversupply because China has pulled back purchases and bypass pipelines for the strait are nearly built.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C05-5|MT161-C05-5]] — Base metals at new highs alongside collapsing iron ore indicate metals strength is from AI and capex demand, not China.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C06-1|MT161-C06-1]] — Hyperscaler free cash flow is falling or negative (Meta $785m for the quarter, Microsoft about $19bn and down 25%, Amazon negative over 12 months).
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C06-2|MT161-C06-2]] — Big tech has moved from asset-light cash-flow machines to capital-intensive businesses, which changes how it should be valued.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C07-1|MT161-C07-1]] — Google raising $85bn of equity, its first raise since the early 2000s, marks a regime shift in big-tech funding.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C07-2|MT161-C07-2]] — Demand for AI exceeds supply so chip suppliers benefit from hyperscaler capex, while hyperscaler returns on that spend are uncertain and may be competed away.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C08-4|MT161-C08-4]] — Meta's revenue grew 28% but expenses grew 55%, with depreciation rising from $4bn to $6bn and heading to $12bn, which will weigh on earnings.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C10-1|MT161-C10-1]] — Private credit grew because post-crisis regulation (Dodd-Frank) pushed banks out of lending, so private credit is a regulatory arbitrage that could be disproportionately bad for wealthy holders in a downturn but is not systemic.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C10-3|MT161-C10-3]] — European equities are cheap for a reason: low-dynamism economies without AI, so cheapness alone is not a buy signal.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C10-4|MT161-C10-4]] — Japan is preferred over Europe because it is exiting deflation and has a return-on-equity reform (delisting firms below price-to-book and ROE minimums).
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C11-1|MT161-C11-1]] — European index highs are narrow: AI build-out names (Schneider, Siemens Energy) plus banks, while autos and luxury, which depend on Chinese demand, are in multi-year bear markets.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C11-3|MT161-C11-3]] — Japanese 10-year yields are about to exceed German 10-year yields for the first time in decades.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C11-X1|MT161-C11-X1]] — Japan outperforms Europe in part because its sector mix has the themes the market wants (chips, AI build-out, defense), while Europe's index highs rest on a few AI-build-out names and banks.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C11-X2|MT161-C11-X2]] — German GDP has not grown for four to five years, supporting the view that Europe's cheap valuations reflect an absence of growth dynamism.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C11-X3|MT161-C11-X3]] — Studies show Europe's richest families are largely the same as 400 years ago while the US has high wealth turnover (shirt sleeves to shirt sleeves in three generations), a structural reason Europe is less dynamic and a harder place to make money.
- [[MT161 - The AI Trade, Rising Rates, and Why the Market Is Still Standing - The Real Eisman Playboo#^MT161-C12-2|MT161-C12-2]] — Healthcare ranks first among defensives: pharma, biotech and life sciences (Thermo, Danaher, Illumina) are leading and made major bottoms, and health insurers put in lows.

## [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield]]
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C03-5|MT162-C03-5]] — Hedge fund money is increasingly run by algorithms (human decisions reduce to rules), but the best people combine coded or scraped inputs with human intuition.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C06-X1|MT162-C06-X1]] — Covid fast-forwarded working from home by perhaps 10-20 years: employees save commuting time and employers need less office space, a structural shift away from large office buildings.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C07-1|MT162-C07-1]] — ESG will become central to investing, unlike niche exclusion-based socially responsible investing, because voters care about climate and its damage is seen as proven.
- [[MT162 - The Boardroom Masterclass - Andrew Law - The University of Sheffield#^MT162-C11-1|MT162-C11-1]] — Machine learning does repetitive pattern-reaction work, but the big opportunities arise in new situations (such as politics) that models cannot learn from past data.

## [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357]]
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C02-2|MT163-C02-2]] — Pressure on trade partners pushes them to alternatives, and China's record Belt and Road activity shows it positioning as the stable partner, making resources a long-run theme.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C03-1|MT163-C03-1]] — The benefits of AI may accrue to resources because of the power and materials required; hence big tech buying nuclear power and rare earth stakes (Apple $0.5B; US DoD).
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C03-3|MT163-C03-3]] — Rising concentration in tech (Mag 7) and finance gives size benefits but increases fragility because firms cannot pivot when something goes wrong.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C06-2|MT163-C06-2]] — Assets whose supply is concentrated (uranium: Kazakhstan, Canada; copper: Chile; rare earths) become unstable and convex in geopolitical conflict because a supplier can flex its muscle.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C06-3|MT163-C06-3]] — FX volatility is the best volatility play for the decade because gold is more of a currency than a commodity and currency instability will rise; bond volatility is the next best.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C06-X2|MT163-C06-X2]] — Copper's record one-day move (since 1968) after tariff news, with production concentrated in Chile, shows how concentrated-supply commodities become convex and unstable when policy changes.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C16-3|MT163-C16-3]] — Only about 20-25% of S&P companies can expand margins into rising rates; the rest face both multiple and margin contraction.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C17-2|MT163-C17-2]] — College-graduate unemployment of about 9% (versus 4% overall, and 6-6.5% a year ago) is a leading indicator of rising unemployment, concentrated in computer science and similar AI-exposed fields.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C17-X1|MT163-C17-X1]] — Within equities in a rising-rate regime, prefer companies with negative working capital and improving results, the ones that can expand margins while multiples compress.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C17-X3|MT163-C17-X3]] — Dario Amodei's reported forecast is 10 to 20% unemployment in 1 to 5 years from AI, concentrated in white-collar jobs; Jim thinks this is likely and a big deal.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C18-X1|MT163-C18-X1]] — Anecdotal evidence of AI productivity: new hires do the work of three or four, so hiring stops sooner and faster than expected.
- [[MT163 - The Calm Before the Storm- AI Risk, and Market Fragility - Systematic Investor - Ep.357#^MT163-C18-X2|MT163-C18-X2]] — The same AI shock is manageable in a free-market, supply-side cycle like 1982 but is volatile in the present populist, unequal, concentrated-power cycle, so political regime conditions the macro outcome.

## [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359]]
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C01-X1|MT164-C01-X1]] — After a China trip, Mark reports a cashless digital economy in major cities (Alipay, WeChat), advanced EVs and visible innovation: structural evidence of China's technological momentum.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C06-2|MT164-C06-2]] — Sudden stops (events causing large moves that reverse quickly) have replaced longer high-volatility spells, which hurts trend followers.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C07-1|MT164-C07-1]] — Markets are more complex now: more factors are needed than the simple growth/inflation/term premium model for bonds or earnings/rates for equities.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C10-1|MT164-C10-1]] — The claim that stablecoins create new demand for Treasury bills is weak because stablecoin money may merely come out of bank reserves already held in T-bills.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C11-1|MT164-C11-1]] — Holders of stablecoins forgo interest, which accrues to the issuer; tokenized money market funds (JPMorgan, BNY) that keep interest while serving as collateral are more important for credit market health.
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C13-X1|MT164-C13-X1]] — Regulators and some European investors treat trend followers as a cause of commodity price rises, so commodity-trading CTAs face institutional restrictions (reputational, not statistical, risk).
- [[MT164 - The Challenge isn’t Prediction—it’s Positioning - Systematic Investor - Ep.359#^MT164-C16-1|MT164-C16-1]] — Trend following is designed for an unknown future (energy, AI, dark factories could change the world), since it adapts along the way rather than predicting.

## [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360]]
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C02-1|MT165-C02-1]] — Firing statisticians and weaker data credibility make the US look more like an emerging market, which could raise risk premia on US assets and affect inflation-indexed contracts.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C04-1|MT165-C04-1]] — Immigration restrictions shrink labor supply, lowering trend growth to about 1% (from 2-2.5%) and meaning that wage pressure would reappear quickly if demand rebounds, which lowers the Fed's threshold for tightening.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C04-X3|MT165-C04-X3]] — The 2023 US rebound was largely driven by a surge in immigration (labour supply), so reversing immigration reverses part of that growth driver.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C05-X3|MT165-C05-X3]] — Rising unemployment among young college graduates (citing Jim) is an early sign of AI's labour effect that headline GDP/employment data do not yet show.
- [[MT165 - The Fed’s Playbook is Broken — Here’s What’s Next - Systematic Investor - Ep.360#^MT165-C12-X1|MT165-C12-X1]] — Man Group's AUM and inflows are growing, but fee income fell because trend funds earned no performance fees; its executives call it one of the toughest periods in 25 to 30 years while saying trend following is not broken.

## [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361]]
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C04-1|MT166-C04-1]] — In an unpredictable world (deglobalisation, protectionism, diverging central banks) non-predictive trend following is attractive because it needs no forecast.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C15-1|MT166-C15-1]] — Asymmetric rules: allow longs more room in commodities (supply-shock rallies) and run wider trailing stops on equity shorts (crises fall violently) to improve tail capture.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C15-X12|MT166-C15-X12]] — When a commodity is already at a historic low and cannot fall much further before zero, the downside tail available to short trades is limited, which is a structural reason for asymmetric rule design.
- [[MT166 - The Four Faces of Trend Following - Systematic Investor - Ep.361#^MT166-C17-X14|MT166-C17-X14]] — Short-term trend funds used to earn crisis alpha by capturing the P&L of a volatility breakout over 3 to 5 days without giving it back the next week; market structure now makes those gains reverse faster, reducing their diversification benefit.

## [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392]]
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C03-1|MT167-C03-1]] — Natural gas pricing depends heavily on transportation cost while oil does not, so gas and oil react to different shocks.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C05-2|MT167-C05-2]] — After-effects of conflict on Gulf tourism and activity may be more damaging than higher oil prices alone.
- [[MT167 - The Hidden Cracks in Systematic Strategies - Systematic Investor - Ep. 392#^MT167-C16-2|MT167-C16-2]] — Quant equity paper: new data (geolocation, card, patents, sentiment) lowered alpha-model correlations from 70-80% to 40-50% and dynamic factor allocation adds regime resilience.

## [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393]]
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C03-2|MT168-C03-2]] — The oil spike was concentrated at the front of the curve (supply constraints) while Brent three years out barely moved, because the long-run market is over-producing (about 105 mb/d produced vs 102 consumed).
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C06-2|MT168-C06-2]] — Energy shocks can be supply availability (rationing) rather than price, affecting activity; Europe and Egypt show vulnerability.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C07-1|MT168-C07-1]] — Back-of-curve oil trading reflects fundamental supply and demand and is insulated from news-cycle shocks (weather vs climate).
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C07-2|MT168-C07-2]] — Energy has about 6% direct weight in inflation, with secondary effects via food and manufacturing, so the oil curve informs the inflation curve.
- [[MT168 - The Illusion of Control in Modern Markets ft. Yoav Git - Systematic Investor - Ep.393#^MT168-C14-1|MT168-C14-1]] — Critique: prices gap on news without trading (nonfarm payroll, weekend Iran attack), so explaining all volatility by trading flows ignores genuine jumps in fundamental value.

## [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9]]
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C03-1|MT169-C03-1]] — Universe: Russell 3000 minus the top 100 caps and illiquid micro-caps, leaving about 2,200-2,500 active mid/small caps; entry on price trend breakouts, equal risk per trade, exit on stops.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C04-2|MT169-C04-2]] — About 40% or more of Russell 2000 companies are unprofitable; avoiding them is a source of alpha versus index funds.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C05-X1|MT169-C05-X1]] — Equal-risk weighting across thousands of stocks avoids the concentration of a cap-weighted index in whatever sector dominates (tech now, energy 20 years ago) and thereby lowers correlation to broad equity beta; Cole agrees correlation is lower than long-only but not near zero.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C06-1|MT169-C06-1]] — The winner-take-all power law is a universal mathematical law of capital markets, supporting trend on single stocks across caps and countries.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C06-2|MT169-C06-2]] — Equity index futures trend portfolios are dominated by large caps and underrepresent the small/mid-cap area where outliers and inefficiency concentrate; cash equities need full funding, unlike futures.
- [[MT169 - The Key to Trend Following Success - Open Interest 9#^MT169-C08-X6|MT169-C08-X6]] — Single-stock futures were not useful for the strategy because they restricted the tradable universe to large, liquid names, whereas the small and mid-cap segment where trend has the most edge was not covered.

## [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418]]
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C02-2|MT170-C02-2]] — Since 2020 the strongest risk-adjusted trend results sit around the 200-day lookback; the 20-day lookback's performance fell to roughly one quarter of its earlier level, so fast managers saw decay in one part of the landscape, not the whole phenomenon.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C02-3|MT170-C02-3]] — Markets contain interacting participants, leverage, feedback, forced activity and changing capital flows that create self-feeding moves; the structure persists but when and where trends appear drifts.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C02-X1|MT170-C02-X1]] — Because it is uncertain when and where self-feeding trends will appear, the opportunity is preserved (it cannot be arbitraged into a known pattern), but it also makes trend following hard to practise since returns do not arrive on the investor's schedule or in the markets they expect.
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C07-2|MT170-C07-2]] — Trends and mean-reversion arise endogenously from participant feedback rather than news events (cf. Bouchaud on endogenous vs exogenous impact).
- [[MT170 - The Market Creates Its Own Trends — Richard Brennan - Systematic Investor - Ep.418#^MT170-C08-X6|MT170-C08-X6]] — In Arthur's view the economy is an ecology in which strategies compete, lose effectiveness and evolve rather than reach equilibrium, so any trading edge can decay and must be monitored instead of assumed permanent.

## [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397]]
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C02-2|MT171-C02-2]] — QIS (quantitative investment strategies) assets are very large, with one bank alone running about 175bn, roughly half the size of the official CTA industry, so systematic non-traditional strategies are a large and growing flow source.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C03-1|MT171-C03-1]] — Assets in non-correlated strategies (precious metals, crypto, hedge funds, options and structured products, trend following, QIS) have gone from a straight line to a rocket ship over four years, a regime change in demand for alternatives to stocks and bonds.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C03-2|MT171-C03-2]] — The alternatives sliver (about 6tn to 20tn) is tiny against a roughly 500tn asset-management world and could reach 100tn or even half the market over 10-15 years because it is a superior risk-adjusted way to invest.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C03-X1|MT171-C03-X1]] — Options, structured products and other non-linear or non-correlated combination strategies are the vehicle for leaving the stock-and-bond world and are available to retail investors for the first time, which is why adoption has accelerated.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C03-X2|MT171-C03-X2]] — Evidence of institutional adoption: the chief investment strategist of the American Red Cross says the organisation now largely relies on QIS-type strategies, cited from the Zuckerman article as a sign the shift is reaching conservative allocators.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C04-5|MT171-C04-5]] — Official narrative diverges sharply from observable preparations: emergency meetings on cybersecurity and private credit, a proposed 50% US military budget increase and automakers asked to build military goods.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C09-1|MT171-C09-1]] — The Hormuz blockade is about leverage over China's oil flow (about 40% of oil transits), so the conflict is unlikely to end soon and oil risk persists.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C10-2|MT171-C10-2]] — A closed Strait is acceptable to the US because it hurts China more than the US, which implies the US has little incentive to reopen it quickly and oil stays disrupted outside the US.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C16-2|MT171-C16-2]] — Fourth-turning eras pair technological booms with populism because winner-take-all supply-side dynamics breed vulnerability of the masses.
- [[MT171 - The Market Isn’t Free Anymore — It’s Being Managed - Systematic Investor - Ep.397#^MT171-C17-X1|MT171-C17-X1]] — A fork for the AI-inflation argument: if AI is used for surveillance and control as in China, politics can move toward authoritarianism and bypass the populist demand for transfers, which would remove the inflationary response; otherwise the populist response dominates.

## [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep]]
- [[MT172 - The Market's Biggest Warning Signs Right Now with Todd Sohn - The Real Eisman Playbook Ep#^MT172-C01-2|MT172-C01-2]] — Semiconductors have risen from about 2% to 18-19% of the S&P 500 in ten years after a massive run since the 30 March 2026 low, so the index is dominated by one group.
